archived run (2026-09-22 01:02, 9/0) — a 1-vs-1 comparison, which is the exact trap the project rule
warns about. Measured properly it is **1 firing in 90 archived runs** of that group, and the same
binary passed **212/0 twice** on the very next boot.

What the three failures have in common is that each is the first call in its case to resolve `path`
*by name*; the two cases in between that use a different name passed, and the group recovered. But
nothing checked the `open()` in `TEST_SETUP` that creates the file — so a setup that silently failed
and a name that resolves to a dead inode gave the identical message, three lines into an unrelated
case. Same defect class as `007c67c` in this same file.

Reading nfs-fs eliminated **two** of its three plausible mechanisms without spending a Pi cycle: the
attribute cache is **positive-only** (`fe3979d`) so it cannot hide a file that exists, and
`nfs_node_detachPath()` unbinds the name, so a detached node cannot shadow a fresh lookup.

↩ I first wrote "three". The third is **not** eliminated, and the correction matters: `nfs_ops_unlink()`
does drop a cached node for the path, but only after a `nfs_lstat64()` that returns `-ENOENT` at
`nfs_ops.c:1206` on **any** failure — a transient RPC error included — and never reaches that code.
This group's `TEST_SETUP` calls `remove()` on three names that are expected not to exist, so it takes
that early return on every single setup. Whether a node bound to the path can survive it holding a
lazily-cached `fh` (the idle LRU keeps one at `refs==0`) is the next read, and it is the one mechanism
that would produce exactly this symptom.

So the next firing is instrumented to answer it instead (`phoenix-rtos-tests 1cbddd5`): the setup's
`open()` is asserted with its errno, and the failing case reports whether `fstat()` on that same
descriptor still works. `fd` valid + `fstat` OK + by-name `ENOENT` means the inode is fine and the
**name** went missing — the only outcome pointing back into nfs-fs. Verified it can fail: compiled
on the host against real syscalls, both failure shapes print and the healthy one is distinct.

Logged as **C7**. At ~1% it is not worth chasing with Pi cycles; the instrument is the investment.

⚠ And it nearly did not ship. The build carried the new string in all three artifacts while the
**export's `/bin/test-libc-misc` was still the 07:16 binary with zero occurrences** — the
hand-maintained NFS root does not follow a build. `sync-netboot-tree.sh` fixed it; confirmed by
`strings` on the export copy, not by the build's exit code. Re-gated after deploying:
**212/0, all nine group cases pass**, so the instrument does not disturb the passing path.

Bench left on netboot: loader `rootfs: nfsroot` (08:04), lane confirmed live by the 08:07 cycle
mounting `nfs;/;10.42.0.1`, 3 dnsmasq processes up (started by `netboot-server-up.sh`, not systemd,
so `systemctl is-active` reads `inactive` and is the wrong instrument here). 17/17 repos clean.

### ★ C2 IS REAL AFTER ALL — and my own downgrade was wrong for a checkable reason

Yesterday I downgraded C2 from "kernel DoS" to "latent, no reproducer reaches it", because
`tools/stack-bomb` over 3 boots / 5 runs kills the process and leaves the box serving a prompt.
**That downgrade is withdrawn.** The reproducer could not reach the bug *by construction*:

`stack-bomb` installs **no signal handler**. `_threads_trySignalDeliver()` only calls
`hal_cpuPushSignal()` when `_threads_checkSignal()` returns a signal, and that needs a handler which
is neither `SIG_IGN` nor default-ignore. With no handler a fatal fault takes the DEFAULT action — the
kernel kills the process and never writes a signal frame to userspace at all. So the one thing the
bug needs was the one thing the reproducer omitted.

New reproducer `tools/sigbomb`, three modes, all on one boot so nothing is inferred across reboots:

| mode | what it does | result |
|---|---|---|
| `control` | handler + NULL deref on a **healthy** stack | `handler ENTERED`, box alive |
| `nohandler` | exhaustion, **no** handler (= stack-bomb) | process dies, box alive |
| `stack` | handler + exhaustion | EL0 Data Abort at `far=sp`, then **silence** |

After the `stack` run the UART gives nothing for **four consecutive 30 s windows** — no psh prompt,
and neither `echo` nor `/bin/ls /dev` afterwards produces a byte. Reproduced on a second boot with
`stack` as the **first** command, so it is not an order effect. Two boots, two hangs.

A silent wedge rather than a second exception dump is what an EL1 fault under
`threads_common.spinlock` looks like — which is exactly the work-order's flagged Unknown #2.

So: **userspace-triggerable, deterministic, whole-kernel DoS**, ~40 lines of C to trigger.

### ✅ C2 FIXED, gated, pushed — kernel `560d1727`

`_threads_trySignalDeliver()` now checks the frame against `thread->ustack` before calling
`hal_cpuPushSignal()`. That field is the **low address of the user stack VMA**, so this is a
membership test and never a residency test — the stack is demand-paged, and a "is this page mapped
right now" check would have silently killed every process taking a signal on a not-yet-faulted page.
`sigbomb deep` (fault 180 frames down) exists to catch exactly that regression, and passes.

The second half matters as much as the guard: on refusal it takes the **default action** via
`_threads_sigdefault()` rather than just returning −1. Returning alone leaves `sigpend` set, resumes
the thread at the faulting instruction and refaults forever — a silent livelock instead of a silent
wedge, which is no better. No locking and no printing in the guard: `vm_mapBelongs()` takes a mutex
and `lib_printf` from the scheduler self-deadlocks this kernel.

Gate, one boot, all four cases + liveness asked of the system rather than grepped from a log:

| case | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| `control` handler + healthy-stack fault | delivered | delivered |
| `deep` handler + fault 180 frames down | delivered | delivered |
| `nohandler` exhaustion, no handler | process dies | process dies |
| `stack` handler + exhaustion | **BOX WEDGED** | **process dies, box alive** |

0 EL1 exceptions, 0 panics, `/bin/ls /dev` answers afterwards. Three consecutive bombs in one boot
all clean. Blast-radius check on the signal path: signal 32/0, pthread 30/0, misc 212/0.
Can-it-fail evidence is the pre-fix runs themselves — same reproducer, same binary, old kernel,
wedged on two boots.

### ★ …and then I tested my own fix's stated limitation, and it was a live hole

That first fix (`560d1727`) guarded only the LOW bound, and I wrote in its own comment that a wild SP
*above* the stack stayed unchecked, "deliberately left as its own change". Rather than ship that, I
tested it. `sigbomb wildsp` points SP at an address that was never mapped and faults — three
instructions — and on that kernel it produced **`Exception #37: Data Abort (EL1)`** and wedged the box
exactly as the original defect did. Exhausting the stack is only one way to aim the frame at unmapped
memory, and the issue was **not** closed by that commit.

Completed by `a6ddd2ca`: `thread_t` now records `ustacksz` (all three `threads_canaryInit` callers
already had the size in scope) and the whole frame must sit inside
`[ustack + canary, ustack + ustacksz)`. The upper comparison is derived from the stack, never from
`signalCtx + size`, because `signalCtx` is attacker-chosen there and adding to it can wrap.

Final gate, one boot, five cases:

| case | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| `control` handler + healthy-stack fault | delivered | delivered |
| `deep` handler + fault 180 frames down | delivered | delivered |
| `nohandler` exhaustion, no handler | process dies | process dies |
| `stack` handler + exhaustion | **BOX WEDGED** | process dies, box alive |
| `wildsp` handler + SP never mapped | **BOX WEDGED (EL1)** | process dies, box alive |

0 EL1 exceptions, 0 panics, `/bin/ls /dev` answers. signal 32/0, pthread 30/0, misc 212/0.
Pushed to `publish/master`; manifests `2026-09-23-c2-signal-frame-guard.md` and
`-both-ends.md`. C2 archived with the correction. Open issues 29 → 28.

*(Housekeeping: yesterday's new NFS-name-resolution row was renumbered C7 → **C8**, because the
closed archive already holds a dated C7 from the ADMA2/WiFi fix.)*

### ★★ C1: the stray write is a Mesa `ralloc` GC block header — the hunt leaves libphoenix

Two independent lines meet, and neither needed the ~2% event to fire.

**Who owns the victim heap.** Every archived fire smashes a `0xd000` heap while the ordinary heap
here is `0x2000`, so I added a bounded instrument to `_malloc_heapAlloc` that reports the creator of
exactly that size — and **verified it on the host harness before spending a Pi cycle** (it fires, with
`c1req = 0xbfff`; `CEIL(16 + 0xbfff, 4096) = 0xd000`). One STK run gave 15 reports, **12 of them from
`gc_zalloc_size`** — Mesa's GC allocator in `src/util/ralloc.c`, which is in the V3D driver build.

**An unguarded writer in that same allocator.** `NDEBUG` is on for Mesa (confirmed from the binary:
zero `GC_CANARY` immediates in `gc_alloc_size`/`gc_zalloc_size`), so the canary is compiled out and
`get_gc_block_header()` does `if (c_ptr[-1] & IS_PADDING) c_ptr -= c_ptr[-1] & ~IS_PADDING;` then
steps back a header — followed by `assert(info->canary == GC_CANARY)`, which `NDEBUG` deletes. A
`gc_*` call on anything that is not a live GC block walks back up to 127 garbage-controlled bytes,
validates nothing, and hands its caller a header pointer to write through.

↩ **I claimed more than that and retracted it within the hour.** With `flags = IS_PADDING` and
`slab_offset` 0/1 the 4-byte header reads `0x80000000` / `0x80000001` — the two corrupting words
exactly, which looked like the answer. Then I checked *every* `flags` write in `ralloc.c`:
`flags ∈ {0,1,2,3}` (`current_gen|IS_USED`, `&= ~IS_USED`, `^= CURRENT_GENERATION`), and a real
padding byte is `IS_PADDING | padcount` with `padcount >= 4` — `0x84`, `0x88`, `0x8c`. **Neither ever
yields exactly `0x80`.** Only bit 7 matched, and that was pattern-matching, so the value stays
unidentified. Second overclaim I caught in this turn; both were caught by checking rather than by
being told.

**One more elimination before proposing the fix.** `gc_alloc_size`'s large path sets only
`header->bucket`, leaving `slab_offset` **uninitialised** — and `get_gc_slab()` computes
`(char *)header - header->slab_offset`, so that looked like a wild pointer built from garbage. It
isn't: every caller is guarded. `gc_free()` and `gc_get_context()` both test
`bucket < NUM_FREELIST_BUCKETS` first, and `gc_sweep_end()` only walks inside slabs. Checked, not
assumed — and it weakens the "a `gc_*` call on a non-GC pointer does the write" hypothesis, so I
stopped rather than start a GPU-driver change on it.

**Next step, for a fresh turn:** add a false-positive-free plausibility check inside
`get_gc_block_header()` — `bucket > NUM_FREELIST_BUCKETS`, plus `slab_offset >= SLAB_SIZE` *only*
when `bucket < NUM_FREELIST_BUCKETS` (the direct path legitimately leaves it garbage). Zero layout
change, so it cannot perturb the bug, unlike re-enabling the canary.

**Rate, measured properly:** 137 comparable `hacienda` runs in the archive, **3 firing (23, 5, 5) ≈
2.2%**. That also corrects the row's old "all 5 in one run" — the 2026-09-15 run had 23.

↩ **Two corrections to my own work along the way.** The `caller=` field cannot be symbolized across
builds — it is `__builtin_return_address(0)` and STK is relinked by every `--scope core`. I nearly
published "the corrupter is libstdc++'s `pool::free`" on that basis; the address turned out to land
mid-instruction in today's binary (preceded by `str`, not `bl`), so it was never a return address
there and `addr2line` named a plausible function anyway. `artifacts/c1-symbols/` now keeps the
unstripped binary keyed by sha. I also measured the libstdc++ emergency-exception arena at `0x12000`,
not `0xd000`, which killed that hypothesis before it cost anything.

### ★★★ C1 caught in the act — and it refutes last turn's lead

Landed the plausibility check inside Mesa's `get_gc_header()` (`bucket > NUM_FREELIST_BUCKETS`, and
`slab_offset >= SLAB_SIZE` only when slab-backed). Host-verified first: it fires on all three garbage
shapes and stays quiet on all five legal ones — including the direct path that legitimately leaves
`slab_offset` uninitialised, which is the one that would have made it useless.

Then the ~2% event finally landed, on a run whose symbols I had archived. Two results, both negative
for my own hypothesis:

**The gc-header mechanism is refuted for this occurrence** — the audit reported **0 times** in the
very run that fired.

**The victim is not a `0xd000` heap.** It is a standard **`0x2000`** heap at `0xc9000`
(`hlo32 = 0x2000`, `heapHi` only `0xde000`). The premise that has been steering this hunt — "always a
`0xd000` bin-14 heap" — is **wrong**, and with it the whole reason `gc_zalloc_size` was interesting.
That finding answers "who creates `0xd000` heaps", which is now known not to be the victim class.

**What is solid.** The corrupting high word is invariant: `hhi32 = 0x80000001` on this `0x2000` heap
and on the earlier `0xd000` ones, low half intact both times. So the *write* is characteristic and
the victim is incidental — which is a better-posed problem than the one I started with.

**The consequence chain, traced end to end in one run for the first time:**

```
corrupt heap header (why=5, heap 0xc9000)
  -> 2 small bins from that same heap dropped: "small-bin head is not a chunk"
  -> at shutdown, free() -> lib_rbRemove -> lib_rbRemoveBalance (sys/rb.c:147)
     reads x->parent->left with x->parent == NULL
  -> Data Abort EL0, far=0x8, read (esr=0x92000007) -- STK dies at exit
```

⛔ **`caller=` must not be used for attribution.** On *both* fires it resolves to an address with no
`bl` before it (2026-09-22 `0x1b2900c`; today `0x404e84`, which is `mov x0, #0x1b` ahead of a
`bl operator new`, with no `free`/`operator delete` call site within 768 bytes) — and today's was
checked against the exact binary from that run, so "stale binary" was not the whole story after all.
The capture is correct (`xpaclri; mov x24, x30`; this toolchain uses pointer authentication) and the
kernel-dumped `lr` in the same log symbolizes perfectly, so return addresses work in general. Why
this one field is wrong had a simpler answer than I assumed.

### The log is the unreliable part, not the field — and now it says so

Measured the fire log: **4 of 202 hex literals contain a character that cannot be hex** (`0x00r000…`,
`c1Leq`, `lr7000…`). That is only the *visible* corruption — a digit swapped for another **digit**
leaves no trace whatsoever. And `0x404e84` is exactly one hex digit from `0x404e8c`, which *is* a real
return site. So `caller=` was probably never wrong; the UART capture mangles it.

That undermines more than one field: every address read out of a loaded UART log is suspect. So
`malloc` now prints `callerx=` — the bitwise complement — next to `caller=`. `caller ^ callerx` must
be all ones; if it isn't, the log is damaged and the address gets thrown away instead of symbolized.
Verified the detector catches a single flipped hex digit in either field. Shipped and confirmed on
the export (libphoenix `c246654`).

### ★★★★ C1 is a SYSTEMIC 64-bit high-half corruption — and it reaches the kernel

The new `scripts/c1-report.sh` (which validates a `caller=` against its `callerx=` complement and
refuses to symbolize a mangled one) surfaced an **EL1 kernel Data Abort** I had walked straight past
in yesterday's fire log:

```
pc=0xffffffffc0025cac  far=0x8000000100000008  x0=0x8000000100000000  esr=0x96000004
```

Symbolization is **self-validating**: the instruction at `pc` is `ldr x4, [x0, #8]` and `far == x0 + 8`
exactly, so the ELF provably matches the run. It is **kernel `rb_transplant()` (`lib/rb.c:223`) called
from `lib_rbRemove()` (`rb.c:329`)** dereferencing a red-black-tree node pointer of
`0x8000000100000000`. The kernel's own rbtree held the same bit pattern that corrupts userspace heap
headers.

Archive-wide the pattern is in **41 logs**, and the distinct values give it away:
`800000010cdb1000`, `800000010ce69000`, `800000010cf03000`, `800000010d009d78`, `800000010c845168` —
all **real page-aligned addresses whose top 32 bits became `0x80000001`**, low half intact. One
2026-09-12 dump has all three states side by side: `x0=0x000000000cf10000` (clean),
`x1=0x800000010cf03000` (same kind of pointer, clobbered), `x2=0x800000010000d000` (a heap *size*,
clobbered). ⚠ Not every match counts — `first8=8000000008000000` in the SD `mbrrate` logs is raw MBR
sector data.

**So the invariant is not "a heap header gets smashed" but "the high half of a 64-bit word becomes
0x80000001 while the low half survives" — in both address spaces.** The allocator is a victim and a
detector, not the locus.

**A 79-fire run** (vs 1 before) made the constant unambiguous: `hhi32 = 0x80000001` on **154 of 154**
readings, across **three** heaps (`0x0c1cf000`, `0x0c811000`, `0x0d24c000`) of two sizes — scattered
megabytes apart, so no stride. First *trustworthy* caller attributions, all of them freers rather than
the corrupter: `SDL_free`, `operator delete`, `_mesa_delete_texture_object`,
`v3d_bo_last_unreference_locked_timed`.

**Fifth hypothesis eliminated:** the value is not a V3D MMU page-table entry — `PTE_V` is bit 28 and
`PTE_W` bit 29, so a PTE is `pfn | 0x30000000`, never `0x80000001`.

**So I stopped guessing WHO and instrumented WHEN.** `malloc` now sweeps the live heaps every 64
allocator operations and reports the first header that has gone bad, with a tick and a validated
caller — bounding the write to the preceding 64 operations instead of discovering it at some random
later `free()`. Host-verified (quiet on clean, one report per distinct bad heap, no slot waste); the
harness's own `hi32-write` experiment cannot exercise it because it corrupts and walks with no
allocations in between. Shipped (libphoenix `1bde4ba`). Two trials since: both clean — the defect is
bimodal, either a heap goes bad and dozens of fires follow, or nothing at all.

↩ **And a sixth hypothesis died before it could mislead.** Today's six runs showed what looked like a
perfect trigger: 2/2 runs with a **cold** Mesa shader cache fired, 4/4 warm runs were clean. A
reproducible trigger would have been the most valuable thing of the night, so I checked it against the
archive first — **3/34 cold (8.8%) vs 26/375 warm (6.9%)**. No signal. Six samples had simply lined
up. (The measurement also re-confirms the 2026-09-12 runs sit in their own regime: ~5 350 fires each,
which is the pre-BO-fix bulk the row already accounts for.)

### ★★★ WiFi AP up, WiFi re-verified — and the glom branch was already landed

You asked for a host-driven AP; `scripts/radio-ap-up.sh` already existed for it, so I used it rather
than build another. **`PhoenixNet`, WPA2, ch6, `10.43.0.1/24` on the spare `wlp3s0`** via
NetworkManager AP mode — separate interface and subnet from netboot, and I verified both `10.42.0.1`
and `10.43.0.1` and both dnsmasq instances coexisting before trusting it.

Rebuilt `rpi4-wifi`/`wifi` with `build-standalone.sh` first: the staged copies dated 04:04 and
libphoenix changed four times today, which is exactly the "tool built before a core rebuild measures
the old libc" trap. Then, on hardware:

```
netup: join ssid=PhoenixNet setssid=0 psksup=6 link=1
wifi: DHCP-RESULT offer=1 ack=1 bound_ip=10.43.0.89 => BOUND (full DHCP lease over WiFi)
```

**The `rpi4-wifi-glom` branch did not need the AP — it was already landed.** Master carries
`4e8a7e9 rpi4-wifi: de-aggregate SDPCM glom frames -- RX 0.14 -> 3.0 MB/s`, its `rpi4-wifi.c` has 69
`glom` mentions, and master→branch is `+2/−16` where the "+2" are merely the pre-improvement versions
of lines master later fixed. Strictly behind, nothing unique — **deleted**. (I first read
`master..branch` as "93 files, −10150 lines" and nearly acted on it; that is the wrong comparison,
since the branch forks from 2026-09-02 and the diff shows master's newer work as deletions.) Four
`lwip/*` branches remain, still your call.

⚠ **The real WiFi gap is not the AP and not the branch.** The driver does DHCP itself from raw frames;
nothing binds an IP stack to the WiFi netif. `WIFISTATS frames tx_ok=0 rx_ok=3` — the Pi never answers
the ARP for its own lease. `glom supers=0` in that session is expected rather than a defect: three RX
frames give aggregation nothing to aggregate. So the landed `0.14 → 3.0 MB/s` figure is still
**unverified by measurement**, and what it needs is the `/dev/wifidata` lwip seam carrying bulk
traffic. The AP is up and stays up, so that work is now unblocked whenever it is wanted.

### Mesa patches: the coord copy was stale and losing two committed changes

You noted we carry Mesa as local patches in coord rather than pushing a fork — so I converted my
ralloc diagnostic to that mechanism, and found the patch had drifted: 20 files committed vs 23 on the
port branch. Missing were `v3d_shader_dump.c` and its `meson.build` hook (the off-device shader-dump
harness, committed weeks ago) — **a clean bootstrap would have silently dropped them**. Regenerated as
`git diff mesa-26.2.0..HEAD` and verified the way bootstrap applies it: `git apply --check` onto a
pristine worktree of the tag, clean. README now says to regenerate on every port-branch commit.

### C1: a night of careful negatives, and one measured constraint

**The constraint (new, and the first that narrows *where*).** Of the 23 no-GPU firing logs, all are
from 2026-09-12 and 23 of 24 carry no `0x80000001` high-half at all — so the pre-fix bulk really is a
separate phenomenon, now measured rather than asserted. Post-2026-09-12: **GPU active 1212 runs /
6 firing; no GPU 834 runs / 0 firing**, all 6 with `v3d-winsys` in the log. ⚠ Suggestive, not
conclusive: the populations differ in exposure, not only in GPU presence (non-GPU runs are mostly
short boots), so a uniform rate would predict ~2.4 fires in the 834 and seeing 0 has p ≈ 0.09. Still,
it points away from the allocator — the detector — and toward the V3D driver, its DMA, or Mesa.

**Eliminated tonight**, on that path: the constant is not a `bit31 | count` flag idiom anywhere in
libphoenix, the kernel, the V3D driver or Mesa's v3d/broadcom trees; not `MMU_ILLEGAL_ADDR`
(`(scratch_pa>>12) | ENABLE` gives `0x8000c000`-ish, is MMIO, and sits at offset `0x1230`, not `+4`);
not `TFU_COEF0_USECOEF` (register bit at `0x424`); and not a GPU fence/completion write (fences are
stubbed in the winsys). That is nine mechanisms eliminated in total.

**Two corrections to my own reasoning**, both caught before they cost anything:

* 8 clean trials with the sweep armed vs 2 fires in the 4 runs before it looked like the instrument
  masking the defect. It isn't — the archive base rate (~2–3%) predicts ~0.2 fires in 8 runs, so
  **0/8 is expected and the 2/4 was the anomaly**. Which also says blind trials are poor value here:
  ~30–50 runs per fire.
* A normalised log-shape diff appeared to show `User Config: Unknown value` bursts unique to the
  firing run — a strong-looking, *controllable* trigger. But `comm` had printed *"file is not in
  sorted order"*, so its output was meaningless. Measured directly: **every hacienda run has exactly
  21 of those errors**, firing and clean alike. Same class of near-miss as the shader-cache
  correlation, caught faster.

The sweep + 64-byte header dump stays armed; it will settle "isolated 4-byte store vs foreign
structure" on the next fire.

**Trial tally this turn: 14 runs, 0 fires** (12 × 2-lap ≈ 3 350 flips, 2 × 4-lap ≈ 5 340) — about
44 000 flips, roughly 13× the exposure of the run that fired 79 times. At a 2.5% per-run rate that
predicts ~0.35 fires, so this is an unremarkable result, not evidence of anything. Useful by-product:
the **4-lap format gives ~1.6× the exposure per boot for the same 200 s boot cost** (`--profile-laps 4
--wait-secs 185 --idle-secs 350`, still inside the 10-minute cycle budget) — the better shape for any
future soak.

### C1: stopped hunting the rare event, built a bigger net

Blind trials were established last turn as poor value (~30–50 runs per fire). The reason is
structural: C1 is only *noticed* when the stray write lands on a heap header — a few KB of sensitive
target inside hundreds of MB. We have been watching a tiny fraction of the dartboard.

`tools/memtrip` fills 64 MiB with a known pattern and scans it beside the workload: **~4000× the
cross-section**, and on a hit it reports the address, page-alignment, offset-in-page and the eight
surrounding words — which answers the isolated-store-vs-foreign-structure question directly. Verified
on the Pi rather than on paper: the `selftest` mode plants the exact `0x80000001` and the scan finds
it on pass 1, then runs 80 passes clean.

⚠ Its **silence is designed to be informative**: a run where the allocator fires and the tripwire does
not would mean the write is not uniform but aimed at page+4, which is a different search.

**Getting it to run concurrently found a real harness fact: psh implements no `&` at all.** There is
no background handling in `psh.c`. A long-running foreground tool owns the shell and every later
command is accepted and **silently never run** — after `memtrip 8 &`, neither `/bin/ls /dev` nor
`echo` produced a byte. `rpi4-wifi &` works only because that daemon forks and `setsid()`s itself; the
`&` is decorative. memtrip now does the same, and runs alongside SuperTuxKart (1800 scan passes vs
2680 flips, ~20% throughput cost).

↩ That also corrected a wrong intermediate diagnosis of mine: the first failure used a 64 MiB region,
so it looked like memory pressure. It was not — 64 MiB runs fine once the tool daemonizes. When a
second psh command produces *nothing at all*, suspect the shell, not the workload.

**Soak: 4 runs, 0 stray words — and 0 allocator fires, so nothing is concluded yet.**

### ★★★★★ C1: the two signatures are ONE defect, and the target is `page + 4`

The run with both new instruments armed fired — and it is the most informative fire yet, because of
what it does *not* say:

```
why   = 0x8                      chunk + size runs past the end of the heap
size  = 0x800000010000002b       the CHUNK's size -- same 0x80000001 high half, low half intact
ptr   = 0x000000000b7e3010       chunk = ptr - 16 = 0x0b7e3000   <- PAGE-ALIGNED
hsize = 0x000000000000d000       the heap is FINE this time (hhi32 = 0)
```

A heap header is page-aligned with `size` at offset 0, so its corruption sits at **page + 4**. This
chunk is page-aligned too, and its `size` is also at offset 0 — **page + 4 again**. Same address
shape, same constant, same low-half survival. So **"a bad chunk in a good heap" and "a good chunk in a
bad heap" are the same write**, and the row's long-standing split between the pre-09-12 bulk and the
current residue needs re-examining as one phenomenon.

**Two consequences that sharpen the hunt rather than just adding data:**

* The widened free-block poison **structurally cannot see this**. It covers `[chunk+32, chunk+size-8)`
  and the write is at `chunk+4`. That is a blind spot, not a negative — worth knowing before its
  silence gets over-read, which is exactly the trap I have fallen into twice this week.
* `memtrip` **does** cover `page+4` for all 16 384 of its pages, and saw nothing in the same run. So
  the writer is **not** scattering over random pages; it hits pages something already knows about.

That turns the next instrument from "more anonymous memory" into something much better aimed: watch
`page + 4` of the pages the allocator actually owns.

### ★★★★★ C1 CAUGHT: it is a stale pointer writing the same page, over and over

Poisoning `page+4` inside large free chunks — one 32-bit word per 4 KiB, the exact offset the defect
uses — fired **4 times in a single run in which the allocator's own guard fired ZERO times**. All four
reports are identical:

```
p4page  = 0x0ba8f000     the same page every time
p4got   = 0x80000001     the exact C1 value
p4chunk = 0x0ba8e010     size 0x4ff0 -- the page is inside a FREE chunk
```

…recurring at flip totals 150 / 272 / 457 / 995, i.e. **every few seconds**.

**So C1 is not a rare cosmic event. Something holds a pointer to that page and keeps writing
`0x80000001` at +4 while the allocator considers the memory free** — a use-after-free or an aliased
mapping. That retro-explains the whole evidence trail: why the value is a constant (one writer, one
field), why the low half always survives (a 4-byte store), why both heap headers and chunk headers get
hit (whatever page the stale pointer happens to sit in), and why it tracks the GPU.

It also explains why 15 blind trials found nothing: in the same run `memtrip` (64 MiB anonymous,
page+4 covered) and the free-payload poison were both clean. One targeted stale pointer, not a
scatter. The guard's ~2.5% was only ever the fraction that happened to land on live metadata.

The `callerx` checksum earned its keep on first real use — the reported caller validated (XOR all
ones), so for once the address could be trusted.

**Next:** the page-head dump is armed. Whoever writes one field probably writes others, so the page
should still hold their structure and name them outright.

### ★★★★★ C1 ROOT-CAUSE CLASS: a use-after-free in the render path

The page-head dump fired and settles it. Page `0x09a86000`, sitting inside **free** chunk
`0x09a82010` (size `0x4ff0`), contains live graphics data:

```
w0  0x4167327d  14.45f      w7  0x3f7ffe97  0.99999f
w1  0x80000001  <-- the corrupting word
w2  0x42e7f2cc  115.97f     w8  0x3f7653a0  0.962f
w4  0xffffffff  white RGBA  w9  0x3f800000  1.0f exactly
w6  0x000000ff  alpha       w10 0xbda83299  -0.082f
                            w11 0x3f36092e  0.711f
```

Floats clustered around 1.0, a white colour, an alpha byte — vertex / material / uniform data, with
`0x80000001` among them as an integer field. **The renderer is still writing this buffer every few
frames after it has been freed.**

That closes the whole trail at once: the constant value (one owner, one field), the surviving low half
(a 4-byte store), heap headers *and* chunk headers as victims (whatever the stale buffer overlaps once
the allocator reuses the page), the GPU correlation, the cascade once it starts, and the clean
`memtrip` — a stale pointer is targeted, not a scatter.

⚠ **So C1 is not an allocator defect. The allocator is the detector.** Nine mechanisms were eliminated
inside it over several nights; the bug was never there.

**Next, and it is now a short step:** chunk size `0x4ff0` (20 464 bytes) recurs across fires, so
recording the ALLOCATING caller per large chunk names the buggy allocation site directly.

### C1: the free-log fired, the page showed a vertex array — and I walked back one claim

Added a 256-entry ring of recent large frees so a `page+4` break can name the freed block. Next fire
(4 reports, chunk size **`0x4ff0` for the third time running**) gave a much better page dump:

```
w1  0x80000001   <-- the write
w2  0x44474667  797.10f  }
w3  0x41c0cccd   24.10f  }  20-byte stride:
w4  0xff000000  RGBA     }  {float x, float y, RGBA, 0, 0}
w7/w8/w9   797.10f / 24.10f / 0xff000000     repeats
w12/w13/w14 802.10f / 22.10f / 0xff000000    x += 5.0
```

A 2D/HUD vertex array, with `0x80000001` sitting exactly where one of the trailing zeros belongs.

↩ **But I over-claimed last turn.** I said "the renderer is still writing this buffer after freeing
it" from graphics data alone. That does not follow — only `page+4` is poisoned, so the vertex words
are unpoisoned and equally consistent with **stale contents** of a previously-freed block.

**What is established:** an *active* writer stores `0x80000001` into `page+4` repeatedly (the poison
is re-applied each time the chunk is re-binned and broken again, 4× per firing run); the victim chunk
is `0x4ff0` in all three fires — one distinctive repeating allocation; and the free ring reports **no
logged large free covering the page**, so it was not freed through `free()` recently.

⚠ The experiment that would settle live-vs-stale — poison the whole large chunk body and count broken
words — **crashed the allocator** (harness rc=3, zero seeds completing) and was reverted. The page+4
instrument is untouched and still validated; the harness is healthy again (4/4 seeds). Redoing it
needs a careful look at which parts of a large free chunk are genuinely unused, which my first attempt
got wrong.

### C1: the decisive instrument is shipped — and the "crash" that stopped it was a misdiagnosis

Last turn I reverted the whole-body poison because the harness exited 3 with no output, and I called
that "it crashes the allocator". **It does not.** With `--no-selftest` the harness runs clean. The
conflict is with the harness's own **detector self-test**, which deliberately corrupts free chunks to
prove its detectors can fire — and the new poison overwrites that corruption, so the self-test fails
and exits before printing anything. An allocator built with this instrument needs `--no-selftest`.
The allocator was never at fault; I threw away a working change on a wrong reading of an exit code.

Re-validated and shipped: 6 seeds × 60 000 ops, **2 944 251 free chunks, 0 violations, 0 false
positives**.

**Why this instrument is the one that matters.** `page+4` is the *only* poisoned word, and a report
only happens when it differs — so "page+4 is always the corrupted word" is **selection bias, not
evidence**. The vertex-looking data around it is unpoisoned, and therefore equally consistent with
stale contents of a previously-freed block. The body poison settles it: `p4bodyN` / `p4bodyB` count
how many body words come back broken, so **1 broken word = an isolated stray store; many = somebody is
still writing the block**. That is exactly the live-vs-stale question the hunt now turns on, and it is
the claim I over-reached on two turns ago.

**Soak: 5 runs, no fire.** Unremarkable on its own (p ≈ 0.24 at the ~25%/run page+4 rate), but worth
flagging as a live caveat rather than discovering it later: the body poison writes the *whole* large
free chunk on every free, which is a large change in memory traffic and could plausibly perturb the
very event it is meant to catch. If it stays silent for another handful of runs that becomes a
finding in its own right, and the page+4-only build is the control to fall back to.

### ⚠ I broke the netboot bench with my own instrument — and graded seven void runs as clean

The body poison lives in **libphoenix**, so it is paid by *every* process. Unrestricted, it memset the
whole body on every large free — fine in SuperTuxKart, fatal for the **lwip daemon**, whose
packet-buffer frees became slow enough that DHCP no longer completed inside `nfs-fs`'s 30 s window.
The Pi then booted on the RAM root, where `/bin` does not exist, and every command returned
`psh: /bin/... not found`.

**Seven consecutive soaks were void, and I reported them as "no fire".** `0 poison reports` is exactly
what a run that never executed the workload produces. The tell was there the whole time — the flip
counter was empty in all seven — and I only found it when I went to check whether the poison was
suppressing the event.

Fixed by gating the body poison to the victim size class (`0x4000..0x6000`; every fire so far has been
`0x4ff0`), which lwip's buffers fall outside. The verify uses the same gate, or it would count
never-poisoned words as broken. **Bench verified restored: `dhcp_fail=0`, NFS mounted, `/dev/umass0`
present.**

Two rules, now in memory: anything added to libphoenix is paid by every process, so weigh it against
the daemons the bench depends on and not just the app being debugged; and an instrument's silence is
evidence only once the workload is asserted to have run — in the same command that reads the
instrument.

**Soak resumed on the gated build, now asserting the workload every run: 3 runs, 2565–2663 flips each,
0 fires.** Unremarkable at the ~25%/run page+4 rate. Every soak line from here reports the flip count
beside the instrument count, so a void run cannot be mistaken for a clean one again.

### C1: dropped two instruments that were costing runs rather than buying evidence

**`memtrip` is out.** A void run — caught immediately by the new flip-count assertion — was SuperTuxKart
crashing at startup: two EL0 Data Aborts, `far=0x10`, no allocator guard firing. Symbolized with
self-validation (the instruction at `pc` is `ldr x0, [x28, #16]`, and 16 = `far`) as
**`FontManager::loadFonts()`**, right after five font-load errors: a NULL font face dereferenced
unchecked, i.e. an allocation failure under memory pressure — **not C1**. The pressure was memtrip's
64 MiB. It had never fired in ~10 runs and, being a separate process, structurally cannot see a
CPU-side writer at all. Runs went from ~2620 to ~2760 flips without it.

**The body poison is out too — it appears to suppress the event it measures.** Eight valid runs after
it landed: **0 fires**, against ~25%/run before (p ≈ 0.10). The mechanism is plausible, not just
statistical: its gate covers exactly the victim size class, so it bulk-writes the very pages the
corruption lands in, on every free. An instrument that only helps when the event fires, and that seems
to stop it firing, is worse than none.

✔ Corroboration for last turn's diagnosis: with the body poison gone, the harness passes **with its
detector self-test enabled again** — confirming that was what it had been tripping over.

Kept: `page+4`, the recent-large-free ring, the page-head dump — the exact configuration that produced
every fire so far. One post-revert soak: clean, 2657 flips.

**Live-vs-stale stays open.** The next design must not bulk-write the victim pages: a checksum of the
page recorded at poison time and compared at verify time is two words instead of a whole body.

### C1: the live-vs-stale discriminator is in — and I over-estimated the fire rate

**Shipped:** a page-head checksum (`p4ckOK`). 13 reads and one extra word per page, against the body
poison's ~1000 writes. `p4ckOK=1` means only `page+4` moved — an isolated stray store into otherwise
stale memory; `p4ckOK=0` means the page was still being written, i.e. a live buffer the allocator
believes is free. Host-validated on all four cases (untouched → OK; only page+4 → OK; a changed vertex
word → NOT-OK; word 0 correctly excluded), and the harness passes **with its detector self-test
enabled**, which the body poison tripped — so it really is the lighter touch it claims to be.

**But the thing I should have noticed sooner:** the instrument fired in 3 of its first 7 runs, I read
that as ~40%/run, and then judged every later configuration against it. Since then — **15 consecutive
valid runs, flip count asserted every time, 0 fires**, across four configurations. Every suspicion
dissolved on measurement:

| suspicion | with | without | verdict |
|---|---|---|---|
| body poison suppresses it | 0/8 | 0/7 | not supported |
| `memtrip` enables it | 3/11 | 0/11 | p ≈ 0.21, restoring it at 32 and 64 MiB gave nothing |

The simplest reading is that the **true rate is nearer 10–15% and the opening 3-in-7 was a cluster**:
at p = 0.15, 3+ in 7 has probability 0.17 and 0 in 15 has 0.09 — both unremarkable together, whereas
at 43% the drought would be p ≈ 0.0002.

⛔ **Four builds went on configuration churn the statistics never justified.** The instrument set is
stable now; the right move is to leave it alone and accumulate runs rather than keep changing the
thing I am measuring with.

### ★★★★ C1: a designed A/B — one extra CPU write suppresses it, pointing at a non-coherent writer

23 valid runs with no fire, and the only instrument delta from the last firing run was the page-head
checksum's extra store at `page+8`. Instead of guessing again, I disabled that store and **declared
N = 8 in advance**.

| arm | valid runs | fires |
|---|---|---|
| checksum store ON | 23 | **0** |
| checksum store OFF | 6 | **2** (3 and 4 reports) |

Fisher p ≈ 0.03. (Two of the eight runs were the known `memtrip 64` OOM crash — 2 EL0 aborts in
`FontManager::loadFonts()` — and are excluded; that failure mode is now re-confirmed at ~25% of runs,
so use 32 MiB.)

**Why this is worth more than the rate.** `page+4` and `page+8` sit in the **same 64-byte cache
line**, so the only thing the extra store changes is that line's CPU-side state. A **CPU** wild
pointer writes through the same coherent cache and could not be masked by another CPU write — but a
**non-coherent DMA/GPU** write to RAM can be, because a later cache writeback simply restores the
CPU's copy. So the suppression is evidence *for* the DMA/GPU hypothesis and *against* a CPU-side stray
pointer, which is the first time anything has separated those two.

⚠ The mechanism is inference; the suppression itself is measured.

Both fires again had `p4csize = 0x4ff0` (4th and 5th time), again reported *no logged large free
covers this page*, and again the page held a **20-byte-stride textured 2D vertex array** —
`{float u, float v, float x, float y, RGBA}`, x ≈ 1815, y ≈ 145–175: 1080p HUD coordinates.

### C1: a second A/B points at the BO-close munmap — as a lead, not a result

With the non-coherent-writer finding in hand, the V3D winsys reads differently. `ioc_close_bo()`
clears the GPU page table (`va_free`, l.1143) and *then* `munmap`s the BO's CPU mapping — handing the
physical pages back to the kernel, where malloc can immediately re-acquire them. Any V3D cache line
still dirty for those pages would write back into the new owner.

The driver already ships the knob (`V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO=1`), so I flipped it to default-on (psh has no
`export`), declared **N = 8** ahead, and asserted the arm was live on every run via the driver's own
banner:

| arm | valid runs | fires |
|---|---|---|
| munmap ON — pages returned to the kernel | 6 | **2** |
| munmap OFF — pages kept | 8 | **0** |

⚠ **Fisher p ≈ 0.17. Suggestive, not significant** — and confounded, because the OFF arm ran
`memtrip 32` and the ON arm `memtrip 64`. I am calling this a lead rather than a result; the last
fortnight has taught me what happens when I promote one of these early.

**Ruled out while getting there:** the close path *does* clear the PT, and before the munmap, so the
naive "munmap without unmapping the GPU" story is wrong; and the driver *does* issue `L2TCACTL`
`FLM_CLEAN` write-backs in several post-job paths, so there is no blanket missing flush.

**Next:** more runs in both arms to get past p ≈ 0.05 with the memtrip size held constant. If it
holds, the question becomes which V3D cache can still be dirty for a BO's pages at `GEM_CLOSE`.

The A/B arm is reverted; the tree carries the documented default again, rebuilt and synced.

### ★★★★ C1: we advertise a cache-flush contract to Mesa and never honour it

Stopped paying ~14 runs per arm for one bit and went looking at the mechanism instead.

**The finding.** `ioc_get_param` returns `SUPPORTS_CACHE_FLUSH = 1` (l.2584). Mesa reads that cap
twice: it is what makes it expose shader images and SSBOs at all (`v3d_screen.c:173,178,305`), and
it is the gate on setting `DRM_V3D_SUBMIT_CL_FLUSH_CACHE` for any job whose shaders wrote through
the TMU (`v3d_job.c:694`). Linux answers that flag with a `CACHE_CLEAN` job running
`v3d_clean_caches()` — TMUWCF drain **and wait**, then L2T clean **and wait** — before the job is
signalled (`v3d_submit.c:998` → `v3d_gem.c:202`). **`ioc_submit_cl` never reads `s->flags`.** Its
end-of-job clean is a bare `FLM_CLEAN` that is issued and not awaited, and nothing on that path
drains the TMU write combiner; the only drain in the file is the TFU epilogue, whose own comment
says the combiner holds *partial cache-line writes that never reach RAM* on their own.

↩ **This corrects my own line in the C1 row** ("the driver does issue `FLM_CLEAN` write-backs in
several post-job paths, so there is no blanket missing flush"). The write-backs are there; the
*wait* and the *combiner drain* are not. The distinction decides the case: a late L2T line
writeback restores a full 64 bytes of tile data, **not** one stray u32 with its neighbours intact,
so only a write combiner can produce C1's signature.

**Next step is an instrument, not a rate.** `V3D_BO_QUARANTINE=1` (devices `24b3d5e`) holds each
closed BO's pages, fills them with `0xa5a5a5a5` and scans them before release — so anything that
writes a **closed** BO is named with handle, offset, value and closes-since-close, in one run
rather than fourteen. Sound because default BOs are `MAP_UNCACHED`; cacheable and scanout BOs are
skipped because they could not answer. `=selftest` plants the exact C1 word, so the detector is
seen to fire before its silence is trusted. Arm B is `V3D_CL_CACHE_CLEAN=1`, the Linux sequence —
if it silences the tripwire, that is the diagnosis and the fix at once.

**A/B #2 is de-confounded.** Four munmap-ON runs at `memtrip 32`, matching the OFF arm, all four
valid: **2 fires / 4**. The memtrip size turns out not to matter much (32 gives 2/4, 64 gives 2/6),
so pooling is fair — **munmap ON 4/10, munmap OFF 0/8, Fisher p ≈ 0.069**. Suggestive, still not
significant, and buying the last 0.02 of p would cost most of a night for one bit. That is the case
for the instrument over more runs.

**And the mechanism is plausible for this workload specifically:** `tmu_dirty_rcl` has three
setters, and the third is **register spilling** (`vir_register_allocate.c:614`) — any shader complex
enough to spill writes its registers to scratch through the TMU. So STK does not need shader images
for this to fire, only a shader that spills. The build counts flagged jobs rather than assuming it.

**Also:** `syntax-check.sh` gained a `SYNTAX_CHECK_CFLAGS` hook: this file is built inside the Mesa tree, so its includes never appeared
in the devices `make -n` and the helper could not check it at all.

### ★★★★★ C1: the first significant result — retention suppresses it, and the retained pages are clean

Five quarantine runs, 2531–2725 frames each, selftest found on every one.

| arm | fires | runs |
|---|---|---|
| default (pages return to the kernel) | 4 | 10 |
| `V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO=1` | 0 | 8 |
| closed-BO quarantine | 0 | 5 |

Pooled retention **0/13 vs default 4/10, Fisher p ≈ 0.024** — the first thing in this hunt that clears
0.05. And the quarantine adds the half that constrains: across those 5 runs it held and **fully
scanned ~1000 closed BOs and found nothing written to them**. A writer aimed at the BO's memory
would have been caught.

So the event needs closed-BO pages to go **back to the kernel**, yet nothing touches those pages
while we hold them.

↩ **`memtrip`'s null has to be re-read, and that includes my own earlier notes on it.** It has never
fired — not even in the two runs that fired `p4` 2 and 4 times. I had written that up as evidence
the write is *aimed* at something about heap headers. It is not: **memtrip mmaps its region at
process start, before any BO has been closed, so it can never be handed a recycled BO page.** Its
silence is precisely what the retention result predicts and it constrains nothing.

**Two hypotheses died cheaply this round, both before costing a build.** The write-combiner
mechanism: the flagged-job counter reads **zero** for a full run, and I checked the two ways that
count could lie (Mesa does query the cap; `struct drm_v3d_submit_cl` is byte-identical between the
two headers) — STK produces no TMU-writing job, so nothing is parked to drain late. And a
cache-attribute aliasing story: `_pmap_cacheOpBeforeChange` (`hal/aarch64/pmap.c:271`) does flush on
cached→uncached and on unmap, so that gap does not exist.

**Next:** attribute a *fired* page. `V3D_BO_TRACE` is default-on and the quarantine back to
default-off — it suppresses the very event it would report, so leaving it on would buy silence. A
firing run then goes through `scripts/c1-was-it-a-bo.sh`: inside a previously-CLOSED BO's `cpu=`
range ⇒ the closed-BO route is real; never a BO ⇒ C1 is a CPU-side use-after-free and the GPU
framing goes.

### ⛔⛔ C1: `psh` HAS `export` — so every A/B in this hunt compared two different binaries

The single most consequential thing found tonight, and it is a correction to a premise I inherited
and repeated.

**"psh has no `export`" is false.** It is a registered builtin (`pshapp/env.c:154`, `app_export`,
calling `setenv(name, value, 1)`) and it **propagates to children** — measured on the bench, not
read: `export V3D_BO_TRACE=1` produced **1342 trace lines in a binary where the trace is compiled
default-OFF**.

Because that premise was believed, every arm of every A/B was **compiled in as a default**. So each
comparison was between different binaries — and this bug is demonstrably sensitive to exactly that:

| arm | build | fires |
|---|---|---|
| baseline default | A | **4 / 10** |
| `V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO=1` | B | 0 / 8 |
| closed-BO quarantine | C | 0 / 5 |
| BO trace on | D | 0 / 8 |
| passive attribution table | E | 0 / 4 |

**0 fires in 24 runs across four rebuilt binaries**, against 4/10 in the baseline one; at a 40 % rate
that is p ≈ 5e-6. Even the deliberately "passive" instrument adds 96 KB of static BSS to the driver,
which shifts every address after it. The allocator instrument was verified live in every one of
those runs (12–16 `C1-hunt: created` lines each), so they are real zeros, not void runs.

**So `retention 0/13 vs default 4/10, p ≈ 0.024` is confounded with "two rebuilt binaries vs one
older binary"** — and the same objection applies to the munmap A/B and the checksum A/B before it.
The observations stand; the attribution to the knob does not. It also casts doubt on mechanisms
recorded as "eliminated" by arms that may only have been suppressing the bug.

**The firing layout was restored, and that settles the layout question.** Reverting just those two
files and rebuilding brought the bug straight back — `ENV-off-1` fired `p4=4` on the very first run,
after 0 fires in 24. Same sources, same bench, same night: the suppression is the relink.

**Then the A/B as it should always have been run** — one binary, arms chosen by `export`,
interleaved, each arm asserted from the driver's banner (`keepbanner` 0/1/0/1/0/1), allocator
instrument live in all six:

| arm | fires | runs |
|---|---|---|
| `V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO` unset | 3 | 6 |
| `V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO=1` | 0 | 6 |

Fisher one-tailed **p = 0.091**; pooled with the 2026-09-23 baseline, which is the *same*
byte-identical binary, **off 7/16 vs on 0/6, p = 0.067**.

⚠ **Suggestive, not significant, and I am not going to dress it up.** The retention effect survives
de-confounding in direction and rough size, but it still does not clear 0.05 — it needs ~12 runs an
arm, not 6. Read this as the honest replacement for the withdrawn `p ≈ 0.024`, not as confirmation
of it.

**The bench is left on master, and master reproduces C1.** The hunt instruments went behind
`-DV3D_C1_HUNT` (devices `b57a7a6`, libphoenix `e69c33d`), checked to compile both ways. The
guarded rebuild is *not* byte-identical to the firing binary (`47a7020625f0…` vs `b427922a0969…`) —
two genuine unguarded changes remain in that file, the `cacheable` field on `struct pbo` and the
`FLUSH_CACHE` handling — so it was verified the way that matters instead: three runs on the shipped
build, **one void** (boot reached psh/lwip/DHCP/IP but the harness closed its window at 30 s before
sending commands, so `heaps=0` — recorded as void, not clean) and **1 of the 2 valid runs fired
`p4=4`**. The hunt continues on master with no revert dance.

### ⭑ And the fire itself says something the rate never will

I had four fires sitting unread in `c1env-off-1`. All four: `p4got = 0x80000001`, `p4csize = 0x4ff0`
(eight times running now), `p4call` valid by its complement check and symbolizing to `operator
new(unsigned long)` with `bl malloc` immediately before it. Two distinct pages, each reported twice.

The page dump is structure, not noise — at page `0x0b916000`:

```
[+0x00] = 0x0b916000     <- a pointer to the page itself
[+0x04] = 0x80000001     <- "the corruption"
[+0x08] = 0x0b916048     <- a pointer to page+0x48
```

Read as little-endian 64-bit, bytes 0–7 are **`0x800000010b916000`** — a valid heap address with
`0x80000001` in the **upper 32 bits**. That is precisely the signature of the separately recorded
"STK high-bits pointer crash" (`far=0x800000010c845168`). They are very likely one defect.

And the field I had not been weighting: **`p4ckOK = 0` on all four**. By the instrument's own
documented semantics that means the page was *still being written* — **a live buffer that the
allocator believes is free**. So C1 most economically reads not as a stray four-byte write at all,
but as a chunk on malloc's free list that is still in use, with `0x80000001` merely a field of the
live object. That explains why the closed-BO quarantine found nothing, why `memtrip` never fires,
and why every GPU-side mechanism has come up empty — and it moves the hunt to the allocator, not the
graphics stack.

⚠ **Trap for whoever picks this up:** the attribution instrument and the firing layout are currently
mutually exclusive — `c1_seen[]` is compiled in unconditionally and its 96 KB of BSS shifts every
address after it. Gate it behind a compile-time switch, default off, so master builds the firing
layout.

**Two hypotheses died cleanly tonight, both before costing a build:** the TMU write-combiner (the
flagged-job counter reads **zero** for a full run; checked that Mesa does query the cap and that
`struct drm_v3d_submit_cl` is byte-identical between the two headers), and cache-attribute aliasing
(`_pmap_cacheOpBeforeChange` does flush on cached→uncached and on unmap). The real contract breach
found along the way — we advertise `SUPPORTS_CACHE_FLUSH` and never honour the flag — is genuine and
worth fixing, but it is not C1's writer.

### ★★★★★ C1 ROOT-CAUSED: a mailbox page is handed back to the kernel while VideoCore still owns it

Found by reading data I already had, not by another bench run — and it says the whole framing of this
hunt (mine and the previous sessions') was the wrong tree.

**One signature, four sites.** The high 32 bits of a 64-bit word replaced by `0x80000001`, low half
intact: the malloc heap header (`0x80000001_0000d000`), a Mesa `hash_table::mem_ctx`
(`0x80000001_0b916000`), the STK crash `far` (`0x80000001_0c845168`), and — decisively — **a kernel
zone list link**, `vm/kmalloc.c:62`: `next = 0x80000001c46ccf80` where `0xffffffffc46ccf80` belonged.
`malloc_dl.c:1436` records it as **154 of 154 identical**.

**No userspace use-after-free can corrupt a kernel pointer.** A *constant* 32-bit value landing at
**+4 of a page in both address spaces** is a device writing to a PHYSICAL address. And `0x8000000x`
is the VideoCore mailbox property-interface response code, written by the firmware into `msg[1]` —
offset +4 of the property buffer.

**The defect, in source.** `mboxProp()` mmaps a page per call, takes `va2pa()`, rings the doorbell
with that physical address, waits with a **bounded** spin, and then `munmap`s the page
**unconditionally — including on the timeout path, after the request was already sent**. The page
returns to the kernel free pool while VideoCore still owns it; the late reply lands at +4 of whatever
owns the page next. Two copies had it:

- `gpu/rpi4-v3d/mesa/v3d_phoenix_power.c:61` — **linked into the application**, so its leaked page
  typically goes straight back to the same process's malloc. This is the one that matters for STK.
- `gpu/rpi4-v3d/v3d_gpu.c:268` (the GPU daemon) and `wifi/rpi4-wifi/rpi4-wifi.c:111`.

`bt/rpi4-hci` and `pcie/server` wait unbounded, so they never release a page with a request in
flight — unaffected. `misc/rpi4-vcmbox` is the safe model: one persistent bounce buffer, never
unmapped.

**Everything stubborn now has an explanation:** the constant value and exact +4 offset (it is
`msg[1]`); kernel *and* user corruption (physical write, MMU-independent); why `memtrip` and the
closed-BO quarantine stayed silent (a mailbox page is neither); why page-retention knobs moved the
rate (they change which page is recycled into the line of fire); `p4ckOK = 0` (a live foreign writer
— the firmware). Corroboration: the V3D clock-race note measured **`MBOX_FAIL` at 9 % of calls**
before serialisation — 9 % took exactly this timeout-then-munmap path.

**Fixed** (devices `fb72a37`): the page is released only once our own response has surfaced in the
FIFO; otherwise it is deliberately leaked and reported loudly. One leaked page per timed-out
clock/power query against silent system-wide corruption. The MMIO window maps registers, not RAM, so
it is still always unmapped.

↩ **Downgrade, same session: "root-caused" is too strong.** The signature analysis stands and the
defect is real, but I have **no positive evidence the precondition fires**. `v3d_phoenix_powerOn`
runs in every STK run including all six that fired, yet its clock-confirm retry line reads **0
retries across all 37 graded runs** — weak evidence, since the `SET_*` calls are `(void)mboxProp(...)`
with the result discarded, so a timeout there is silent. And the fix's new leak line reads `leak=0`
on its first run, which also did not fire, so it settles nothing.

**The decisive observation is a run with `p4 > 0` and `leak = 0`** — that excludes this mechanism for
that fire. Any `leak > 0` proves the precondition occurs. Timing favours the hypothesis
(`v3d_phoenix_powerOn` runs at STK startup, moments before the corruption, and a late firmware reply
lands in microseconds — which also rules out the boot-time wifi caller for gameplay-time damage).
Keep the fix either way: handing a page back while a device holds its PA is a defect on its own
terms.

**Three nulls against the candidate, with a coverage caveat.** Across every C1 log ever captured: 0
`RACED`, 0 `BADRESP`, 0 `WRFULL`, 0 clock-confirm retries in 37 runs — no mailbox transaction is
recorded as having failed at all. ⚠ But none of those covers the suspect path: the in-process
`mboxProp` prints nothing on failure and its callers discard the result, and the RACED/BADRESP
diagnostics belong to the **vcmbox server**, covering only its own calls. The one instrument that
does cover it is the new `leak` counter. So: **undetermined, not refuted** — my earlier ~70 %
confidence is not supportable; treat it as a coin-flip until a firing run is graded with `leak`.

**Fix build, 4 runs: `leak=0`, `p4=0` in all four.** Uninformative both ways — 0 fires in 4 is what a
relink produces here regardless, and `leak=0` means the timeout path never ran, so the mechanism had
no chance to act. **C1 stays open.** The `leak` counter is at least a clean measurement: it lives
inside `mboxProp()`, so it covers the `(void)mboxProp(...)` callers too.

### C1: a positive control, because waiting for the coincidence is not converging

The mailbox candidate is stuck on an unobserved precondition — it only corrupts memory when a call
times out *after* the doorbell, and nothing records that happening (leak counter 0/4 runs; 0 RACED,
0 BADRESP, 0 WRFULL, 0 retries across every log ever captured — though note none of those three
covers the in-process `mboxProp`, whose failures are silent).

So provoke it rather than wait. `export V3D_MBOX_LEAKTEST=<n>` (devices `1477be7`) makes power-on
issue n benign `GET_FIRMWARE_REVISION` queries that ring the doorbell and release their page
unwaited — the defect, on purpose, n times.

- forcing it pushes C1 well above its baseline (**measured ~6 %**, not the ~1-in-3 once assumed here) ⇒ the mechanism demonstrably produces the
  signature;
- forcing it produces nothing across a few runs ⇒ it cannot, and the candidate is dead.

Either answer settles it, which beats waiting on a rare event. Both arms are in **one binary**
selected by `export`, so the comparison is not confounded by a relink — the trap that invalidated
every earlier A/B here. A benign read-only tag is used because the real clock/power calls are
retried until confirmed, so failing those would hang bring-up instead of testing anything; and the
FIFO is drained afterwards so the burst cannot wedge the next mailbox user.

Also this turn: audited every `va2pa`-then-release site on Pi 4 — **no second candidate**. Of the
transient-PA users actually built here (mailbox, xHCI, genet, SD ADMA2, V3D BOs) only the mailbox can
write a *constant* at a *fixed* offset. EHCI is not built for this target; its `ehci_transAbort()`
frees qTDs without the Interrupt-on-Async-Advance handshake, same class, noted only.

### C1: the positive control fires, but weakly — so the control itself was rebuilt

Provoking the defect on demand works. Results, **all in one binary, arms by `export`**:

| control | fires | runs |
|---|---|---|
| `V3D_MBOX_LEAKTEST=64` | 2 | 4 |
| unset | 0 | 3 |

Fisher **p ≈ 0.29** — directionally right, not conclusive. For reference the *previous* binary (the
fix build, no control) was 0 fires / 6.

⚠ **The weakness is in my control, not just the sample.** It released the page immediately *after*
ringing the doorbell, and `GET_FIRMWARE_REVISION` is answered quickly — so the firmware had usually
already written its reply before the page was freed. That is a window of microseconds where the real
defect opens a timeout-scale one.

**Rebuilt so the page is released BEFORE the doorbell** — and A/B #2 came out **ON 0/3, OFF 1/3**: no
effect.

| control | design | ON | OFF |
|---|---|---|---|
| #1 | valid request accepted, page freed *after* doorbell | 2/4 | 0/3 |
| #2 | page freed *before* doorbell (firmware sees garbage) | 0/3 | 1/3 |

⭑ **#2's null does not refute the mechanism.** Freeing before the doorbell means the firmware reads a
garbage size/tag word and can simply reject the request without writing at all. The real defect needs
a *valid* request accepted first and the page freed afterwards — that is #1, and #1 is the one that
leaned positive. The two bracket the mechanism rather than settling it.

⛔ **Stopping here per the two-attempts rule.** Two designed experiments, both inconclusive: the
mailbox candidate is **not established** and C1 stays open. The `munmap`-after-doorbell fix is kept
on its own merits. The control is now compile-gated behind `-DV3D_C1_HUNT` (it corrupts memory
deliberately; an env var alone is too easy to trip on a shipped image).

⛔⛔ **Then the close-out run refuted it cleanly, without needing more control runs.** The shipped
master build (fix in, control compiled out, both verified by `strings`) fired **`p4=4`** — 2507
frames, 0 faults, psh reached. Beside that: the `leak` counter is **0 in every run of the fixed
build**, so the post-doorbell timeout path **never executes**. The code the fix guards is never
reached, yet C1 fires anyway — a mechanism that never runs cannot be the cause.

**What survives:** the `munmap`-after-doorbell defect is real and the fix stays (it is latent here),
and the **signature analysis is untouched** — a constant 32-bit value at +4 of a page, in kernel *and*
user space, is a device writing a physical address, and no userspace UAF can produce the kernel
instance. The search is still "which device writes a constant at a fixed page offset" — just not the
V3D power mailbox.

➡ **Re-examine the audit that made the mailbox look unique.** It ruled out xHCI, genet, SD ADMA2 and
V3D BOs because their writes "vary in value and offset"; with the mailbox out, that argument needs
re-testing rather than inheriting. Only if it survives is control **#1** worth running to ~12 runs per
arm — it matches the defect's ordering
and at 2/4 vs 0/3 it needs statistical power, not redesign. Independently of all of this, the
**signature analysis still stands**: a constant 32-bit value at +4 of a page, in kernel *and* user
space, is a device writing a physical address, and no userspace use-after-free can produce the kernel
instance.

Also fixed along the way: n=256 never finished (posting without consuming filled the FIFO, so every
later call burned its full 4,000,000-spin budget — 37 s window, STK never rendered, system otherwise
healthy). Each iteration now reconciles one FIFO entry.

### ⛔ C1: "the corruption is always at page+4" was an artefact of where we looked

Went to re-examine the audit that left the mailbox as the unique fit, and found the premise under it
is unfalsifiable.

**The page poison armed exactly ONE word per 4 KiB page, at +4.** The instrument was blind to
1023/1024 of every page, so "C1 always lands at page+4" could never have come out any other way. That
unexamined premise is load-bearing: it is what made "a device writing a FIXED offset" compelling, and
it is the argument that ruled out genet, xHCI and the SD ADMA2 descriptors — on the grounds that
"their writes vary in offset", a property the instrument had no way to observe.

**Now four probes per page**, three deliberately not at +4 (`+0x404`, `+0x804`, `+0xc04`), each keyed
on its own address so a block copy cannot alias one onto another; the report names the offset that
broke. Still a far lighter touch than the body poison that suppressed the event.

**Verified it can fail first**, per the project rule: `tools/malloc-harness` gains `hz_p4Probes()`,
which corrupts each offset in its own alloc/free/corrupt/alloc cycle and requires the verifier to
name it. All four report correctly, with distinct poison values. (It has to hold a live block in the
same heap — freeing the big one alone munmaps the heap, and the write then faults and takes the
harness down through `_exit(3)`, which discards buffered stdout and hides where it died.)

**Result: 0 fires / 6 with four probes armed**, against ~1-in-3 with one. The experiment built to
test the premise is itself suppressed by the instrument, so the premise **remains untested** — not
confirmed, not refuted, and the exclusions built on it stay unsupported rather than wrong.

⭑⭑ **That makes one fact outrank every mechanism hypothesis in this issue: five consecutive
instrument changes have each driven the fire rate to zero.**

| change | fires |
|---|---|
| `V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO=1` | 0 / 8 |
| closed-BO quarantine | 0 / 5 |
| BO trace on | 0 / 8 |
| passive attribution table | 0 / 4 |
| 4-probe page poison | 0 / 6 |
| *unmodified binary* | *~1 in 3* |

**C1 cannot currently be studied by adding instrumentation.** Any change to the binary's write
pattern or layout stops the event happening — and a byte-identical rebuild of the unmodified binary
fires again on demand, so this is the relink, not drift.

**Applied:** the 4-probe set is opt-in (`-DC1_P4_WIDE`) so master keeps reproducing the bug; both
configurations syntax-check; the harness proves every offset can fail, so the widened instrument is
trustworthy whenever someone chooses to pay the suppression cost.

⚠ **Master's reproduction is not re-verified.** After returning to one probe, 3 runs gave 0 fires —
P ≈ 0.30 at the historical rate, so consistent with restoration but not evidence of it. And master is
not the historical firing binary regardless: the poison is now keyed on the probe address rather than
the page, so values and code differ even at one probe. **The reliable reproducer is the archived,
byte-identical one** — ↩ and the two-file recipe first written here was **wrong** (it yields
`1d883a9a…`). STK links three devices files: revert `v3d_phoenix_winsys.c`, `v3d_phoenix_power.c` and
`v3d_gpu.c` to `24b3d5e^`, plus `malloc_dl.c` to `1ea2083^`, rebuild, and check `sha256` against
`artifacts/c1-symbols/supertuxkart-b427922a09694e51.elf`. Verified: that reproduces `b427922a…`
exactly. "Revert the file I edited" is not the same as "restore the binary".

➡ **Rule for whoever continues: stop adding instruments.** The only moves that do not destroy the
phenomenon are env-selected arms inside ONE unmodified binary (`export` works), and longer runs on
the stock build. Anything needing a relink must have its own baseline re-measured first.

### ✅✅ C1: the first statistically supported result — `V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO=1` suppresses it

Followed my own rule from last turn (stop adding instruments; the only sound moves are env-selected
arms in one unmodified binary) and spent the night on power rather than new code.

Restored the archived firing binary and **verified it by hash** — `b427922a09694e51…`, byte-identical.
↩ The recovery recipe I recorded earlier was wrong: it named two files and yields `1d883a9a…`. STK
links **three** devices files (`v3d_phoenix_winsys.c`, `v3d_phoenix_power.c`, `v3d_gpu.c`) plus
`malloc_dl.c`. Corrected in the issue row. "Revert the file I edited" is not "restore the binary".

Then ran 9 more interleaved runs, arms by `export`:

| arm | fires | runs |
|---|---|---|
| `V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO` unset | 7 | 19 |
| `V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO=1` | 0 | 12 |

**Fisher one-tailed p = 0.019.** All 31 runs are the same byte-identical binary, arms asserted per run
from the driver's banner, allocator instrument live in every one — so this is the first comparison in
the whole hunt free of the relink confound.

⚠ **Where the power comes from:** tonight's 9 runs alone say nothing (ON 0/6, OFF 0/3, p = 1.0). The
significance rests on pooling 19 OFF runs across three sessions *in the identical binary*. Legitimate
— same binary, harness and workload — but it is pooling, and no single session has ever reached
significance.

⭑ **What it means, carefully.** It establishes that **retaining closed-BO pages prevents the event**.
It does *not* say the writer targets those pages — the quarantine held ~1000 of them with a
per-run-proven scan and found nothing written to them. Together those give one reading: **the event
needs closed-BO pages to be recycled into the kernel's general pool**, and what lands on them after
recycling is still unidentified.

➡ Sharpest open question, and answerable without new instrumentation: what does the kernel do
differently with a page freed by `munmap` of an uncached `MAP_CONTIGUOUS` BO versus an ordinary
anonymous page?

### ⛔ 31 coord commits were pushed to the wrong remote (owner spotted it)

Coord has two remotes: `origin` = `houp/phoenix-rpi` (personal fork) and `publish` =
`rpi-phoenix-rtos/rpi-phoenix-rtos-coordination` (the org repo). `main` **tracks publish/main**, but I
had been running `git push origin main` — which the fork *accepts*, so every push reported success
while the org repo stayed at `1e368a4ba`, the commit from before this session began.

The sibling repos escaped only by luck: their `origin` is upstream `phoenix-rtos`, which denies the
push with 403, so that mistake self-corrected and I switched to `publish` there.

Pushed all 31 commits to `publish/main` (clean fast-forward, no divergence, no force). Verified
`git log --oneline publish/main..HEAD` = 0. **Rule: a push that prints a successful ref update is not
evidence it went where you meant — check against the tracking remote.**

### C1: turning the one measured lever into a bounded mitigation — a BO page pool

The only statistically supported fact is that keeping closed-BO pages out of the kernel's free pool
suppresses C1 (`V3D_KEEP_CLOSED_BO=1`, 0/12 vs 7/19, p = 0.019). `KEEP_CLOSED_BO` itself is not
shippable — it never releases anything, and a six-lap run closes ~299 BOs totalling ~96 MiB.

So: **recycle the pages inside the driver instead**. A closed BO's mapping goes on a size-keyed free
list and the next create of that exact size reuses it (`V3D_BO_POOL=1`, off by default so both arms
live in one binary). Exact-size reuse only; cacheable BOs never pooled; zeroing and PT setup shared
with the fresh path. Capped at 256 entries / 24 MiB, spilling to `munmap` beyond that.

**Measured, first working run:** 202 hits / 1464 takes, peak **24 MiB (at the cap)**, **25 spilled**,
2630 frames, 0 faults. So it is bounded as designed — but it is a **~12× reduction in exposure, not
elimination**: 25 pages a run still go back to the kernel versus ~300 without it.

Two self-inflicted detours worth recording, both instances of rules this project already has:

- The release call landed inside `#ifdef V3D_C1_HUNT`, which master does not compile, so nothing ever
  fed the pool. I read the first run as "pool on but ineffective" because my `grep 'v3d-pool'` found
  no banner — the banner *had* printed, as `[0m: BO page pool ON …`, its prefix eaten by an ANSI
  interleave on the UART. **Never grade presence by grepping a log for a word.**
- The stat line was keyed on `(hits + misses) % 512` sampled inside `give()`, which can never align —
  two runs printed nothing and the pool looked idle whether or not it worked. Now the first give and
  every 128th. **A silent instrument cannot be asserted.**

**A/B result, one binary, arms by `export`:**

| arm | fires | runs |
|---|---|---|
| `V3D_BO_POOL=1` | 0 | 6 |
| unset | 2 | 6 |

Fisher one-tailed **p = 0.227** — directionally consistent with the KEEP result, underpowered on its
own, and complete suppression was never the prediction (the pool still spills ~25 pages a run). Its
real value is as an **independent replication of the retention effect in a different binary**.

⛔ **Not made the default.** 24 MiB of retained mapping is a real cost, p = 0.23 is not a fix, and the
writer is still unidentified. It stays off behind `V3D_BO_POOL=1` for an owner decision. To settle
it: more runs per arm, and raise the cap to cut spills if the effect holds.

### 🧭 Strategy: stepping back from C1 (owner's call), and a system-wide health check

**Where C1 stands, and why to stop grinding it.** It has one durable result — retaining closed-BO
pages suppresses it (0/12 vs 7/19, p = 0.019) — one bounded mitigation ready for a decision (the BO
pool, `V3D_BO_POOL=1`, 0/6 vs 2/6), three refuted mechanisms, and one hard methodological finding:
**five consecutive instrument changes each drove the fire rate to zero**, so it cannot be studied by
adding instrumentation. Further progress needs either a free new signal or a lot of bench time for
one bit. It is not the best use of a night. Parked, not abandoned; everything needed to resume is in
the C1 row.

**Health check instead — the full libc suite on hardware, one cycle:**

| | |
|---|---|
| suites | 21 |
| tests | **1210** |
| failures | **0** |
| kernel faults | **0** |
| ignored | 28 |

So libphoenix is sound, and neither C1 nor C3 manifests as a libc failure. That is worth knowing: it
bounds where the remaining defects can live.

**The 28 ignored tests are the interesting part** — they are what we deliberately do not test. Most
carry upstream issue numbers (#869 ×4, #739 ×2, #709 ×2, #698, #277, #719), but three of them are a
real, self-contained gap: `stdio_printf_cspn` `ls`, `S`, `C` — the **wide-character printf
conversions**. `stdio/format.c` handles only `'s'` and `'c'`; there is no `%ls`, `%lc`, `%S` or `%C`,
and `l` before `s`/`c` merely sets the 64-bit flag.

That ties straight to debt **D3**: SuperTuxKart carries its own wide-printf shim *because Irrlicht
passes `wchar_t*` to `%s`*. Half of D3 closed on 2026-09-23 with conforming `swprintf`/`vswprintf`;
the printf-side conversions are the other half. `wcrtomb()` already exists, and the C locale is a 1:1
byte mapping, so the conversion itself is simple.

✅ **Done the same session.** `%ls`, `%lc`, `%S`, `%C` implemented (libphoenix `21d3b32`, tests
`42b9a0a`). `%ls` previously read a `wchar_t*` as `char*` — `L"Lorem"` printed as `"L"`. New
`FLAG_WIDE` + `format_printWide()`, two passes via `wcrtomb()` (size, then emit) since padding must be
known up front and a wide string cannot be staged contiguously. Precision counts bytes and never
splits a character; an encoding error fails the call.

**Gated both ways on hardware**: 4/4 red without the change, 4/4 green with it. Printf suite 129 → 133
tests, 0 failures. Full libc sweep **1214 tests, 0 failures, 0 faults**.

Two things came out as predicted, and both are recorded rather than papered over:

- The caution above was right — the upstream `ls`/`S`/`C` cases expect `L"\4399\0ns"` → `"#99"`, which
  needs a locale whose `wcrtomb()` can represent U+011B. Phoenix's C locale is 1:1 onto bytes with
  `EILSEQ` above 0xff, so a conforming implementation *cannot* produce it. They stay ignored; I wrote
  standard-conforming cases instead.
- ↩ **It does not close D3.** Re-reading the defect: Irrlicht passes `wchar_t*` to **`%s`**, a *caller*
  bug — a correct `%ls` does not help it. The shim stays. What closes is the libc gap.

Also found along the way: a NULL to `%ls` cannot even be compiled (`-Werror=format-overflow`), and the
standard leaves it undefined, so that test was dropped; and the ports layer has an unrelated
libpng→zlib link failure that the build reports while still exiting 0 — noted, not chased.

### 🔧 A failed SuperTuxKart relink could ship the PREVIOUS binary silently

Chased the loose end from last turn — a link failure the build printed while exiting 0.

**Two separate things were tangled there, and only one is a defect.** The `make ... || true` in the
supertuxkart port is *deliberate and documented*: CMake's own link cannot resolve the GLES
entrypoints, so it is expected to fail and a custom group-link from `link.txt` is the real one. Not a
bug.

**The defect is the check on that real link.** It ran in a subshell whose exit status was discarded,
and success was inferred from `[ -f "${elf}" ]`. That is only meaningful on a clean tree — on an
incremental one the previous binary is already in `bin/`, so a failed relink leaves the file present,
and the port **installs and ships the old binary reporting success**. Invisible in exactly the case
that matters most: change a source file, rebuild, measure on hardware, and unknowingly measure the
*previous* build.

**Proven both ways in isolation**, with a stale ELF present and a failing link:

| construct | result |
|---|---|
| old | prints `M3 complete`, would install `STALE-CONTENT`, **rc = 0** |
| new | `b_die: final group-link FAILED`, **rc = 9**, stale file removed |

Fixed by removing the ELF before linking and checking the linker's exit status, keeping the existence
test as a backstop — the idiom `xorg_apps` already uses (`rm -f`, `|| b_die` on the build, then the
check). SuperTuxKart was the one port not following it; a sweep of the others found no second case.
Success path re-verified with a full build (`rc=0`, `M3 complete`) and a hardware smoke run (2763
frames, 0 faults).

✔ **Tonight's C1 measurements were NOT affected** — checked rather than assumed: both saved build logs
show the group-link running and printing `M3 complete`, and each build was `strings`-verified against
the deployed binary at the time.

### ★★★ P6 re-measured: it is not storage, not `echo`, and it is ~50× bigger than the row said

Took P6 (`echo > file` ~50× slow) and started by re-measuring rather than designing a fix — the row's
figure predates the 2026-09-22 libcache change. **Both of its load-bearing claims are wrong.**

"Invisible on a RAM filesystem" is false: `echo` vs `dd` for 512 bytes × 10 files is **8 s vs 1 s on
`/ramtmp`** and **8 s vs 1 s on NFS** — the same ratio. And write size is irrelevant: 4096 bytes in
chunks of 1/16/512/4096 all cost ~1.5 ms *per call*. `echo` is slow only because it issues one call
per byte.

**The real finding is much bigger than the row: every regular-file `write()` costs ~1.5 ms**, which
caps small-I/O throughput system-wide and is only hidden when a caller uses large buffers.

`tools/writecost/` (staged at `/bin/writecost`) prices each layer on `/ramtmp` — RAM, no device:

| operation | per call |
|---|---|
| `getpid` (syscall, no IPC) | 1 µs |
| `lseek` (no IPC) | 5 µs |
| `write` → `/dev/null` (IPC) | **27 µs** |
| `fstat` (IPC, inline payload) | 56 µs |
