| `read` / `write` on a file | **1403 / 1488 µs** |

**Four hypotheses tested and killed, including two of mine:**

- *Storage* — no: identical on RAM and NFS.
- *The kernel's `msg_map()` payload mapping* — no: `/dev/null` crosses address spaces the same way for
  27 µs.
- *The partial-page copy path* — no: aligned-4096 1542 µs vs misaligned 1624 µs vs aligned-1B 1528 µs.
- *Many round trips per write* — no: `pwrite` 1492 µs == `lseek`+`write` 1496 µs, so it is **one**
  message.

**Where it localises:** `/dev/null` and `/ramtmp` are two instances of the *same* dummyfs code, and the
device-node path answers in 27 µs while the regular-file path takes 1488 µs — 55× inside one codebase
with no storage behind either. Requests serialise (1/2/4 threads → 132/232/492 ms), so the server is
single-threaded.

↩ **One experiment I had to redo.** The CPU-vs-wait test first used a single hog thread and measured
+6.6 % — worthless on a 4-core board, where one hog just occupies an idle core. With one hog per core
the per-write cost goes **1489 → 2940 µs**, which says the time is largely CPU work that must be
scheduled rather than a timer wait. Not airtight: everything is oversubscribed at that point.

➡ Next: find what dummyfs's regular-file write handler spends ~1.5 ms of CPU on.

### ✅ `/ramtmp` was NFS, not RAM — RAM-staging silently staged onto the network (54× fixed)

Chasing P6 took a wrong turn that turned out to be worth more than the original question.

↩ **First, a correction to my own last entry.** I reported that P6's row was "wrong in its two
load-bearing claims" because `echo` was just as slow on `/ramtmp` as on NFS. **That was my error:
`/ramtmp` is not a RAM filesystem on the netboot lane**, so I never measured RAM at all. Measured on
`/dev`, a genuine dummyfs instance: write **28 µs**, read 29 µs, ftruncate 23 µs. Phoenix's
regular-file write path is fast; there is no 1.5 ms per-write defect, and the original row — including
"invisible on a RAM filesystem" — stands.

The tell was in my very first table: `/ramtmp` and `/` measured **identically** (1488 vs ~1520 µs). A
RAM filesystem and an NFS mount cannot coincide like that. Identical numbers meant one backend; I read
them as "storage doesn't matter".

**The real defect, from the boot log:**

```
nfs-fs: re-bound /dev  (takeover, devfs port=3)
nfs-fs: re-bound /tmp  (takeover, tmpfs port=6)
```

The takeover *resolves* the tmpfs by looking up `/ramtmp`, then binds it onto `/tmp` — and leaves
`/ramtmp` resolving through the new NFS root. But `/ramtmp` is exactly where the RAM-staging
convention puts things: `quakespasm` and `vkquake` glue both probe `"/ramtmp/quake"` **before**
`"/tmp/quake"`, and `tools/ffmpeg-port` stages clips to `/ramtmp/e4`. So "stage it into RAM so it is
not served over the network" was staging it **onto** the network, silently.

**Fixed** (filesystems, one node): re-bind `/ramtmp` alongside `/tmp`. Same path, same build, before
and after:

| op on `/ramtmp` | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| write | 1519 µs | **28 µs** (54×) |
| read | 1404 µs | 32 µs |
| ftruncate | 2656 µs | 23 µs |

Gated: `re-bound /ramtmp` in the boot log, change strings-verified in `loader.disk`, psh reached, 0
faults; regression smoke renders STK 2666 frames with NFS mounted.

⚠ **P6 itself is still unverified on real storage** — the ext2-on-SD arm could not mount, so whether
`echo > file` is still ~50× slower after the libcache change is untested and needs the SD lane.

**Kept:** a per-layer latency table for this port — `getpid` 1 µs · `lseek` 5 µs · dummyfs write
**28 µs** · dummyfs fstat 46 µs · NFS write **~1500 µs**. `tools/writecost/` reproduces it; it takes
the directory as `argv[1]`, and the lesson is to **point it only at a path whose backend you have
confirmed**.

### What needs you

1. **Hot-plug** — physical insert/remove.
2. ~~**An AP for WiFi.**~~ ✅ Done 2026-09-23 — AP is up (`PhoenixNet`), WiFi joins and takes a full
   DHCP lease. The glom branch turned out to be already landed and was deleted. What remains is the
   `/dev/wifidata` lwip seam, so bulk traffic can actually be measured.
3. **Two decisions**, unchanged: comment density in the published ext2 fixes, and whether busybox
   goes now the six GNU tools ship.
4. **One word to let me delete 14 GB of my own scratch.** The Pi's NFS root (= its `/`) holds 14
   one-GiB images left over from the closed libext2 investigation — `after{,2}.img`, `afterrm.img`,
   `clean4k.img`, `final.img`, `p2{,b,chain,clean,full,so}.img`, `ranged.img`, `unlink{1,2}.img`.
   Nothing reads them any more. I have not removed them because they are on your disk and the delete
   is irreversible. `sdimage.img` stays — the flash skill needs it.


## 1. WHERE THINGS STAND — the SD lane (still accurate)

Everything is green and, for the first time, **green on the card as well as over the network**.

- **The SD card driver is done in both directions.** ADMA2 32-bit scatter-gather is the data path
  for reads *and* writes — the engine Linux runs on this silicon. The long-recorded "BCM2711
  DMA-write quirk" was **ours**: a raw CPU-physical address where the emmc2bus `dma-ranges` wants a
  bus address. Measured end-to-end (`/usr/bin/dd`, 64 MiB): PIO 12.2 · SDMA 12.3 · **ADMA2
  12.7–12.9 MB/s** — the SD bus bounds all three, so ADMA2's win is structural, not a rate.
- **The card now carries a current build** (devices `876e9605`, flashed 2026-09-20 **by the ADMA2
  write path itself**, 1.1 GB in 88.6 s). It is no longer the old `b95e983a` artifact, so the audio
  containment, the upstream sweep and every fix of the week are on it.
- **Six-app showcase gate 6/6 on the SD lane** (`sdrootgate`: x, qspasm 9369, q3 8480, q2 8431,
  vkq 6190, stk 1186 frames; torches present; 0 faults, 0 ADMA errors, 6/6 boots found both
  partitions) — every asset, font, shader blob and pak read off the card's ext2 root through ADMA2.
  And 6/6 on netboot (`fulldmagate`). Manifest `2026-09-20-w38-adma2-full.md`.
- **Flashing is autonomous and takes ~90 s.** The card never leaves the Pi: netboot Phoenix, `dd`
  the image from the NFS root onto `/dev/mmcblk0`, drop dnsmasq, power-cycle. Procedure kept in the
  `rpi4-flash-sd` skill. ⓘ Two traps in it: `/bin/dd` is busybox (~19× slower) and coreutils `dd`
  cannot *read* a block device here.
- **Public docs reconciled 2026-09-20** — README, `KNOWN-ISSUES.md`, the hardware-support matrix and
  `HARDWARE.md` all now say ADMA2-both-directions, and the stale "the `b95e983a` card predates the
  audio containment" advice is gone from the on-stage notes.

⏸ **Your one call:** **TD-19-adjacent break-before-make at `_pmap_mapScratch`.** TD-19 itself is
fixed and gated (the `isb` now lands where the ARM ARM requires it, and the once-proposed "add an
`isb` to the five TLBI helpers" would *not* have prevented the halt that was actually observed).
What is left is a separate, correctness-sensitive change to the same path, and it wants a separate
gate and you watching. Not urgent — nothing observed depends on it.

ⓘ **Leave the card in.** Bench default is **netboot**; both lanes are one command apart
(`netboot-server-down.sh` ⇒ SD boot, `-up` ⇒ netboot). A *written* card does not block netboot; a
**blank** one stops the Pi booting at all.

## 1b. 2026-09-20 — a retraction, and the base-tool question it opened

↩ **The "busybox `dd` is 19× slower than coreutils" claim was mine and it is FALSE.** Measured A/B in
one boot (`ddbench`, 64 MiB, same source, same sink, both binaries): **5 s vs 5 s at `bs=1M`, 6 s vs
6 s at `bs=128k`**. The "28-minute flash" was **~25 min of `--idle-secs 1500` after `dd` had already
returned** — the identical harness artefact I had retracted three other numbers for hours earlier,
re-committed on the next variable. Fixed in the `rpi4-flash-sd` skill (which now also sizes
`--idle-secs` to the transfer), `docs/done/WEEK-2026-W38.md`, `docs/misc/2026-09-19-sdma-write-…`,
and memory. Surviving difference: coreutils `dd` self-reports a rate, this busybox build cannot
(`FEATURE_DD_THIRD_STATUS_LINE` off).

⏸ **Owner question (raised 2026-09-20): should busybox leave the base image?** The census:

- **`/bin` precedes `/usr/bin`**, so busybox **1.27.2 (2017)** shadows coreutils 9.5 for **22 names**
  — `base64 cat chmod cp date dd echo false kill link ln ls mkdir mv pwd rm rmdir sleep stat stty
  touch true`. A third layer exists: **psh resolves its own builtins before PATH**, so `ls` is psh's
  at `(psh)%`, busybox's under bash, and coreutils only by full path.
- **busybox is NOT load-bearing on Pi 4.** plo starts every driver from the syspage and
  `/etc/rc.psh` runs `bind`/`dummyfs`/`posixsrv`/`psh`. The busybox `/etc/rc` + `/etc/inittab` are
  unused ports-layer legacy, and **no script in `/bin`, `/sbin` or `/usr/bin` has a `#!/bin/sh`
  shebang**.
- It does uniquely supply **15 names**: `sh/ash, sed, grep/egrep/fgrep, tar, gzip/gunzip/zcat, vi,
  ed, login, usleep`. (`awk`, `find`, `xargs`, `diff`, `cmp` are already GNU in `/usr/bin`.)
- ⓘ **Not a problem, checked:** the 37 busybox names are hardlinks and the **SD image preserves
  them** (inode 15, `Links: 61`). No image bloat. Only the NFS *export* staging copy breaks them
  (~24 MB) — a host-side `rsync -aH` fix.

✅ **And the real reason coreutils looked inferior was our bug.** `dd: cannot fstat '/dev/mmcblk0':
Function not implemented` — `sdstorage_srv.c` handled `mtOpen/mtRead/mtWrite/mtGetAttr/mtMount` but
**not `mtStat`**, so every `stat()` of the card hit `default: -ENOSYS`. busybox worked only because
its `dd` does not stat first. Fixed (devices `a2b0c75` + include fix) — building, then gating.

⛔ **`scripts/syntax-check.sh` could not fail for the file you asked about.** `make -n <obj>` prints
the recipes for *every* prerequisite in that directory; the script took `grep -m1`, i.e. the **first**
gcc line, so requesting `sdstorage_srv.c` compiled **`sdcard.c`** instead and reported CLEAN for a
file the real build rejects. Found by running a negative control after the real build failed on the
error the check had passed. Now matches the compile line by source name and refuses to guess if the
match is not unique; verified in **both** directions plus a second repo.

## 1c. 2026-09-20 — USB mass storage: the driver already existed

⏭ **Next big thing (owner, 2026-09-20): mount USB sticks in a running system, hot-plug and
unplug. No booting from them.**

✅ **`stat()`/`fstat()` on `/dev/mmcblk0` FIXED and HW-verified** (devices `a2b0c75`+). The
server answered `mtStat` with the switch default `-ENOSYS`, so coreutils `dd`/`cat`/`cp` failed
on a healthy card with `cannot fstat … Function not implemented`; busybox worked only because its
`dd` does not stat first. Gate (`mtstat`): `stat` returns, coreutils `dd` read 8 MiB at
**28.4 MB/s**, `cmp` against busybox's copy **silent = byte-identical**, 0 faults. ⓘ A *path*
`stat` still reports `character special file` / size 0 — that is **devfs** answering, a separate
and smaller gap; the `fstat`-on-an-open-fd path is what was broken and is what is fixed.

★ **The USB mass-storage driver has been in this tree all along** — `devices/storage/umass/`,
BOT/SCSI, filter `08/06/50`, exactly matching the stick. It was only ever built for **ia32**.
Wired up for Pi 4 (devices + build `2026-09-20`):

- `libusbdrv-umass` into `DEFAULT_COMPONENTS` **and** `USB_HOSTDRV_LIBS` — both, because the
  component alone builds a lib nothing links, and the device then enumerates with no driver
  bound (the #126 mouse shape).
- `UMASS_MOUNT_EXT2` on for aarch64 (libext2 already backs the SD root).
- Two `-Werror=format` fixes: `%lld` against an `off_t` that is `long int` on this ABI. Most
  likely *why* it stayed ia32-only.
- `mtStat` added there too, same gap, same fix.

✅ **Gated on HW with nothing plugged (`umasswire`): 0 faults, `kbd0` + `mouse0` still enumerate,
clean `ls /dev`.** So linking it into the daemon costs nothing. Manifest
`2026-09-20-w39-mtstat-and-umass-wired.md`. ⏸ **Untested until the stick goes in.**

⚠ **Two limits to expect, both recorded before the first plug:** `_umass_check()` reads the MBR
and takes **partition 0 only**, so the stick's `sda2` will be invisible; and **hot-UNplug of a
mounted volume has never been exercised** on this port.

🔌 **The test stick is prepared and waiting** (host `/dev/sda`): SanDisk Ultra `0781:5581`,
29.45 GiB, erased, MBR, `sda1` = 2 GiB ext2 **1 KiB blocks** (`PHXTEST1` — byte-for-byte the
geometry Phoenix mounts on every SD boot) and `sda2` = 27.5 GiB ext2 **4 KiB blocks**
(`PHXDATA` — never mounted by this port). Block size is the only deliberate difference.
Each carries `/phoenix/SHA256SUMS` over five files incl. a 17 MiB one that crosses the
double-indirect boundary, so a mount is graded **by content, not by rc**. Full detail:
[`2026-09-20-usb-mass-storage-test-stick.md`](../misc/2026-09-20-usb-mass-storage-test-stick.md).
⛔ **`/dev/sda` on this host is that stick, not the SD card** — the `rpi4-run` skill said the
opposite and has been corrected.

## 1d. USB mass storage — bulk WORKS; mount is the open item

**★ OWNER GOAL (2026-09-20):** the SanDisk stick in the Pi fully usable — enumerate, block
device, mount, read+write, hot-plug/unplug — **and its read/write throughput MEASURED**. Work
autonomously (cron `a93931c8`, every 2 h).

✅ **xHCI bulk endpoints implemented and HW-PROVEN** (devices `3330b1d`). The HCD had grown one
scenario at a time and knew only control + interrupt-IN; bulk was not *refused*, it was
**unrepresented**, so `usb_open()` handed back a valid pipe the hardware had never configured and
every transfer failed on a healthy device. Added EP types 2/6, per-endpoint pipe storage (a slot
had ONE `interruptPriv`; mass storage needs bulk IN and OUT at once), and a submit path that
splits a buffer into physically contiguous runs — the interrupt path never needed that because its
buffers are a few bytes, but a kilobyte bulk buffer can straddle non-adjacent pages.
On HW: `xhci: bulk-OUT pipe ready slot=2 ep=4` / `bulk-IN … ep=3`, and
`umass: New USB Mass Storage device: /dev/umass0 sectors: 4194304` — exactly `PHXTEST1`, so the
MBR read and SCSI READ CAPACITY/READ(10) are all correct. Reads verified.

★ **The umass driver already existed upstream** (ia32-only) — wired up for aarch64, plus a
`-Werror=format` fix (`%lld` vs an `off_t` that is `long int` here, most likely why it stayed
ia32-only) and an `mtStat` handler.

🐞 **Three umass bugs found and fixed on the way:**
1. Hosted in-daemon, `ops.init()` gets NULL args and the NULL path set `mount_root = true` — it
   tried to mount the first stick anyone plugs in **as the system root**, contradicting the
   driver's own `-r` opt-in default.
2. `umass_mountFromDev()` set `part.fs` *before* attempting the mount and never cleared it on
   failure. `part.fs` **is** the "already mounted" flag, so one failed attempt made the device
   permanently unmountable, reporting `-EEXIST` — which reads as "busy", not "the first attempt
   failed".
3. `UMASS_MOUNT_EXT2` was gated on `$(filter ia32 aarch64,$(TARGET_FAMILY))`, and **TARGET_FAMILY
   here is `aarch64a72`** — the gate silently matched nothing.

✅✅ **THE STICK WORKS — mount, read and write, all HW-verified, 0 faults.**
`mount rc=0`, and graded **by content**: `sha256sum -c SHA256SUMS` → **5/5 OK, rc=0**, including
`big.bin` (17 MiB, well into the double-indirect region) and the 4-deep `deep/a/b/c/leaf.txt`.
Writes verified too: 8 MiB written and read back, plus a create/read/delete round-trip.
Manifest `2026-09-20-w39-usb-stick-working.md`.

🐞 **Two more umass/xhci bugs found getting there, each mis-pointing at the previous layer:**
4. `UMASS_MOUNT_EXT2` enabled the ext2 code but **nothing linked libext2 into the usb daemon** —
   `UMASS_LIBS` in umass's Makefile applies to the standalone binary, not to the
   `libusbdrv-umass.a` hosted in the daemon. Added `USB_HOSTDRV_DEP_LIBS` so a hosted driver can
   bring its own non-USB dependencies (usb `Makefile`, ordered after the driver because static
   archives resolve left to right).
5. ★ **umass silently truncated every I/O to its 4 KiB bounce buffer** and returned the truncated
   count as if it were the whole transfer. Measured: `512→512, 4096→4096, 8192→4096, 16384→4096`.
   libext2 reads more than 4 KiB while mounting, saw a short read, and reported **-EIO** — an error
   that points at the device rather than at the driver that shortened the request. Now loops at up
   to 64 KiB per SCSI command.

⚡ **Completion latency fixed — and the first attempt made it worse, which is the interesting part.**
Bulk completions now reaped by the submitting thread instead of the 1 ms roothub poll. The first
version left the poller polling bulk **too**, so both consumers raced for one event and the loser
blocked until its own timeout: every transfer became a multi-second stall, **slower than the poll it
replaced** (the test that had checksummed three files got through none). Fixed by giving each
endpoint type one owner — poller keeps interrupt-IN, the submitting thread owns bulk and must
therefore complete it on every path, including `-ETIMEDOUT`.

📊 **THROUGHPUT MEASURED — GOAL ITEM MET** (coreutils `dd`'s own reported rate, never harness
wall-clock; writes use `conv=fsync` so the flush is inside the number, and `cksum` verifies what
landed):

| path | before the poll fix | **after** |
|---|---|---|
| **raw `/dev/umass0`, bs=1M** | 14.7 MB/s | **29.3 MB/s** |
| raw, bs=64k | 14.5 MB/s | **28.2 MB/s** |
| raw, bs=4k | 1.3 MB/s | **8.1 MB/s** |
| **ext2 read** (17 MiB, `PHXTEST1`, 1 KiB blocks) | 320 kB/s | **3.0 MB/s** |
| **ext2 write** (16 MiB, `conv=fsync`) | 3.2 MB/s | **5.4 MB/s** |

**29.3 MB/s is essentially USB 2.0 High-Speed line rate** (the stick negotiated 480 Mbps behind the
Pi's hub). ⚡ The 2–6× jump came from one thing: `xhci_eventAwait` polled the event ring with a flat
`usleep(1000)` while the controller finishes a transfer in tens of microseconds. One SCSI command is
three transfers, so it cost ~3 ms per command — **fixed cost, not bandwidth**, which the raw
block-size sweep made unambiguous (4 KiB → 3.1 ms/command vs 64 KiB → 4.4 ms). Now 20 µs for the
first 4 ms, then back off.

↩ An earlier write figure of 3.1 MB/s was taken with a `sync` that had silently failed (psh's
`/bin/sync` needs a device argument) and could have been measuring cache. It happened to be close to
right, but it was not evidence when quoted.

⏭ **ext2 read is still 10× below the raw device**, and the cause is structural: umass has **no block
cache at all**, where `bcm2711-emmc` runs libcache with 128 KiB sectors. With 1 KiB ext2 blocks that
is ~17 400 SCSI commands for a 17 MiB file. Adding libcache is the next lever, well-precedented here.

🐞✅ **Hot-unplug of a MOUNTED stick was a use-after-free — fixed, but NOT hardware-tested.**
`_umass_devFree` freed the device struct while `umass_fsthr` was still blocked in `msgRecv` on a
stack **inside that struct**, never unmounted the filesystem and never destroyed the partition port.
Stopping the thread needed an explicit handshake, not just a `portDestroy`: it only ever left on
`mtUmount` and retried `msgRecv` forever on error. ⚠ Found by inspection — the stick cannot be
physically unplugged while the owner is away.

✅✅ **RELEASING A MOUNTED DEVICE NOW WORKS — full lifecycle proven on hardware.**
Three consecutive `mount → sha256sum -c → umount` cycles, all
`mount=0 sha256_rc=0 files_ok=5 umount=0`, then a **write round-trip across a umount**: a file and a
16 MiB `conv=fsync` write both survived umount+remount with a matching `cksum`. 0 faults.

★ **The hang was a self-deadlock, and it had two levels.** `umass_read`/`umass_write` take
`umass_common.lock`; the mtUmount handler held that lock across a synchronous `msgSend` whose
completion path runs `umass_poolthr → fs->unmount() → libext2 flush → umass_write()`. The driver
blocked against itself. Fixed by not holding the lock across the round-trip — and then the identical
bug was found one level up in `umass_handleDeletion`, which held it across `_umass_devFree`, so
**every unplug of a mounted stick would have deadlocked too**. That now unlinks under the lock and
tears down outside it.

ⓘ **The design was right all along and the machinery already existed** — `umass_poolthr` performs
the `fs->unmount()` and `umass_fsthr` exits on an `mtUmount` to its **own** port. libphoenix sends it
to the **device** port, so it only ever needed bridging. Two dead ends are recorded in the code so
they are not retried: `portDestroy()` cannot wake a thread blocked *inside* `msgRecv`, and
`_umass_partUnmount` must never be called holding `umass_common.lock`.

ⓘ Also fixed on the way, each a "server answers only the attribute its own code path needed" gap:
**`mtStat`**, **`atMode`** (umass *and* `bcm2711-emmc`), **`mtMountPoint`** + the mountpoint oid that
`mount()` passes and umass was discarding. ⚠ `umount` **by mountpoint** is still an unimplemented
TODO in libphoenix (`sys/mount.c:154` → `ENOTSUP`); only `umount /dev/umass0` works.

⚠ **Hot-plug/unplug remain physically untested** — the stick cannot be plugged or pulled while the
owner is away. The unplug path now shares the exact teardown proven through `umount()`, which is the
strongest evidence available without hands on the hardware.

✅ **MULTI-PARTITION WORKS.** `_umass_check()` took MBR entry 0 and discarded the rest; it now
walks all four and creates a node per non-empty one. `/dev/umass0` (`PHXTEST1`) and `/dev/umass1`
(`PHXDATA`) both appear and both mount, 5/5 checksums on umass0. A stick has one set of bulk pipes
and one CBW tag counter, so partitions share them through a new `dev->owner` and stay serialised;
only the owner destroys the lock. `mbr.h` gained `PENTRY_EMPTY`/`MBR_PARTITIONS`.
⏭ umass1's content check did not run in that cycle (the `[ -d ]` guard took the false branch) — re-run
it; the mount itself succeeded and listed `lost+found` + `phoenix`.

★★★ **OWNER GOAL (2026-09-20): max out transfer speed. ROOT CAUSE FOUND, fix REVERTED, bench
restored.**

**We were switching the USB 3 link off ourselves, every boot.** `PORTSC` bit 1 (PED) is **RW1CS —
writing 1 DISABLES the port** — and `SetPortFeature(PORT_POWER)` did `portsc | PP`, feeding a live
SuperSpeed port's own `PED=1` straight back at it:

```
probe:                   PORTSC=0x00281203  ccs=1 ped=1 speed=4 (SuperSpeed, U0)
SetPortFeature(1..5, 8)  <- PORT_POWER
GetPortStatus(2):        PORTSC=0x00000280  ccs=0
```

↩ **Fixed, measured, and REVERTED — because the fix alone makes the board worse.** With port 2
staying up, the stick correctly stopped presenting its USB 2 personality (a SuperSpeed device does
not use its USB 2 pins), port 1's reset came back **speed=1 with `Fail to get device descriptor`**,
and **nothing enumerated at all** — no hub, no stick, no keyboard, no mouse.

★ **The finding that matters: our USB 2 enumeration has only ever worked BECAUSE we were tearing the
SuperSpeed link down.** Every device reaches us through the VL805's USB 2 hub, which only happens
while the SS side is dead. **Half of USB 3 support is worse than none**, so the PORTSC fix must land
*together* with SS enumeration as one gated change: slot speed 4, route string to the SS root port,
ep0 = 512 (`bMaxPacketSize0` is an **exponent**, 9, not a byte count), and the SS Endpoint Companion
`bMaxBurst` into the endpoint context. ⚠ **Do not re-try the PORTSC fix on its own.**

✅ **Bench verified restored** after the revert: hub, SanDisk, `/dev/umass0` + `/dev/umass1`, mouse,
keyboard, 0 faults. Kept: the Supported Protocol / port-state diagnostic that made all of this
visible. Full detail: [`2026-09-20-usb3-superspeed-gap.md`](../misc/2026-09-20-usb3-superspeed-gap.md).

✅✅ **BLOCK CACHE LANDED — ext2 reads 5.1× faster, correctness intact.**

| | before | **after** |
|---|---|---|
| **ext2 read** (17 MiB, `PHXTEST1`) | 2.9 MB/s | **14.7 MB/s** |
| ext2 read, second pass (warm) | — | 14.8 MB/s |
| ext2 write (`conv=fsync`) | 5.4 MB/s | 4.6 MB/s |
| raw `/dev/umass0` (reference) | 29.3 MB/s | 27.4 MB/s |
| `sha256sum -c` | 5/5 OK | **5/5 OK** |

ext2 read went from **10% of the raw device to 54%**. umass had no cache at all where
`bcm2711-emmc` has run libcache with 128 KiB sectors for years; each partition now gets a libcache
with the line sized to `UMASS_MAX_IO` (64 KiB), so one miss is one SCSI command. Both the filesystem
callbacks and the raw `/dev/umassN` path go through it, so the two views cannot disagree.
**WRITE_THROUGH deliberately** — this is removable media and a write-back cache would hold data a
yanked stick never receives; the small write regression (5.4 → 4.6) is the read-modify-write on
partial lines and is the right trade. 0 faults.

⏭ The residual gap to raw is that libcache has no read-ahead: each new line is a separate blocking
64 KiB command with nothing overlapping it.

🐞✅ **`/dev/umass1`'s wrong contents ROOT-CAUSED: a 32-bit overflow in ext2, and it is not
USB-specific.** `ext2_block_read/write` computed the device offset as `bno * fs->blocksz` with both
operands `uint32_t`, so **the multiply wrapped at 4 GiB** before being widened to the `off_t` the
callback takes. Every ext2 filesystem over 4 GiB read and wrote the wrong place, silently.
The 2 GiB `PHXTEST1` was perfect because its highest offset is `0x80000000` — it just fits; the
27.5 GiB `PHXDATA` listed its root directory (low blocks) while a top-level directory in it would not
stat as a directory, the Orlov allocator having put that inode past the wrap. Fixed by casting before
multiplying (filesystems `ext2/block.c`). ⓘ **The SD card would hit this too** with a root over
4 GiB; today's is ~1 GB.

⏳ **umass1 still unverified — a NEW crash blocks it.** With the offset fix in, touching `/dev/umass1`
raises `Exception #32: Instruction Abort (EL0)`, `pc=far=0x46fa3c`, `esr=0x82000007` (level-3
translation fault on an **instruction** fetch), twice, with `/umass1` on the faulting stack. That is a
**userspace demand-paging** fault, not the driver's data path — the same family as the
`libc-uninit-main` / "page reads back zero" row in KNOWN-ISSUES, and the binaries live on NFS, not on
the stick. ⚠ `umass0` is completely unaffected (14.8 MB/s, 5/5 checksums), so the bench remains
usable; investigating this is its own track.

★★★★★ **USB 3 SUPERSPEED IS RUNNING — raw throughput DOUBLED.** (2026-09-21)

```
usb: New device: 0781:5581 SanDisk, SanDisk Ultra (3, 00000021)
xhci: bulk-OUT pipe ready slot=2 ep=4 maxpkt=1024 burst=4
xhci: bulk-IN  pipe ready slot=2 ep=3 maxpkt=1024 burst=4
```
**maxpkt 1024** = SuperSpeed bulk (HS is 512) · **burst=4** = `bMaxBurst` from the SS Endpoint
Companion reaching the endpoint context · location **`00000021` = ROOT PORT 2**, i.e. the stick is on
the SuperSpeed root port directly, no longer behind the VL805's USB 2 hub. That also settles the open
question about port 2: it is the **stick**, not the external hub's SS personality.

| | USB 2 (before) | **USB 3 (now)** |
|---|---|---|
| **raw `/dev/umass0`, bs=1M** | 29.3 MB/s | **59.1 MB/s** |
| raw, bs=64k | 28.2 MB/s | **54.9 MB/s** |
| raw, bs=4k | 8.1 MB/s | **27.4 MB/s** |
| **ext2 read** (17 MiB) | 14.8 MB/s | **21.9 MB/s** |
| ext2 write (`conv=fsync`) | 4.6 MB/s | 4.7 MB/s |

Hub, keyboard and mouse still enumerate over USB 2; mount rc=0, **`sha256sum -c` 5/5**, umount rc=0,
**0 faults**. Manifest `2026-09-21-w39-usb3-superspeed.md`.

ⓘ **Landed as a gated unit, and the gate-off A/B was run first**: with
`XHCI_SUPERSPEED_ENUM_READY 0` the whole SS code base is a verified no-op, reproducing the previous
bench exactly. That is what made flipping it safe, after the earlier isolated PORTSC fix broke all
enumeration.

★ **The framework had no SuperSpeed concept at all** (`phoenix-rtos-usb` `877cace`) — four silent
gaps, each turning a healthy SS device into a plausible-looking USB 2 one: `enum usb_speed` stopped
at high-speed; no SS bit in the port-status word; `bMaxPacketSize0` used as a byte count when on SS
it is an **exponent** (9 → 512); and ★ an SS config **interleaves a 6-byte Endpoint Companion after
every endpoint descriptor**, while the config parser treated endpoints as a contiguous array — so a
mass-storage device's **bulk-OUT pipe would never have been found** even once enumeration succeeded.
⊕ Also fixed: `xhci_slotForDev` mapped every root-port device to `slots[0]`, so a second root-port
device's `GET_DESCRIPTOR` ran down the first device's ep0 ring — the bogus duplicate `2109:3431` hub
seen earlier.

✅✅ **`/dev/umass1` FULLY WORKS, and 4 KiB blocks are nearly 2x faster than 1 KiB.** (2026-09-21)
`mount rc=0` · `phoenix` now stats as a **directory** (was "Not a directory") · all files correct ·
**`sha256sum -c` 5/5 OK** · `umount rc=0` · **0 faults**.

| ext2 read, 17 MiB | rate |
|---|---|
| `umass0` — PHXTEST1, **1 KiB** blocks | 21.9 MB/s |
| **`umass1` — PHXDATA, 4 KiB blocks** | **42.6 MB/s** |
| raw device (reference) | 59.1 MB/s |

✅ **The ext2 >4 GiB offset fix is confirmed on hardware** — that was the whole cause of umass1's
wrong contents.
↩ **The EL0 Instruction Abort did NOT reproduce** (0 faults over the full mount + stat + listing +
5-file checksum + 17 MiB read). So it was **intermittent and not caused by the ext2 fix** — it
belongs to the existing userspace demand-paging family (`libc-uninit-main` / "page reads back zero"
in KNOWN-ISSUES), which is consistent with the faulting registers holding path strings
(`x7="/umass1"`, `x12="/umass0/"`, `x13="phoenix"`) rather than driver state.

★ **This also settles where the remaining ext2 gap lives: per-block CPU cost, not the bus.**
Raising the cache line cannot help — at 59 MB/s and a 0.08 ms fixed cost, one 64 KiB line already
costs 1.19 ms of device time and a 128 KiB line would cost 2.30 ms for twice the data, i.e. the same
total. What changed between 21.9 and 42.6 MB/s is **4x fewer filesystem blocks** (and far less
indirect-block traversal) for the same file. ⏭ So the levers are libext2's per-block path and
libcache's per-call cost — not bigger transfers.

📊 **WRITE AMPLIFICATION MEASURED, not assumed** (2026-09-21). umass now counts bytes asked for vs
bytes actually sent and prints the ratio at unmount:

| | asked | device | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| `umass0`, 16 MiB bulk write (1 KiB blocks) | 17.2 MB | 45.6 MB | **2.65x** |
| `umass1`, small metadata writes (4 KiB blocks) | 122 kB | 2.49 MB | **20.4x** |

Cause: libcache's WRITE_THROUGH calls `cache_flushLine`, which writes the **whole** `lineSize`
(64 KiB) regardless of how few bytes changed. Bulk writes batch enough to keep it to 2.65x; scattered
metadata writes pay ~20x.

★ **But amplification is NOT the whole story, and the arithmetic says so**: 16 MiB at 2.8 MB/s took
5.7 s while the device received 45.6 MB — i.e. **~8 MB/s device-side**, against ~59 MB/s for reads.
So the stick's own NAND write rate is the larger factor, and removing all amplification would take
the user-visible rate to roughly **7.4 MB/s**, not to read speed. ⏭ Worth doing (a ranged
write-through flush in libcache would also help the SD card), but it is a 2.6x, not a 20x.

✅ **`umass1` file creation works** — `touch rc=0`, shell redirect rc=0, `df` shows 28G/504M used. The
earlier `EINVAL` was transient, not a real limitation.

🐞 **`umass1` corruption: REAL but NOT on the normal path. Narrowed hard, two of my own readings
retracted.** The original damage was genuine — `stat -c '%F'` read `lost+found` as
`regular empty file` across two boots, where it had been `drwx------ 16384`. But:

↩ **Retraction 1 — "libext2 hands out an in-use inode" was WRONG.** Instrumenting
`ext2_inode_create` exonerated it: `group=0 ino=12 bmpBlk=66 bmp[0]=000007ff` on a clean filesystem
is exactly right (inodes 1..11 used, correctly returns 12).
↩ **Retraction 2 — "creating a file corrupted data.bin" was WRONG, and for an embarrassing reason.**
The checksum count went to 0 because **`sha256sum` CRASHED**: `Data Abort (EL0) far=0x30
esr=0x92000007`, which project memory already records as `fwrite()` on a NULL `FILE*`
(`libc-uninit-main`, 0x30 = `FILE.lock`). I read a dead process as a data fault — the mirror of the
"a PASS where nothing ran is vacuously true" trap.

✅ **Clean-filesystem create is SAFE, proven with two independent tools.** A known-good 1 GiB
4 KiB-block ext2 was `dd`'d onto the partition **from the Pi itself**, then one file created:
`lost+found` still a `directory`, new file at inode 12, and `data.bin`'s sha256 **identical to the
host-generated value** (`c95413d29bea…868a44`), 0 faults.

⏭ **So the corruption needs the EINVAL episode, which is now the whole remaining lead:** in
`wrprobe`, three `open(O_CREAT)` failed with EINVAL **after heavy write traffic on `umass0` in the
same boot**; in `wramp` the same call succeeded once `umass0` had been unmounted. Reproduce that
deliberately — heavy `umass0` traffic, then create on `umass1` — and the failed-create rollback path
is where to look.

📈 **Also corrected: raw device WRITE is 18.0 MB/s, not ~8.** Measured directly by the restore
(`1073741824 bytes … 59.5 s, 18.0 MB/s`). My earlier "the device only absorbs ~8 MB/s so NAND
dominates" came from dividing amplified bytes by wall-clock in a run with other overheads — it was
an inference, not a measurement, and it understated the headroom. **Reducing the 2.65x write
amplification is therefore worth more than I said.**

⏭ **Also open:** ext2 read is 10× below the raw device because umass has **no block cache**
(`bcm2711-emmc` runs libcache with 128 KiB sectors); and `_umass_check()` reads MBR entry 0 only, so
the 4 KiB-block `PHXDATA` partition stays invisible — which is the comparison the stick was
deliberately formatted for.

## 1f. 2026-09-21 — ✅ ROOT CAUSE FOUND AND FIXED: libext2 freed live inodes

🐞➡✅ **`_ext2_obj_create()` released an inode number it did not allocate.** It is called two ways:
with `inode == NULL` to allocate, and from `ext2_obj_get()` with an *existing* inode to pull it into
the object cache. In the second case `ino = pino` names a **live file**, but the error path ran
`free(inode); ext2_inode_destroy(fs, ino, mode);` unconditionally. Any failure in that block — the
`MAX_OBJECTS` (512) LRU eviction returning `-ENOENT` when every object is held, `malloc`,
`mutexCreate` — marked an in-use inode free.

The two inodes pulled into the cache most often are **root (2)** and **`lost+found` (11)** — exactly
the bits observed missing: group 0's inode bitmap read `0x000003fd` where a healthy filesystem reads
`0x000007ff`. Once inode 2 was free the allocator handed the **root directory** to the next
regular-file create, the object layer refused it, and **every** create returned `EINVAL` — while
`mkdir` still worked, because Orlov places directories in another group. One defect explains the
`lost+found` damage, the EINVAL, and the intermittency (it needs object-cache pressure).

Fix in `phoenix-rtos-filesystems` `ccd11e4`: release the number only when this call allocated it
(`free(inode)` stays unconditional — the object takes ownership on success). Also hardened
`ext2_inode_destroy()`, which rejected only inode 1, to refuse the whole reserved range 1..10;
`ext2_inode_sync()` deliberately untouched, since syncing root is legitimate.

✅ **HW-verified** on a freshly restored 1 GiB 4 KiB-block ext2. Before: every create `EINVAL`,
allocator printing `ino=2 bmp[0]=000003fd`. After: `touch`, shell redirect, `dd` and `mkdir` all
`rc=0`, allocator prints **`ino=12 bmp[0]=000007ff`**, `lost+found` still a directory, reference
`data.bin` sha256 unchanged (`c95413d29beacf15`).

↩ **Retraction 3 — "the EINVAL reproduces deterministically" was WRONG.** It was 1 reproduction in 2
valid runs; a third run was clean. It is pressure-dependent, which is consistent with the LRU
eviction trigger found above.
↩ **Retraction 4 — "heavy `umass0` traffic is required" was WRONG.** The EINVAL reproduced on a
pristine filesystem with `umass0` **never mounted**, and none of the three umass markers fired. The
two-partition framing was a coincidence of how the first runs were ordered; the defect is in
libext2 and has nothing to do with the block device.

⚠ **Process note (mine).** I read a *partial* log from a still-running cycle, drew a conclusion, and
launched a second cycle on top of it — breaking the one-cycle-at-a-time rule and killing both runs
(`device reports readiness to read but returned no data`). Confirm a cycle has exited before reading
its log as final.

🔻 **Still to do here:** remove the TEMPORARY instrumentation from `ext2/inode.c` and `umass.c`
(hypothesis now resolved), and re-run the object-cache pressure test (800 creates) to completion.

## 1g. 2026-09-21 — ✅ SECOND ROOT CAUSE: every ext2 block > 1 KiB was off by one

🐞➡✅ **libext2 reserved bitmap bit N and handed the file block N+1.** A group's block bitmap is
indexed from the filesystem's **first data block**, which ext2 defines as **1 for 1 KiB blocks and 0
for every larger block size**. libext2's bitmap helpers take a 1-based bit index, and the block
number was computed as `group * groupBlocks + offset` — algebraically correct **only** when
`s_first_data_block == 1`.

Inside a free run this is invisible: each allocation reserves the previous block. **At the end of a
free run it hands out the first block of the next, USED run.** Group 0's free runs
(`585-623, 672-703, 896-1023, 1280-1535, 3072-…`) are each followed by a `data.bin` extent, so a
large write clobbered exactly **624, 704, 1024, 1536** — the first block of every extent, and
nothing else. That is why a 4 MiB and a 64 MiB write produced the *same* bad checksum.

`e2fsck -fn` on a pre-fix read-back is unambiguous:

```
Multiply-claimed block(s) in inode 12:     624 704 1024 1536   <- speed.bin
Multiply-claimed block(s) in inode 49154:  624 704 1024 1536   <- phoenix/data.bin
Block bitmap differences: -585 -672 -896 -1280 -3072 +19018
```

The `-585 -672 -896 -1280 -3072` are the first block of each **free** run: reserved, never handed
out. Both halves of the off-by-one, in one report.

**Scope: every ext2 with a block size above 1 KiB** — which is mke2fs's default for anything but a
tiny volume. It was never seen here because the SD rootfs is built `mke2fs -b 1024`
(`scripts/build-rpi4b-rootfs-ext2.sh`), so `fstBlock == 1` and the old arithmetic was accidentally
right. The USB stick was deliberately formatted with 4 KiB blocks, which is what exposed it.

Fix: one conversion pair (`ext2_blockToGroup` / `ext2_blockToOffs` / `ext2_blockFromOffs`) in
`block.c`, used by all six mapping sites including the free path. For `fstBlock == 1` they reduce
symbol-for-symbol to the old expressions, so **the SD lane is bit-for-bit unaffected**.

↩ **Retraction 5 — §1g's first version was wrong, and I had flagged the reason myself.** It said
"not the medium: the block bitmap is byte-identical and blocks 624..631 unchanged". Those dumps came
from the run whose **restore stopped at 255 MiB of 1 GiB without an error line**. On a full restore,
block 65 *does* change and 624 *is* zeroed. The `ext2_obj_t.ind[]`-cache hypothesis built on that
reading is dead — the indirect block was never corrupted; `data.bin`'s own data block was.

⚠ **Two tooling traps that produced those false readings**, both worth remembering:
`dd if=/dev/umass1 skip=<large>` fails with `cannot fstat` and leaves a **0-byte** file — check dump
sizes. And a restore can stop early while still printing a normal `copied` line — confirm
`1073741824 bytes`.

⏭ **Open:** anything Phoenix wrote to a >1 KiB ext2 *before* this fix carries a shifted bitmap —
`e2fsck` or reformat `umass0`/PHXDATA before trusting any measurement on them, or the next
"corruption" is a fossil.

## 1i. 2026-09-21 — ✅ THIRD root cause: unmount deleted the files it had cached

🐞➡✅ `libext2_unmount()` → `ext2_objs_destroy()` ran `_ext2_obj_destroy()` over **every** object left
in the tree, and that frees the **on-disk inode**; `ignoreSync` only suppresses the error it
returns. So every unmount marked free the inode of everything still cached — root, `lost+found`, and
anything touched since the mount. Invisible until the next mount, because the directory entries and
the data survive. `e2fsck` on a read-back of a filesystem that was merely mounted, written and
unmounted:

```
Inode bitmap differences:  +(11--13)
Free inodes count wrong for group #6 (8193, counted=8189)
Directories count wrong for group #6 (65535, counted=1)     <- unsigned underflow
```

**This is what turned `lost+found` into a 0-byte file** — the original symptom, three bugs ago. Fix
(`269306a`): release the in-memory object; free the inode only when `links == 0`, the
unlinked-while-open case. ⚠ **Not 4 KiB-specific** — `obj.c` has no block-size terms, so the 1 KiB SD
root is equally affected; it has not bitten there only because nothing unmounts that root, but
`ext2_objs_destroy()` is also the mount-failure cleanup path. ✅ Verified: `e2fsck` on a 1 GiB
read-back now reports **no inode-bitmap, no block-bitmap and no group-count errors**.

🐞➡ **Fourth, smaller: `i_blocks` was never maintained on the bulk write path.** `ext2_block_syncone()`
accounted for it, `ext2_block_sync()` (what `dd` and every buffered write use) did not, and indirect
blocks were not counted at all — so every file we created reported `i_blocks == 0` and `stat`/`du`
read 0 blocks. Both sites added; the truncate path already decremented correctly. In the cycle now.

### Score after today
Four defects, one symptom. `lost+found` corruption = §1i. Every-create-EINVAL = §1f. Silent
overwrite of an existing file = §1g. Wrong `st_blocks` = this one. Data integrity on the stick is
now clean by `e2fsck` and by byte-compare, including an 8 MiB non-zero payload.

## 1j. 2026-09-21 — ✅ DELETE PATH CLOSED: e2fsck completely clean

Five defects, all fixed and HW-verified. **`e2fsck -fn` on a full 1 GiB read-back now reports NO
errors of any kind** — the first fully clean run.

| | defect | commit |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `rm` released **no blocks at all** — only `ext2_destroy()` truncated first | `8a35d91` |
| 2 | truncate freed an indirect block then **allocated a replacement** (walked forwards) | `282a46f` |
| 3 | the destroy path **could allocate at all** — `readind` creates on a zero pointer | `244f1cf` |
| 4 | a **parent** indirect was freed **before its children** (my own #2 exposed this) | `844198a` |
| 5 | `i_dtime` never set — fsck flags a freed inode with `dtime == 0` | `844198a` |

✅ **Measured (`df`, idle fs, 4 KiB blocks, both orderings):**
8 MiB (double indirect) **34188 → 25984**, 1 MiB (single indirect) **27012 → 25984**, baseline
**25984** — every block returned. Reference file byte-identical throughout.
Progression across the night: leak **4044 KiB → 4 KiB → 0**.

⚠ **Honest note:** #4 was *introduced* by #2. The backwards walk is required (a level may only be
released once empty), but the parent tests were written for a forward walk, where `offs[k] == 0`
means "first entry". Backwards it means "last entry visited" — true only for the deepest level, so
each level's test now includes every level below it.

### Where the USB stick stands
**Correctness: DONE.** Data integrity and space accounting both clean by `e2fsck` across
create / write / delete / umount / remount, plus byte-compare of a reference file.
**Next: PRIORITY 2 — speed** (ranged write-through flush in libcache).

## 1-gate3. ⚠ 2026-09-22 09:49 — showcase re-run on the CURRENT card: 6/6 render, but STK tripped the heap guard 5x

Re-ran the full gate because the card now carries changes the 05:32 run did not have (defect 21's
guard, the `sync` chain, the storage short read).

| app | rc | faults | frames |
|---|---|---|---|
| x, qspasm, q3, q2, vkq | 0 | **0** | 9420 / 8000 / 9408 / 5500 |
| **stk** | 0 | **5** | **1189** (vs 988 at 05:32) |

⚠ **The gate returned rc=1**, and the five are **not** crashes: `malloc: free() of a corrupt chunk
header -- leaking the block`, all from the **same caller**, resolved with `addr2line` to
**`operator delete(void*)`**, with `why = 5`, firing mid-gameplay after 268 frames. No `Exception`,
no `Data Abort`; STK ran to completion and rendered *more* frames than the previous run.

That is the signature of the **known, contained allocator defect**
(`project_allocator_double_free`: guards fire, block is leaked, run continues; callers land in
Bullet teardown). ⚠ **But it is the only one of the last TEN stk logs to show it** — the other nine
are 0 — so "known intermittent" is a hypothesis, not a conclusion, and I am not filing it as one.

### ➡ Did NOT reproduce. Two immediate re-runs clean.

| run | corrupt-chunk | Exception | frames |
|---|---|---|---|
| `gate-stk` 09:49 | **5** | 0 | 1189 |
| `stkrep1` 09:58 | **0** | 0 | 1651 |
| `stkrep2` 10:05 | **0** | 0 | 1687 |

Frames went *up* each time, and `stkrep2`'s final frame is a normally-rendering hacienda race —
kart, four opponents with lap times, minimap, speedometer, 32 KTris — checked by looking at it.

**What the evidence supports:** 1 occurrence in 3 runs on this card, 0 in the 9 before it, and the
documented defect is intermittent (the archive has 4 positive logs). **No evidence of a rate
change** — which is not the same as evidence of no change, and with n=3 I am not claiming the
stronger version. Today's storage work stays on the list of things to bisect against if it recurs.

**Verdict: the showcase renders 6/6 on the current card**, with one leaked block in one STK run
from the known contained allocator defect. The gate's rc=1 is correct and useful — it is the
detector doing its job on a real guard firing, not a false positive.

## 1-gate. ✅ 2026-09-22 05:32 — SIX-APP SHOWCASE GATE 6/6 ON THE SD LANE, VISUALLY VERIFIED

The regression test that actually matters for seven filesystem fixes: on the SD lane **every asset,
font, shader blob and pak is read through ext2**, whereas the netboot lane roots on NFS and
exercises none of it.

| app | rc | prompt | faults | frames | snaps |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| x | 0 | yes | **0** | — | 487 |
| qspasm | 0 | yes | **0** | 9413 | 450 |
| q3 | 0 | yes | **0** | 8205 | 444 |
| q2 | 0 | yes | **0** | 9442 | 451 |
| vkq | 0 | yes | **0** | 5515 | 457 |
| stk | 0 | yes | **0** | 988 | 466 |

vkQuake torches **PRESENT** — 13 of 13 frames at the reference viewpoint passed, 2 required,
`mae 3.4-3.6`. Netboot restored afterwards (`rootfs: nfsroot`).

★ **And I looked at the frames**, because the gate script says in as many words that a clean log
does not mean anything was drawn:

* **x** — window manager, dock, `xclock` with hands, a V3D GL window with shaded geometry, and
  `xbill`'s logo bitmap loaded off the card.
* **qspasm** — textured geometry, weapon model, HUD reading 83 health, 38 FPS.
* **q3** — full textured architecture, statues, bot models, **correct lightmaps**, HUD 100/123,
  bot chatter, 26 fps.
* **q2** — industrial base, weapon with hand, HUD 100 + health cross. **Not black**, which is this
  app's known failure mode.
* **vkq** — the QUAKE archway with **both torches visibly lit**, matching the scorer, 31 FPS.

move in **both** directions. The runs are time-boxed, so these are fps proxies with real
run-to-run spread; **nothing here supports a claim of speed-up or slow-down** and I am not making
one. What it establishes is that seven ext2 fixes and a libcache change did not break asset
loading on the lane that uses ext2.

## 1a. ★★★ 2026-09-22 — DEFECT 19: directories too. `unlink` never wrote its link counts to disk

The seven defects fixed today were **all in the file paths**. The directory paths had **no coverage
at all**, so I wrote one (`tools/libext2-hosttest/dirstress.c`) — and it failed on its first run,
**4 of 5 seeds at 1 KiB and at 4 KiB**:

```
Inode 2    ref count is 14, should be 13     (the root)
Inode 2056 ref count is 3,  should be 2      (a subdirectory)
```

Always exactly **one too high**. `ext2_unlink()` decrements correctly — `obj->inode->links--`, and
`dir->inode->links--` too when the entry is a subdirectory, since removing it takes its `..` away
from the parent — but it **never marked either object dirty and never synced the parent**, so the
decrements lived only in memory. `ext2_link()` does the mirror of this and marks the parent dirty
and syncs it.

★ **Why the file stress could never have found this:** a file whose link count reaches 0 is
destroyed, so its stale count never matters. Only a *surviving* directory keeps a wrong number on
disk. Same defect class as the sparse-file blind spot — the workload determines what you can see.

| `dirstress`, 300 random dir ops | 1 KiB | 4 KiB |
|---|---|---|
| before | 4/5 seeds fail | 4/5 seeds fail |
| `9e1ff6a` | **0/8** | **0/8** |

No regression: file stress 10 seeds x 800 ops clean at both block sizes, sequential harness PASSES
at both, truncate-tail leak test clean, `syntax-check.sh` CLEAN.

⚠ **Not yet on hardware** — committed after the image now under the showcase gate was built.

### ↩ And then my own fix had to be PARTLY BACKED OUT (`ba9149a`)

`9e1ff6a` marked **both** the parent directory *and* the unlinked object dirty. The parent half is
right and is what fixed the directory counts. The `obj` half made `ext2_objs_destroy()` sync that
object at teardown — and that path has an **object-lifetime bug**: ASan reports a
heap-use-after-free reading the `ext2_obj_t` that the same loop freed on an earlier pass.

I only found it because I went looking for the *next* untested area and wrote `linkstress.c` for
hard links and symlinks. `uaf.c` then narrowed it case by case:

| case | result with `9e1ff6a` |
|---|---|
| plain file unlink | clean |
| short symlink (target in the inode) | clean |
| long symlink (target in a block) | clean |
| rmdir | clean |
| **hard link, remove one name** | **use-after-free** |

So the exposure is exactly a file that **survives** the unlink with links > 0 — the case the dirty
flag existed to serve. Backed out to the validated half.

### ✅ ROOT-CAUSED AND CLOSED — `fs->root` was left dangling (`d4ff499`)

The workaround above lasted about twenty minutes. Dumping the object tree before teardown
(`uaf.c` now does this) showed only two objects, both valid — so the freed thing had to be
something a *pointer* still referenced, and it was:

**`ext2_inode_init()` and `ext2_inode_sync()` validate their `ino` against `fs->root->id`.** The
root is the lowest in-use inode, so `ext2_objs_destroy()` reaches it **first** and freed it while
`fs->root` still pointed at it. Every later object's sync then dereferenced freed memory.

Those checks are already written `(fs->root != NULL) && ...` — which is *precisely* this
situation: mid-teardown there is no root any more. Clearing `fs->root` before releasing that
object is the whole fix.

| `uaf.c` case | `9e1ff6a` | `d4ff499` |
|---|---|---|
| 1 file unlink | clean | clean |
| 2 short symlink | clean | clean |
| 3 long symlink | clean | clean |
| 4 rmdir | clean | clean |
| **5 hard link** | **use-after-free + wrong ref count** | **clean, `e2fsck` clean** |

With the lifetime fixed, defect 19's `obj` half is **re-applied and now behaves** — removing one
name of a hard-linked file writes the new count, and `e2fsck` no longer reports `Inode 12 ref
count is 2, should be 1`. Only survivors are marked dirty; an object reaching 0 links is destroyed
by `ext2_obj_put()`, and flagging that one asks the teardown path to sync an inode already gone.

Regression, all under ASan+UBSan: file stress **12 seeds x 800 ops** at 1 KiB and 4 KiB — 0
mismatches, 0 `e2fsck` errors, 0 SEGV; `dirstress` 0/10 seeds both sizes; `linkstress` all checks
pass with `e2fsck` clean both sizes; sequential harness PASS both sizes; `syntax-check.sh` CLEAN.

★ **Worth keeping:** the ASan stack named `ext2_inode_sync` and I read that as "the inode was
freed". It was the *root object* behind a validation the same function performs. **A
use-after-free names the code that touched the memory, not the code that owns the lifetime** —
dumping the live data structure is what turned it from a guess into one line.

⚠ I also hardened `ext2_objs_destroy()` while I was in there — it computed
`next = lib_rbNext(node)` **before** a removal that rebalances the tree, so the traversal could
return to a node it had already freed. It now re-queries the minimum each pass, frees only an
object that actually left the tree, and stops if the count did not move rather than spinning.
**That did not by itself fix case 5**, so it is hardening, not the cure, and I have not claimed
otherwise.

## 1-gate2. ✅ 2026-09-22 06:01 — defects 19+20 gated on hardware; NEW target tests pass

Card reflashed with `filesystems d4ff499` (1093+1 records, 1146849280 B exact, 12.5 MB/s), SD-booted
(`bcm2711-emmc;-r;/dev/mmcblk0p2:ext2`, no `nfs;/`), **0 faults**.

★ The regression tests added to `test_sparse` this morning (`5581259`) **pass on the real stack** —
driver, libcache and DMA included, which the host harness cannot reach:

```
trunc-holes: truncate to 0 survives                  PASS
trunc-holes: writable afterwards                     PASS
trunc-tail: truncate to an unaligned size            PASS
trunc-tail: no deleted content is readable           PASS
SPARSE TEST OK (0 failure(s))
```

⚠ **A clean SD boot cannot grade defect 19.** ext2 mounts a filesystem with wrong link counts
perfectly happily — that is the whole reason the defect survived until `dirstress`. So the gate
also ran a `mkdir`/`rmdir` workload on the card, and the **oracle is a separate netboot cycle**:
read `/dev/mmcblk0p2` (1026 MiB) back to the NFS export with busybox `dd` — coreutils `dd` cannot
`fstat` the device — and run `e2fsck -fn` host-side. Phoenix writes, Linux judges.

### ✅ THE ORACLE SAYS DEFECT 19 IS FIXED — zero ref-count errors

`e2fsck -fn` on the 1026 MiB read-back: **no "ref count is N, should be M" anywhere**, which was
defect 19's exact and only signature. Pass 4 clean. That is the gate.

### ⚠ But it found something else, and it is NOT a regression

e2fsck also reported three inodes "part of a corrupted orphan linked list", and the superblock
showed **2 fewer free inodes and 2 fewer free blocks** than the image the card was flashed from.

**Regression-checked first**, as always: I extracted p2 from the pristine `rpi4b-sd-2part.img` the
card was written from and ran `e2fsck` on it — **completely clean**. So the difference did appear
after Phoenix ran. Then I compared the two superblocks field by field:

| field | pristine | after Phoenix |
|---|---|---|
| `s_last_orphan` | **12** | **12** — unchanged, and already non-zero in the source image |
| free blocks | 238278 | 238276 |
| free inodes | 514767 | 514765 |

↩ So the orphan-list *head* is a **host-side image-build artefact**, not something Phoenix wrote —
libext2 declares `lastOrphan` and never touches it. And the three flagged inodes are exactly what
the gate created and deleted: 10522 a regular file (test_sparse), and **386843/386844 directories
mode 0777 — my own `gd1` and `gd1/sub`** — all with `Links: 0, Size: 0`, correctly deleted.

★ **The real explanation is the shutdown, not the filesystem.** The SD root *is* `/`, so it can
never be unmounted, and the harness cuts power at the end of a cycle. Metadata still sitting in
libcache never reaches the card: two inodes and two blocks freed in memory stay marked used on
disk. Every USB-stick `e2fsck` this week was clean **because those scripts call `/bin/umount`
first** — a difference I had not registered until the numbers forced it.

⚠ **This means the SD root accumulates a small leak on every power-cut boot.** That is ordinary for
a journal-less filesystem with no clean unmount — but it is worth knowing for a product that boots
from SD, and `/usr/bin/sync` *is* in the image. **Prediction, stated before the run:** repeating the
same workload but ending with `sync` should leave the free counts unchanged (delta 0 from the
current 514765 / 238276) if Phoenix's `sync` actually reaches libcache; if the deltas are the same
as before, it does not, and that is worth knowing too.

### ✅ `sync` WORKS — prediction confirmed exactly, delta 0/0

Same workload, once ending in `/usr/bin/sync`:

| | free inodes | free blocks |
|---|---|---|
| without `sync` | **−2** | **−2** |
| **with `sync`** | **+0** | **+0** |

A clean A/B — identical workload, the only difference being the final `sync`. **Phoenix's `sync`
does reach libcache and flush metadata to the card.** Zero ref-count errors in both, and the three
orphan-flagged inodes are *the same three* as before (10522, 386843, 386844) — left by the earlier
non-sync run, not created by this one.

↩ **CORRECTED 07:35 — `sync` REDUCES the leak but does not reliably eliminate it.** I
over-generalised from that one measurement. A *fresh flash* + the same workload + `sync`, read
back in full and compared against the **pristine image**:

| | files | blocks |
|---|---|---|
| pristine image | 10521 | 813006 |
| after flash + workload + `sync` | **10522** | **813007** |

**+1 inode and +1 block**, and `e2fsck` again reports orphan-list inodes — but **different ones**
(386842/386843, where the non-sync run left 10522/386843/386844), so they come from *this* run.

The earlier "delta 0/0" was measured against a card that was **already dirty**: it showed no
*additional* leak, which is not the same as no leak. Both numbers are n=1, so the honest statement
is: non-sync leaked 2+2, sync-ended leaked 1+1, and `sync` helps — no claimed factor.

### The focused experiment: boot alone does NOT leak

Ran it — SD boot with `sync` as the *only* command, no workload, then a full read-back:

| card | files | blocks |
|---|---|---|
| pristine image | 10521 | 813006 |
| after flash + workload + `sync` | 10522 | 813007 |
| **after a boot that did nothing but `sync`** | **10522** | **813007** — unchanged |

So **the shutdown path is not the leak**; whatever the residue is, the workload causes it. That
rules out my "metadata dirtied after the sync" guess.

### ★★★★ ROOT CAUSE, and it is bigger than the residue: `sync()` WAS AN EMPTY STUB

Rather than spend two more Pi cycles splitting the +1, I modelled the power cut **on the host** —
`noumount.c` does the workload and then simply stops, with **no `ext2_objs_destroy()`**, which is
the one thing every other harness does and the SD root never can.

| mode | result |
|---|---|
| `mkdir`/`rmdir` only, no unmount | inode count **unchanged**, `e2fsck` clean |
| create/write/unlink files, no unmount | **unchanged**, clean |
| both | **unchanged**, clean |

**So libext2 does not leak, even unmounted.** That isolated the difference to the one layer the
host harness does not have — the driver's libcache — and following that up produced this:

```c
void sync(void)
{
}
```

**`sync()` was an empty stub, and `fsync()`/`fdatasync()` do not exist at all.** Meanwhile `mtSync`
(`sys/file.h`, `0xf52`) is implemented by **eight** storage drivers including `bcm2711-emmc`, where
it calls `cache_flush()`. **Nothing in libphoenix ever sent it.**

So there was no way for *any* application on Phoenix to get data onto storage — and on the Pi 4,
where the SD root **is** `/` and can never be unmounted, `sync` is precisely the thing an operator
would reach for. Implemented in `libphoenix 40a1efb` (root filesystem only; libphoenix has no
userspace mount table, and that is documented in the code).

↩↩ **This means my earlier "sync fixes it, delta 0/0" was wrong TWICE OVER** — measured against an
already-dirty baseline *and* calling a function that did nothing. I have corrected it above; this
is the explanation.

### ↩ CORRECTION, then a SECOND defect: `fsync()` exists — and it synced the wrong object

↩ I wrote "`fsync()`/`fdatasync()` do not exist at all". **Wrong.** `fsync()` is a *syscall*
(`unistd/file.c` `WRAP_ERRNO_DEF` → kernel `posix_fsync()`), which is why grepping libphoenix's
`.c` files for `int fsync(` found nothing; the binary carries both `T fsync` and `T sys_fsync`.
Corrected in the source comment too (`libphoenix a1bce81`), since that file is published.

Checking whether `fsync()` was any better then turned up a **real kernel defect**
(`kernel 482b54c2`). `posix_fsync()` built its message as:

```c
hal_memset(&msg, 0, sizeof(msg_t));
msg.type = 0xf52;                               /* mtSync */
hal_memcpy(msg.i.raw, &f->oid, sizeof(f->oid)); /* FIXME: Replace this hack */
```

That `FIXME` was describing a **bug**, not inelegance. Every storage driver implementing `mtSync`
reads **`msg->oid.id`** — `bcm2711-emmc`, `zynq7000-sdcard`, `pc-ata`, `flashdrv` — and **none**
reads `i.raw` (0 hits across `storage/`). `msg.oid` was left **zeroed by the memset**, so
`fsync()` reached the right port and then asked it to sync **storage id 0** instead of the file's
device. **`fsync()` did not sync the file.**

Fixed by setting `msg.oid = f->oid`; the `i.raw` copy is kept as well as, not instead of, so an
out-of-tree server reading the old location cannot break.

### ⛓ The chain was broken at THREE levels, and the third is the one that mattered

Following it down: `sync()` → `mtSync` → **the filesystem server**, not the block device. And
`libext2_handler()` had **no `mtSync` case at all**, so it fell through `default: break;` and left
`o.err` at the 0 the caller had memset — **`sync()` and `fsync()` returned SUCCESS while flushing
nothing.** Silent success is worse than failure: an application that called `fsync()` *and checked
the result* was told its data was durable.

The driver side was ready the whole time — `bcm2711-emmc` publishes `.sync = sdstorage_cachedFlush`
→ `cache_flush()`. Nothing ever sent it a message.

| level | state | fix |
|---|---|---|
| libphoenix | `void sync(void) { }` — empty stub | `40a1efb` |
| kernel | `fsync()` sent `mtSync` with a **zeroed** `msg.oid` | `482b54c2` |
| **libext2** | **no `mtSync` case — returned success, did nothing** | `017eb3a` |

↩ **Scoping the kernel half precisely, having re-read it:** `libext2_handler()` dispatches
`case mtSync: libext2_sync(fdata)` — it uses **`fdata`, not `msg->oid`**, so it flushes the whole
filesystem and the zeroed oid does not affect `fsync()` on a *file*. The kernel bug bites where the
receiver **does** use the oid: `fsync()` on a **raw block-device fd**, where
`storage_sync(msg->oid.id)` syncs storage object 0. Still a real defect, and still worth fixing —
but narrower than "fsync never worked", and I had written it the broad way.

⚠ **The gate at 08:21 could NOT have shown this working, and I am not reading it as evidence.** That
image carries `libphoenix 40a1efb` but **not** `ext2 017eb3a` (committed after the build started),
so `sync()` correctly sent a message that libext2 still ignored. Boot was clean — right lane,
`test_sparse` OK, 0 faults — but a residue measurement from it would say nothing about the fix.
Rebuilding with the whole chain instead of spending a cycle confirming a known gap.

★ **SQLite is the thing to gate this with** — it is ported and HW-verified here, it uses the unix
VFS unmodified, and it calls `fsync()` for durability. Until now that call reached the driver and
synced the wrong object.

### ✅✅ RESOLVED — there was never a leak. The "residue" is `/var/tmp`.

Tracked it to the inode. Group 95 was the only group whose free counts moved; diffing the two
images' **inode bitmaps** bit by bit named **inode 386841**, and `debugfs -R "ncheck 386841"` says:

```
Inode   Pathname
386841  /var/tmp
```

`/var` is **empty in the pristine image** and contains `tmp` (mode 0777, `Links: 2`) after boot.
The whole +1 inode / +1 block is a directory the system **legitimately creates at startup**.

And the three `e2fsck` "orphaned inode list" complaints are **benign**: `testi` says all three
(10522, 386842, 386843) are **not in use** in *both* images. A correctly freed inode carries a
non-zero `dtime` — that is what defect 12's fix writes — and `e2fsck` walks the orphan chain only
because `s_last_orphan` is non-zero, which is a **host image-build artefact** present in the
pristine image too. Free inodes with a dtime, flagged and ignored.

**So the SD root is clean.** Nothing leaked.

### ↩↩↩ What I got wrong along the way, in order

1. "`sync` fixes it, delta 0/0" — measured against an **already-dirty** baseline, so it answered
   "did it get worse", not "is it clean".
2. "`sync` reduces but does not eliminate it (2+2 → 1+1)" — the 1+1 was `/var/tmp` all along.
3. Comparing the *new* image's card against the **old** image's pristine counts. The totals gave it
   away: 1051314 vs 1051284 blocks, and a filesystem's total block count cannot change from use.
   Each build produces a fresh image with its own baseline.
4. The whole "unclean shutdown leaks metadata" framing, which I stated confidently and which the
   full-chain run **falsified**: with `sync()` working end to end the residue is *unchanged* at
   `/var/tmp`.

★ **The one measurement that dissolved it was the cheapest**: `ncheck` on the inode. Four hours of
counting files, and the answer was to ask the filesystem what the inode's *name* was.

⚠ **The fixes stand on their own.** `sync()` was an empty stub, `fsync()` sent a zeroed oid, and no
filesystem handled `mtSync` — three real defects with real durability consequences, all still worth
having. The investigation was productive; its premise was not.

`e2fsck` flags **three** inodes — 10522 (a regular file, from `test_sparse`) and **386842 / 386843,
both directories mode 0777**, i.e. the `c1` and `c1/sub` I created and removed. But the used-inode
count is only **+1** against the pristine image, not +3.

Three inodes "part of the orphaned inode list" and a count that moved by one are not consistent
with each other, and I do not have an explanation that fits both. Candidates, none verified:

* a freed inode legitimately carries a non-zero `dtime` (that is what defect 12's fix writes), and
  `e2fsck` may be flagging *correctly-freed* inodes only because `s_last_orphan` is non-zero — in
  which case the flags are benign noise and the real residue is just the +1;
* or one inode is genuinely leaked and the other two are that noise.

**The attribution experiment, not yet run:** from a fresh flash, one boot running *only*
`mkdir`/`rmdir` + `sync`, and another running *only* `test_sparse` + `sync`, each read back. That
splits the +1 between the two halves of the workload. Cheap — two cycles — and it is where this
should resume.

⚠ Scale check before anyone spends more on it: **1 inode and 1 block out of 525288 and 1051284**,
on a journal-less filesystem with no clean unmount, which `fsck` repairs. It is a loose end worth
closing, not a problem with the SD lane.

### ⚠ ...but my read-back is short, and I nearly misread that as a defect

`e2fsck` on the image also says:

```
The filesystem size (according to the superblock) is 1051284 blocks
The physical size of the device is 1050624 blocks
```

That reads like "the filesystem does not fit its partition", which would mean the last 660 blocks
are unreachable — a real defect. **It is almost certainly my measurement.** The partition is
2102724 sectors = 1026.72 MiB and the filesystem is 1026.06 MiB, so it *does* fit (the pristine
image `e2fsck`s clean with no size complaint). I asked for `count=1027` and got exactly 1026 MiB —
and **`dd` printed no `records` line at all**, where the earlier `count=1026` run did. That is the
signature of the capture window ending before `dd` finished, not of a device EOF.

Settling it rather than guessing: `dd if=/dev/mmcblk0p2 of=/dev/null bs=1M` with no `count` reads
to EOF and reports the true device size at no NFS cost. Running now.

★ Worth naming: I came within one sentence of reporting "Phoenix truncates the partition device"
as a defect, on the strength of a number my own short read produced.

### ✅ SETTLED — no truncation, and the arithmetic explains everything

`dd bs=1M count=1` at increasing offsets on `/dev/mmcblk0p2`:

| read | result |
|---|---|
| offset 0 | 1+0 records |
| `skip=1025` → [1074790400, 1075838976) | 1+0 records |
| **`skip=1026`** → [1075838976, **1076887552**) | **Invalid argument** |

The partition is 1076594688 bytes, so that last read **overruns the end by 286 KiB**. `bs=1M`
simply cannot address the final 0.72 MiB. The device is not truncated, and my
readiness-race hypothesis was wrong too — the driver was fine all along.

### 🐞➡✅ What IS real: `-EINVAL` where POSIX wants a SHORT READ (`822e933`)

`storage_read()` rejected any request with `(offs + len) > strg->size`. POSIX `read()` at or past
the end is EOF (0), and a read that *crosses* the end returns the bytes that are there. Returning
`-EINVAL` for both means **reading a device to EOF fails**: `dd if=/dev/mmcblk0p2 of=img` with no
count aborts after the last whole megabyte, prints **no summary**, and leaves an image short of
the filesystem — which is precisely why this looked like a truncated device twice, and why my
`e2fsck` read-backs were 756 KB short each time.

Fixed in the Pi driver: `offs >= size` returns 0, and `len` is clamped to what remains, in both the
MTD and block branches. ⚠ The same pattern exists in `zynq7000-sdcard/sdstorage_srv.c` and
`zynq-flash.c` and is **left alone deliberately** — neither runs on this hardware, and the fork
policy is to fix for Pi4 without touching other arches.

`syntax-check.sh` CLEAN. **Gate that can fail:** `dd if=/dev/mmcblk0p2 of=/p2.img bs=1M` with no
count must now print a summary and produce exactly 1076594688 bytes. It demonstrably fails before
the change — and it finally yields a *complete* partition image, which is what a full `e2fsck` of
the SD root has needed all along.

`56fe0a5` then hoists the clamp so there is one copy rather than one per backend — pure
restructure, single-exit style preserved. ⚠ For the record: the gate in flight was built from
`822e933`, the duplicated-but-equivalent form; the behaviour it validates is unchanged.

### ✅ GATED 07:02 — and the `+1` is the proof

`dd if=/dev/mmcblk0p2 of=/p2full.img bs=1M`, no count, on a netboot loader carrying `822e933`
(the driver comes from TFTP on this lane, so no reflash was needed):

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| `dd` summary | **absent** — aborted with `Invalid argument` | `1026+1 records in / 1026+1 records out` |
| image size | 1075838976 (756 KB short) | **1076594688 — exactly the partition** |

The trailing **`+1`** is the final *partial* block — the 0.72 MiB `bs=1M` could never address. That
is the short read, visible in one character.

**First FULL `e2fsck` of the SD root**, on a byte-exact image:

| check | result |
|---|---|
| "physical size of the device is..." / "partition table is likely corrupt" | **gone** — it was purely my short read |
| ref-count errors (defect 19's signature) | **0** |
| everything else | the **same three** orphan-list inodes from the earlier pre-`sync` power-cut cycle |

So the SD root's only remaining complaint is the one already explained and already mitigated
(§ `sync`, delta 0/0). Nothing new, and the "corrupt partition table" scare is formally closed.

### ⚖ `storage_write()` has the same pattern — considered and DECLINED

`storage_write()` also returns `-EINVAL` for `(offs + len) > strg->size`, and that is equally not
what POSIX says (a short write, or `ENOSPC`). I am **not** touching it:

* there is **no measured symptom** — the read case cost two Pi cycles and a near-miss defect
  report, the write case has cost nothing;
* a *short write* on a storage device silently truncates the caller's data, which is worse than a
  clean refusal. Getting it right means choosing between short-write and `ENOSPC`, and that is a
  design call;
* it is the write path of the driver that owns the boot filesystem.

Changing it would be unforced risk. Recorded so the asymmetry is deliberate and visible rather
than looking like something I missed.

## 1d. ★★★★ 2026-09-22 — DEFECT 21: `setattr(atSize)` on a DIRECTORY destroyed it (`d46ba88`)

**Found by working down the "still uncovered" list written two turns ago.** `setattr` was on it;
reading the code showed `atSize` reaches truncate by a **second route** that the guard never covered.

`ext2_truncate()` refuses a non-regular file — `-EISDIR` for a directory, `-EINVAL` otherwise.
`ext2_setattr(atSize)` calls `_ext2_file_truncate()` **directly** and never saw that check, and the
inner guard only covers device nodes and short symlinks, whose block array holds an rdev or a
target. **A directory's block array holds real block numbers.**

| `setattr(atSize, 0)` on a 40-entry directory | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| return value | **0 — success** | `-EISDIR` |
| entries still found | **0 of 40** | 40 of 40 |
| `e2fsck` | zero-length directory; `'..'` is `<The NULL inode>`; ref counts wrong; **40 unattached inodes** | clean |

⚠ It **cannot** be fixed inside `_ext2_file_truncate()`: `_ext2_obj_destroy()` legitimately uses
that path to release a *removed* directory's blocks. The guard belongs where the operation is
requested. Both block sizes, full regression clean, `syntax-check.sh` CLEAN.

★ The worst shape a defect can have — **destroys data and returns success**. Nothing above the
filesystem had any way to notice.

## 1x. 🐞 2026-09-22 — THE UMASS UNMOUNT RACE REPRODUCES, and it loses data with rc=0

Once the stick turned out to be plugged in all along (§1y), the fix from §1e-resolved stopped being
a code-reading claim and got an A/B. **Baseline first**, on `devices d593201` (master, no drain),
`umountrace-A` 19:29:

```
TAG-umount-issued t=2
umass: /dev/umass0 write amplification: asked 4320256 B, device 4307968 B (0.99x)   <- the unmount ran
dd: writing '/mnt/usb/race.bin': Invalid argument
6+0 records in
5+0 records out
TAG-umount-returned rc=0 t=3
```

**`umount` returned SUCCESS one second after it was issued, having torn the filesystem down under a
live 200 MiB write that had done 5 MiB.** The writer got `-EINVAL` — the port and context were gone
— and `umount` reported `rc=0`. So the observable failure is not (in this run) the use-after-free
crash: it is **silent truncation of in-flight data behind a successful unmount**, which is worse to
live with because nothing reports it. The UAF is the same race's other outcome; which one you get
depends on where the other pool thread happens to be.

⚠ Note what the *obvious* test would have said here. "Mount, copy a file, umount" completes the copy
before the umount is sent, so it passes on exactly this code. The discriminator is having the write
still in flight, and the measurement is the **timestamp**, not the return code — `rc=0` is what the
broken version gives you.

## 1n. 2026-09-23 — D4 root-caused, and deliberately NOT fixed tonight

plo installs a tag-and-halt vector table and `VBAR_EL3`/`EL2`/`EL1` all point at it, so an exception
in the bootloader yields one letter. The row said "either give it an entry or remove it" — the real
reason it exists is sharper than that.

The proper dispatcher is already there (`hal/aarch64/_exceptions.S:24`), but it reads **`spsr_el3`,
`elr_el3`, `esr_el3`, `far_el3`**, and plo installs that **single** table at **three** exception
levels (`_init.S:132`, `:216`, `:239`). At EL1 those EL3-only accesses trap, so restoring it makes a
crash *worse* — precisely what the TD-diag comment records. Closing it means a `CurrentEL`-aware
dispatcher, or one table per EL.

⛔ **Not attempted unattended.** It is assembly on the bootloader's boot path — the same
"highest blast radius, invisible to a green boot" class the project marks attended — and plo takes no
exception on a healthy boot, so the payoff is diagnosability and upstreamability, not correctness.
Recovery would in fact be cheap (the loader is re-fetched over TFTP every boot), so this is a
judgement about *supervision*, not about risk of bricking. Row now carries the mechanism and the fix
shape so the owner can action it in minutes.

## 1a2. ✅ 2026-09-23 — C1: every heap-size reporter now splits the word, not just one

The `hlo32`/`hhi32`/`hfixed` fields went into the **corrupt-header** path only. But three other
reporters also print a heap size — `chunk handed out twice`, the stale large-bin node, and the
double-free path — and **any of them can fire first**. A fire there would have printed the raw
64-bit word and left the reader to re-derive the split, which is exactly the waste the fields exist
to prevent. Given C1 is event-gated and the events are rare, the cheapest thing worth doing without
an event is making sure *whichever* guard fires is decisive.

Factored into `malloc_reportHeapSize()` and used at every site (libphoenix `HEAD`). It repeats
`malloc_heapSizeValid()`'s size tests against the **real base** rather than calling it — that
function also tests its argument for page alignment and window membership, so a fixed-up copy would
return 0 every time and `hfixed` would look like evidence while being a constant.

Verified after the refactor, not assumed: the harness's `hi32-write` injection still prints
`hlo32 0x1000 / hhi32 0x80000001 / hfixed 1`, and the split now also appears from the other
reporters (`hhi32=0` on uncorrupted heaps), which is the point of the change.

Gate: STK ran a real race (8.69 fps, 45 `flipstat`), **0 `malloc:` lines** — silent on a healthy run,
as a printf in a fault branch should be — 0 faults. That run is also hunt trial 17.

The ADMA2 `ADDR_2` fix was gated on netboot with a raw `dd` read — but the surface that actually
matters for it is the **SD boot path**, where an ext2 root is mounted *through* ADMA2, and the card
was still carrying the 02:24 build. So: `--variant sd` build (qemu: *"image PASSES — safe to
flash"*), staged, netboot loader rebuilt, flashed by Phoenix itself, then booted from.

**Flash:** `1093+1 records`, **1 146 923 008 bytes** — exactly the image — at 12.5 MB/s, 0 faults.

| check | result |
|---|---|
| lane | **SD** — `mmcblk0p2:ext2` present, `nfs;/` absent |
| engine | **`reads=ADMA2 writes=ADMA2`** — the fix is on the boot path, not bypassed |
| root | `mounted /dev/mmcblk0p2 (ext2, id=2) as / after 0 tries` |
| ext2 | **`SPARSE TEST OK (0 failure(s))`** |
| faults | **0** |

★ **And the card's own `build-versions` confirms what it carries** — checked against what I intended
to build, rather than assuming the flash did what I meant:

```
libphoenix               07a0a29   (swprintf)
phoenix-rtos-devices     92844ab   (ADMA2 ADDR_2 fix)
phoenix-rtos-filesystems 522ecc8   (f_fsid)
phoenix-rtos-kernel      20179bfe  (crash-dumper guard)
phoenix-rtos-ports       fd34552   (quake3 monotonic clock)
```

All five match. Both lanes now run the day's work: netboot gated by the six-app showcase (§1c), SD
gated here. Netboot restored as the default lane. Manifest `2026-09-23-sd-reflash-adma2.md`.
