## 1d. ✅ 2026-09-23 — audit after C7: no second instance of that bug class

C7 was "a 64-bit register whose upper half is never written", and a bug like that costing WiFi for
three weeks is worth checking for siblings rather than assuming it was unique.

| check | result |
|---|---|
| `_ADDR_2` / `_HI` / `_UPPER32` register pairs across **all** Pi 4 drivers (`rpi4-*`, `bcm2711-emmc`, `rpi4-v3d`) | **exactly one** — `SDHOST_REG_ADMA_ADDR_2`, the one just fixed |
| `SDHOST_REG_*` constants defined but never referenced in any `.c` | 8, all benign: `CAPABILITIES_RESERVED`, `CURRENT_CAP*`, `FORCE_EVENT`, `VERSION` (read-only/optional) and `RESPONSE_1..3` |
| `RESPONSE_1..3` — do we drop the upper words of a 136-bit R2? | **no** — read in a loop as `RESPONSE_0 + i` (`sdcard.c:1071`), so all four words are taken |

⇒ **Clean.** Recorded as a bounded negative result so nobody re-runs it: the C7 class does not recur
in this driver family, and the unused constants are not programming gaps.

## 1e. ✅★★★ 2026-09-23 — C7 FIXED IN ONE LINE: the ADMA2 address had an uninitialised upper half

`_sdio_admaArm()` programmed `ADMA_ADDR_1` (0x58) and left `ADMA_ADDR_2` (0x5C) holding whatever it
had. The descriptor address is a **64-bit** register; writing only its low half leaves the engine
running with an uninitialised upper word.

SD never showed it — ADMA2 moves the card at 12.9 MB/s and `e2fsck` comes back clean — but the stray
bus accesses broke the **other** SDHCI on the chip, so the BCM43455 could not be loaded at all.

| | firmware upload |
|---|---|
| ADMA2 armed, before | `staged 24576 B / 0 windows`, `rc_w=-5`, **`fw_alive=0`** |
| ADMA2 armed, **after** | **`staged 643648 B / 20 windows`, `rc_w=0`, `fw_alive=1`** |

**Gated in one boot with ADMA2 fully armed**: SD reads `64+0 records` off `mmcblk0p2`, WiFi firmware
boots, 0 faults. So the fix keeps ADMA2's throughput *and* restores WiFi — the trade-off recorded
yesterday turned out not to be one. `devices 92844ab`, manifest `2026-09-23-adma2-addr2-fix.md`.

### What made it findable was narrowing before touching anything

Four A/B configurations, each verifying the variable really changed, before a single line was
written: driver absent → healthy; driver on **SDMA** → healthy; driver with the descriptor page
**allocated but the engine never armed** → healthy. That ruled out the driver, DMA in general, and
contiguous-memory pressure, and left one 30-line function. Then the two MMIO bases (`0xfe340000` vs
`0xfe300000`) ruled out register aliasing, so the interference had to be via the bus — which is
exactly what an unwritten address half produces.

⚖ **The mechanism is inferred, not observed.** Writing 0 fixes it; I never read back what 0x5C
actually held. A future session wanting certainty should print it before the write.

**C7 closed** and moved to `docs/done/closed-issues-archive.md`; the list is 28.

## 1e1. ★★★ 2026-09-23 — C7: the ADMA2 *transfer* is the culprit; the allocation is innocent

Third experiment, one build. Kept the descriptor page allocated (init untouched) but never armed the
engine — `if (0 && _sdio_admaArm(...))` — so transfers run on SDMA while every allocation ADMA2 makes
still happens. That splits "the extra `MAP_CONTIGUOUS` page starves WiFi" from "the ADMA2 transfer
does it".

| config | SD driver | descriptor page | engine armed | WiFi firmware |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| baseline | running | yes | **yes** | `24576 B / 0 windows`, `rc_w=-5`, **`fw_alive=0`** |
| §1f | absent | — | — | `643648 B`, `fw_alive=1` |
| §1e2 | running | no | no | `643648 B`, `fw_alive=1` |
| **this** | running | **yes** | **no** | **`643648 B`, `rc_w=0`, `fw_alive=1`** |

⇒ **The allocation is innocent.** And `SDCARD_MAX_TRANSFER` is 128 KiB for *both* engines — checked in
the header, not assumed — so the 128 KiB contiguous staging buffer is identical either way. The only
difference left is one extra 4 KiB page, and this run proves that page is harmless.

**What remains is what `_sdio_admaArm()` actually does:** program `ADMA_ADDR_1` with
`SDCARD_DRAM_BUS(admaDescPhys)`, set HOST_CONTROL's DMA-select to ADMA2, and let the engine fetch its
descriptor list from DRAM. The two controllers have separate MMIO, so the interference reaches WiFi
through the bus/AXI path or the descriptor fetch itself — not through the register file.

⚠ **A diagnostic that can mislead the next person:** the boot line `data paths: reads=ADMA2
writes=ADMA2` reports whether the descriptor page was **allocated**, not whether the engine is used.
It printed `ADMA2` in the never-armed run. Worth fixing when this is touched.

Experiment reverted and rebuilt.

## 1e2. ★★★ 2026-09-23 — C7 narrowed to ONE `#define`: it is ADMA2

Second experiment, one build. Kept the SD driver running but switched its engine to SDMA
(`SDCARD_DMA_ADMA2 0`), and verified from the boot line that the variable actually changed rather
than trusting the edit — `sdcard: data paths: reads=SDMA writes=SDMA`, card up, `2 partition(s)`.

| config | SD driver | engine | WiFi firmware |
|---|---|---|---|
| baseline | running | **ADMA2** | `staged 24576 B / 0 windows`, `rc_w=-5`, **`fw_alive=0`** |
| §1f | absent | — | `staged 643648 B / 20 windows`, **`fw_alive=1`** |
| **this** | **running** | **SDMA** | **`staged 643648 B / 20 windows`, `rc_w=0`, `fw_alive=1`** |

⇒ **Not the SD driver, and not DMA in general — ADMA2 specifically.** The SD driver can initialise
the card, switch to DDR50 and read a real MBR without disturbing WiFi at all, provided it does so
over SDMA.

⚖ n=1 per configuration. The signals are binary and look deterministic (`rc_w` 0 vs −5, 643 648 vs
24 576 bytes), and the code paths are not racy, so I have not spent further cycles repeating them —
but that is the standard of evidence, stated rather than implied.

⚠ **A trade-off the owner should see while this is open:** ADMA2 buys about **5%** SD throughput
(12.9 vs 12.3 MB/s) plus architectural headroom — no 512 KiB request cap, no contiguous-buffer
requirement — and it currently costs **WiFi entirely**. Reverting to SDMA is a one-line change that
would restore WiFi today; whether that is the right trade is yours.

⏭ **Next:** find what ADMA2 touches that the *other* SDHCI can see — the descriptor table's memory,
the shared VideoCore clock, or AXI/bus-address territory. The two controllers have separate MMIO, so
the interference is through something shared, not through the register file.

Experiment reverted and rebuilt; the bench is back on the committed tree.

## 1f. ★★★ 2026-09-23 — C7 ROOT-CAUSED: the SD driver is what kills the WiFi firmware

One experiment, one build, decisive. Disabled `bcm2711-emmc` for the netboot variant only, rebuilt,
re-ran the identical WiFi command. Verified the driver really was absent (0 mentions, no
`mmcblk0 ready`) rather than trusting the edit.

| | `bcm2711-emmc` running | disabled |
|---|---|---|
| `fw_load` | 24 576 B, **0 windows**, `worst rc_w=-5` | **643 648 B, 20 windows, `rc_w=0`** |
| nvram | `rc_nvram_w=-5` | **`rc_nvram_w=0`** |
| memory probes | 0/6 changed | **2/6 → *CR4 IS EXECUTING*** |
| verdict | **`fw_alive=0`** | **`fw_alive=1` — firmware booted** |
| `wifi scan` | `escan_rc=-1041, events=0` | 0 faults |

643 648 is the whole firmware blob (643 651 B). **Same binary, same tree — the only variable was
whether the SD driver ran.**

### Why it regressed, and why nobody noticed

The Pi 4 has **two** SDHCI controllers, and the SD driver is clobbering the WiFi SDIO host. The
regression window fits what changed: on **2026-09-04**, WiFi's last working day, the card was
**blank** (`0 partition(s)`) and the driver was pre-ADMA2, so it barely transacted. Today it reports
`reads=ADMA2 writes=ADMA2` and reads a real MBR at boot. Nothing caught it because **no gate runs
WiFi** and nothing in the image even builds the driver (§1h).

⚖ **What is established vs what is not.** Established: running `bcm2711-emmc` prevents the WiFi
firmware loading, reproducibly, with the numbers above. *Not* established: which part of it —
ADMA2, the clock programming, the mailbox, or DMA/bus-address territory. **Next experiment is one
build:** `SDCARD_DMA_ADMA2 0` and retest, which isolates ADMA2 from the rest of the driver.

⚠ This is now a defect in a **shipping** component, not just in WiFi: the SD driver is in every
image. Bench restored — the plo edit was reverted and rebuilt, it was an experiment, not a change.

## 1g. 🐞 2026-09-23 — WiFi REGRESSED: the firmware upload fails with −EIO (new issue C7)

Having staged a freshly-linked `rpi4-wifi` (§1h), I ran it — the first time WiFi has been exercised
since **2026-09-04**. It does not work, and the reason is precise.

```
rpi4-wifi: WL_REG_ON readback=1 (expect 1)
fw_load: staged 24576 bytes across 0 windows  HS=0  worst rc_w=-5
nvram:   1728 bytes -> chip 0x25f940 ...      rc_nvram_w=-5
rstvec@addr0 readback: 00 00 00 00 vs fw[0..3]: 98 f1 3e b8 -> MISMATCH
  -> 0/6 points changed => no memory writes anywhere -- fw genuinely not running
SOCRAM-tail rc=-5
  -> fw_alive=0
```

**The SDIO writes fail (`rc=-5`, −EIO): ~24 KB of a ~4 MB image reaches the chip.** The rstvec
mismatch and `fw_alive=0` follow from that. ⚠ The driver's own hint — *"chase rstvec/activate"* —
points at the wrong layer; it was written before this failure mode existed.

Confirmed functionally, not just by the probe: `wifi scan` returns
`(no APs found; done_status=-1 escan_rc=-1041 events=0)`.

### It is a regression, and nothing would have caught it

| | |
|---|---|
| driver source last changed | `843d193`, **2026-09-04** — unchanged since |
| last `fw_alive=1` in the archive | **2026-09-04** (`wifi-reconfirm`, `wifi-dhcp` — the full DHCP lease) |
| `fw_alive=0` before today | only **2026-08-09**, the pre-exec-gate bring-up era |
| libphoenix commits in between | **94** |

Undetected for three weeks because **no gate runs WiFi**, and per §1h nothing in the image even
builds the driver.

⚠ **The obvious A/B is not available.** I staged the surviving 2026-09-04 binary to compare — it
produces **no output at all** on today's system. That is the known stale-binary/ABI trap, so the run
says nothing about firmware start; it is an unusable instrument, not evidence. Recorded as a failed
experiment rather than dressed up as a finding.

💡 **Untested hypothesis for whoever picks this up:** the Pi 4 has two SDHCI controllers, and
`bcm2711-emmc` — which has changed a great deal since 09-04 (ADMA2 both directions, MBR handling,
the DMA work) — shares clocking/pinmux territory with the WiFi SDIO host. Worth trying before a
94-commit libphoenix bisect.

Filed as **C7**; the list is 29.

## 1h. ↩↩ 2026-09-23 — WiFi has NO userspace in the image, and my own tooling claim was wrong

Chasing yesterday's "`syntax-check.sh` can't check the WiFi driver" note turned up something bigger.

**`rpi4-wifi` is not built by anything.** No binary in `prog/`, none on the NFS root, not in any
target program list. It lives in `devices/wifi/rpi4-wifi/` with its own **`build-standalone.sh`** —
deliberately outside the project build, because the BCM43455 firmware / NVRAM / CLM blobs are
compiled-in C arrays under a Cypress EULA and are gitignored.

⇒ **F1 was wrong.** It said the netif was wired up and only the join was manual — *"start the
`rpi4-wifi` daemon"*. You cannot start it: nothing puts it on the system. What *is* in the image is
the lwip half, which registers a netif every boot and then waits forever for `/dev/wifidata`. Every
proven WiFi result — WPA2 join, 4-way key, full DHCP lease — came from hand-built binaries.

✅ **Partly unblocked rather than just re-labelled:** ran `build-standalone.sh` against today's tree —
**0 undefined symbols** — and staged `rpi4-wifi` + `wifi` to the NFS root. WiFi is now actually
runnable on the netboot lane (`rpi4-wifi &`, then `wifi scan`).

⚠ The copy that had been sitting there was dated **2026-09-04**, linked against a months-old
libphoenix — exactly the "a tool left over from before a core rebuild measures the old libc" trap the
diag-tool script exists to avoid.

### ↩ And the correction to yesterday's note

I wrote that `syntax-check.sh` has a *tooling gap* because it lacks the generated-header paths. That
is not the reason. It works off the **project build's** compile commands, and this file **is not in
the project build**. There is nothing to fix in the script; the right instrument is the driver's own
`build-standalone.sh`.

Which is a better check than the hand-rolled `-fsyntax-only` I used: under it, **both master and
`wip/rpi4-wifi-glom` link with 0 undefined symbols** against today's tree. So the 21x branch is
confirmed buildable by the project's own means, not just parseable.

## 1i. ★ 2026-09-23 — the unlanded WiFi branch is LANDABLE (21x RX, in the real path)

Yesterday's branch sweep surfaced `devices/wip/rpi4-wifi-glom` claiming *"slot-based walk works
(RX 0.14 → 3.0 MB/s)"*, unlanded since 2026-09-02. Triaged it, because a 21x networking result
sitting outside master is worth more than another debt row.

**What it is:** SDPCM glom de-aggregation. The BCM43455 firmware packs several 802.11 subframes into
one SDIO superframe; without de-aggregating, the driver takes one subframe per read — which is
exactly what 0.14 MB/s looks like.

| check | result |
|---|---|
| size | **one file**, `wifi/rpi4-wifi/rpi4-wifi.c`, +201 / −14 |
| leftover diagnostics in added lines | **0** (`printf`/`TODO`/`FIXME`/`DEBUG`) — "diagnostics removed" holds |
| in the real RX path? | **yes** — `diag_glomNext` ← `diag_wifiFrameRx` ← the `/dev/wifidata` handler, i.e. the lwip netif seam |
| compiles against today's tree? | **yes, clean** |

⇒ The 21x is about **networking**, not a measurement tool. ⓘ The `diag_` prefix is this driver's
house style throughout, not a marker that something is a probe — worth knowing before dismissing it.

⚠ **`syntax-check.sh` cannot check this file, and master fails identically** — it lacks the paths to
the generated `wifi-fw-43455.h` (in `phoenix-rtos-lwip/port/`) and `clm-43455.h` (in
`tools/wifi-probe/`). Compiling by hand with both on `-I` is what settled it. That is a tooling gap
to fix, not evidence about the branch; without the master control it would have read as "the branch
is stale and no longer builds".

**What it still needs is yours:** an AP plus credentials to re-measure. Before landing, its
file-scope `g_glom_*` state is worth checking against however many consumers the driver has.

## 1j. ↩ 2026-09-23 — D9 was wrong about where the noise is

The row said "residual debug prints from the bring-up era remain in the kernel and plo". Both halves
are wrong. The **kernel has none** — its only five `diag`-ish hits are structured `lib_sprintf` into
diagnostic buffers (`kmalloc_diagBuf`, `threads_diagBuf`, a pmap marker), which are facilities, not
leftovers. And the noise that *does* exist is mostly the **armstub**, which the row never mentioned.

Every boot log opens with this, before anything readable:

```
1111444422225555AS0
TR0
TR1
TR2
TR3
```

| chars | source | meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `1 4 2 5`, ×4 | `phoenix-armstub8-rpi4.S` | errata 859971+1319367 · L2 prefetch policy · SMPEN · L2CTLR timing — **once per core** |
| `AS0` | armstub | end-of-stub |
| `TR0`–`TR3` | `phoenix-kernel8-reloc.S` | one per core |
| `A` + `3`/`2`/`1` | plo `hal_exitToEL1` | entry, then which EL it exited from |

Every one is commented where it is written; none is legible where it is read.

⛔ **Not changed tonight, and the reason is not risk of control flow** — a `uart_putc` cannot change
behaviour. It is that this is the first code that runs, on all four cores, spread across three
components, and the useful version of this change does all three at once. Doing the plo third of it
unattended at 06:00 would leave the log looking much the same while making the next pass harder.
plo's markers also still carry real information in the one window that has no other reporting: if
the `eret` to EL1 fails you get `A3` and nothing else, since `hal: entry EL1` is printed *after* the
transition.

⇒ Row rewritten with the decode, so the supervised pass is a half-hour job rather than an
investigation.

## 1k. ✅ 2026-09-23 — branch backlog cleared, and what is left is a decision not a chore

22 stale local branches → **5**. Each deletion was checked with `merge-base --is-ancestor <b> master`
first, so nothing was removed that is not already in `master`.

Two needed `-D`: `git branch -d` refuses a branch that is merged into HEAD but not into its
**upstream tracking ref** — expected here, since our `master` is far ahead of upstream
phoenix-rtos. Confirmed ancestry in our master before forcing.

★ The five that remain are **not** cleanup. They hold unlanded work, and the row now says what:

| branch | ahead | what it claims |
|---|---|---|
| `devices/wip/rpi4-wifi-glom` | +3 | *"slot-based walk works (RX 0.14 → 3.0 MB/s)"* — a **21×** result not in master |
| `lwip/agent/rpi4-genet` | +115 | 2026-06 genet work |

`rpi4-wifi-glom` one is the interesting one: a 21× WiFi RX throughput improvement sitting outside
master since 2026-09-02.

ⓘ `sources/_build/` is a stray git repo rather than one of the 17 siblings; left untouched.

## 1l. ✅ 2026-09-23 — C6's app-side exposure removed without touching psh

The row's fix was recorded as owner-gated, because making psh wait for the clock delays the prompt on
every cold boot on every target. But that is the *systemic* fix. The **observed** failure — Quake III
printing `CL_InitCGame: -1201626.86 seconds` and rendering 0 frames — is an app timing its own
startup in **wall-clock** time, and that has an ungated fix.

`Sys_Milliseconds()` anchored `sys_timeBase` to its first `gettimeofday` reading and subtracted later
ones, so a step between the two poisons every subsequent duration — and the engine drives *all* its
timing off that function. Now `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` (`ports fd34552`).

★ **quake3 was the only port glue still using `gettimeofday`** — quakespasm, vkquake and sdl2 already
used monotonic. The one app with wall-clock timing is the one app that ever showed this failure.

### The property is measured, not assumed

"The header defines `CLOCK_MONOTONIC`" is not evidence: `clock_gettime` accepts the id either way,
and a libc that mapped it to realtime would look identical until the step. `tools/clockstep` steps
the wall clock by the real 56 years **on hardware**:

```
TAG-real-delta 1766015999858 ms    <- the full step
TAG-mono-delta 1000 ms             <- exactly the sleep(1), unmoved
TAG-verdict MONOTONIC-SURVIVED-THE-STEP
```

Confirmed in the source too: libphoenix's `clock_gettime` reads a raw timer plus a wall-clock
*offset* and adds the offset only for `CLOCK_REALTIME`, so a step moves only the offset.

Gate: `objdump` confirms `Sys_Milliseconds` calls `clock_gettime`; Quake III renders **270 frames at
53.8 fps**, 0 faults. Manifest `2026-09-23-quake3-monotonic.md`.

⚖ **Stated honestly: I did not observe the fix preventing a real step-induced failure** — that needs
the step to land inside the startup window, ~1 in 174 runs. What is established is that the exposure
is gone by construction and the property it rests on is measured. The psh-side fire-and-forget
remains for any *other* app that times across the step.

⚠ And one verification instrument of mine was void: I hashed
`prog.stripped/quake3` to prove the rebuild happened, but the binary is `quake3e` — the path did not
exist, `sha256sum` printed nothing, and "UNCHANGED" was a comparison of two empty strings. The same
"a check on a missing path passes" trap already in the skills. Settled instead by mtime plus an
`objdump` of the actual call.

## 1m. ✅ 2026-09-23 — libphoenix gains `swprintf`/`vswprintf` (D3, half closed)

`wchar.h` declared neither and `libc.a` exported neither, so any port needing wide formatted output
had to bring its own. Implemented (`libphoenix 07a0a29`): conversion specs are ASCII by
construction, so each is rebuilt as a narrow spec and rendered through `snprintf` then widened;
`%c`/`%lc` and `%s`/`%ls` are handled directly. Flags, width, precision, `*` for both, the
`hh h l ll j z t L` modifiers, `d i u o x X`, `f F e E g G a A`, `c s p n`, `%%`.

**Validated differentially against glibc's own `swprintf`, not against hand-written expectations** —
the latter would encode the same reading of the standard as the code and prove nothing. 34/34 match.
⚠ And the test was checked that it *can* fail: adopting snprintf's return contract turns 3 of them
red. Harness in `tools/swprintf-hosttest`.

Two traps, which are what the 11 target tests pin:

* **`swprintf` does NOT share `snprintf`'s contract.** It returns a **negative** value when `n` or
  more wide characters are requested (C99 7.24.2.7) — not the would-be length. Code ported from the
  narrow family that grows a buffer on a large return loops forever.
* **`%s` is MULTIBYTE, `%ls` is wide.** The reverse of what a lot of code assumes.

Gate: `wide_printf` **11/11**, full `test-libc-printf` **129 tests 0 failures**, STK still races
(7.39 fps), 0 faults, 0 allocator guards. Manifest `2026-09-23-swprintf.md`.

### ⚠ It broke the SuperTuxKart build, and then my own tooling broke the fix

STK's shim defines `static inline swprintf`, which in C++ is a hard error once `<wchar.h>` declares
it `extern`. Fixed by redirecting Irrlicht's call sites with a `#define` (`ports 6257d19`), keeping
today's behaviour byte for byte — the shim still earns its place because Irrlicht's `%s`-with-wide
usage is non-conforming.

↩ **The first attempt at that patch edit corrupted the patch file.** `irrTypes.h` is CRLF, so the
patch body is CRLF — and Python's text mode translates `\r\n` → `\n` on read and writes `\n` back,
silently rewriting **the whole file** to LF. It then failed to apply with *"different line
endings"*, which reads like a problem with the source tree rather than with my editor. Caught by
dry-running the **original** patch for comparison, redone in binary mode, and verified with
`patch --dry-run` against a fresh extract **before** spending another 40-minute STK build.

⇒ **Edit patch files in binary mode.** And when a patch suddenly fails to apply, diff your version
against the last good one before suspecting the tree.

## 1o. ↩ 2026-09-23 — two debt rows were wrong; merged into one, with the number that decides them

C1 is event-gated (§1p) and a soak cannot settle it in a night, so this stretch went to rows that
*can* move. D2 and D7 both turned out to be inaccurate, and they are the same item.

**They are one root cause, not four IDs.** Three TD-14 markers are live, each saying "restore the
fatal path once the underlying IPC slowness is rooted out":

| marker | site | what it softens |
|---|---|---|
| `TD-14-tty0-nonfatal` | `pl011-tty.c:1271` | `/dev/tty0` registration failure is non-fatal |
| `TD-14-console-alias` | `pl011-tty.c:1290` | an extra `portRegister()` alias beside `create_dev()` |
| `TD-14-ttyopen-nonfatal` | `pshapp.c:1701` | psh continues on inherited stdio (upstream: `return err`) |

The fourth, `TD-14-psh-ttyopen-errno`, is listed as "STILL ACTIVE diagnostic" but has **no marker in
source at all** — what it described is the errno print at `pshapp.c:1710`, which belongs to the third
item. One ID, not a fourth debt.

↩ **And the retry budget was misquoted in a way that inverts its meaning.** The registry says ours
deviates from "the upstream default (20 × 100 ms)". Upstream is actually **5 × 100 ms**:

```
ours:     PSH_TTYOPEN_RETRIES 50  x  PSH_TTYOPEN_RETRY_US 10000  = 500 ms
upstream: retries = 5            x  usleep(100000)               = 500 ms
```

**The same total budget.** Ours is not more tolerant — it polls ten times finer, so psh comes up
sooner when the device appears early, and is arguably the better default to upstream rather than
revert. The real deviation is the *non-fatal* treatment.

### The measurement that actually decides all three

Across **7 067 boot logs in the archive**:

| message | logs |
|---|---|
| `pl011-tty: tty0 register failed` | **0** |
| `pl011-tty: failed to register /dev/console` | **0** |
| `psh: ttyopen … failed` | **1** |

Sound evidence in this direction — unlike a presence check, each message is printed
**unconditionally on its own failure path**, so absence means the path did not run. The IPC
slowness these were written for is effectively gone (≈1 in 7 000).

⇒ Restoring the fatal paths is no longer "wait for latency to improve"; it is a decision about what
a ~1-in-7000 failure should do — **die, or boot degraded**. That is the owner's call, so the row now
carries the number instead of a guess. D7 merged into D2; the list is 28 issues.

## 1p. 2026-09-23 — C1: the instrument is now PROVEN, and the corrupting word reads as a float

**The instrument was only half-tested.** Last turn showed `hlo32`/`hhi32`/`hfixed` stay *silent* on a
healthy run — which says nothing about whether they are *right* when the corruption is there. C1
fires roughly once in 42 STK runs, so the next occurrence would have been the first time that code
ever executed. Fixed with an injection experiment in the existing host harness
(`tools/malloc-harness`, `hi32-write`): it reproduces the field **state** seen on hardware — a
four-byte write at `heap+4` — without pretending to reproduce the writer.

Predicted, then measured, and they match exactly:

```
injected: size 0x1000 -> 0x8000000100001000
malloc:   why   = 0x5
malloc:   hlo32 = 0x0000000000001000     <- the surviving legal size
malloc:   hhi32 = 0x0000000080000001     <- the corrupting word, as the 32-bit value it was
malloc:   hfixed= 0x0000000000000001     <- validates once the high half is cleared
```

### ★ And the two observed words are float bit patterns

| word | as `float32` | as `int32` |
|---|---|---|
| `0x80000000` | **`-0.0f`** | INT_MIN |
| `0x80000001` | smallest negative denormal | INT_MIN+1 |

`-0.0f` is one of the most common 32-bit values in 3D code (any negation of zero), and a tiny
denormal is what underflow produces. Every app that has ever shown this — SuperTuxKart, Quake II,
Quake III, the glamor desktop — is 3D. So **a stray 4-byte float store** is a more natural reading
than the refcount-with-flag-bit one, and it is the first hypothesis here that explains *why these
particular values*.

⚖ Stated as a hypothesis, not a finding: with n=2 both readings still fit. What discriminates is
more `hhi32` samples — values like `0xBF800000` (`-1.0f`) or `0x3F800000` (`1.0f`) would settle it
for floats; only ever `0x80000000`/`0x80000001` would keep the counter reading alive.

### Hunt in progress

C1 now needs an occurrence, so the Pi is running one. Trials are single-app cycles rotating across
the affected apps, each graded on evidence the app did work:

| trial | app | evidence the app worked | fires | faults |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| T1 | stk (4 laps) | 41 `flipstat`, 8.27 fps | 0 | 0 |
| T2 | stk (4 laps) | 41 `flipstat` | 0 | 0 |
| T3 | **quake2** | 45 `flipstat`, 192 frames @ 38.2 fps | 0 | 0 |
| T4 | **quake3** | 47 `flipstat`, 271 frames @ 54.2 fps | 0 | 0 |
| T5 | **X desktop** | 13 X log lines, 19 HDMI frames | 0 | 0 |
| T6 | stk (6 laps) | 45 `flipstat` | 0 | 0 |
| T7–T13 | stk (6 laps) ×7 | 45 `flipstat` each | 0 | 0 |

**13 trials, 0 guard lines, every one with the app's own work asserted** (frame counts, or X log
lines plus HDMI frames — never "no errors appeared").

⚖ **This bounds nothing, and the arithmetic says so.** Against the historical rate — one firing run
in 42 — seeing nothing in 13 has probability ≈ **0.73**. Reaching even p < 0.1 would need ~97 runs,
about ten hours of bench time, so a soak cannot settle C1 in one night and should not be reported as
if it had. Recorded this way so the count is not later mistaken for a clearance.

⇒ The soak's value is not the tally: it is that **whenever the next fire lands, it is now read by
tested code** (§1p) and arrives with `hlo32`/`hhi32`/`hfixed`. The hunt continues in the background
as the cheapest thing the idle bench can do; it is not the thing that will solve this.

## 1q. ✅ 2026-09-23 — C1 instrumentation gated (libphoenix `6d40cf7`); ⚠ one VOID run first

Gate: STK ran a real hacienda race — `flipstat 39 frames in 5072 ms = 7.69 fps`, repeated — with
**0 guard fires, 0 faults**, and the new fields correctly silent (they live in a branch that does
not execute when healthy). Manifest `2026-09-23-c1-heap-instrumentation.md`.

⚠ **The first attempt was a VOID RUN and would have read as a pass.** I launched
`/usr/bin/supertuxkart` directly; it died immediately on
`FileManager: Set $SUPERTUXKART_DATADIR`, and the cycle then reported 0 fires, 0 faults, psh
prompt — a perfectly clean-looking result in which **nothing exercised the allocator at all**. The
showcase gate launches `stk --track=hacienda --numkarts=4 --profile-laps=2`, a wrapper that sets the
data dir. Caught only by asking for the frame count and finding it empty.

⇒ For any allocator or app-level gate, **the pass condition must include evidence the app did work**
(frames, laps, records) — never "no errors appeared".

## 1r. ★★★ 2026-09-23 — C1 sharpened: it is FOUR BYTES at `heap+4`, not "heap corruption"

Re-reading the five `gate-stk` fires of 2026-09-22 — data already in the archive, no new run —
changes what this defect is.

| fires | heap | `hsize` | masked | chunk offset |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | `0x0c2f0000` | `0x80000000_0000d000` | **`0xd000`** ✓ legal | `0x8c28`–`0x8fc8` ✓ inside |
| 1 | `0x0b6a0000` | `0x80000001_0000d000` | **`0xd000`** ✓ legal | `0xbf98` ✓ inside |

**Mask the high 32 bits and both heaps are entirely self-consistent**: a legal size, every reported
chunk inside it, sane chunk sizes (`0x43`, `0x2b`), an ordinary grid. Only the **high half of
`heap->size`** is wrong — and `heap_t` is `{ size_t size; size_t freesz; … }`, so `size` sits at
**offset 0 of a page-aligned heap**. A wrong high half with an intact low half is a **four-byte
write at `heap+4`**.

⇒ The defect is not "the heap is corrupt". It is one field, in one heap, and then **every later
`free()` from that heap fails validation and leaks** — which is exactly why the fires arrive in
clusters and why nothing ever crashes.

★ **The corrupting word is not constant**: `0x80000000` and `0x80000001`, two different heaps in one
run. A fixed marker would be the same twice. Differing by one in the low bit reads like a **refcount
or lock word with a flag in the MSB**.

⛔ **And the residue is a DIFFERENT signature from the bulk, not less of it.** Across the whole
archive `hsize` is printed 24 231 times: **24 226 with a sane `0x2000`** and only **5 corrupt** — and
every high-count log (2 339 / 1 637 / 1 426 …) is dated **2026-09-12**, i.e. *before* the
BO-ownership fix landed that evening. So the pre-fix bulk was *a bad chunk in a good heap*; the
post-fix residue is *a good chunk in a bad heap*. That independently supports the row's existing
claim that the double-ownership route does not explain what is left.

**Instrumented for the next occurrence** (libphoenix `HEAD`): `hlo32`, `hhi32` and `hfixed` — does
the heap validate once the high half is cleared? `1` = one stray 4-byte write into an otherwise
intact live heap; `0` = the single-write story is dead. Printf-only, in a branch that does not run
when healthy.

⚠ **`hfixed` nearly shipped as a constant.** The obvious implementation calls `malloc_heapSizeValid()`
on a fixed-up *copy* — but that function also tests its argument for page alignment and
`[heapLo, heapHi)` membership, so a stack copy returns 0 **every time**. It would have looked like
evidence while being a fixed 0. It now applies the size tests to the real base.

⚠ **Staging trap, new:** a libphoenix change reaches **apps**, not `loader.disk`, so
`strings loader.disk` cannot verify it — check a userspace binary. And `--scope core` does **not**
sync the NFS export, so the staged `supertuxkart` was still the previous build until
`sync-netboot-tree.sh` ran. Same family as the stale `/etc/build-versions`.

## 1s. ✅ 2026-09-23 — the dumper guard is GATED BOTH WAYS (kernel `20179bfe`)

A guard that silences a crash dump is worse than the fault it prevents, so it is gated in both
directions — the fix must fire when it should **and stay quiet when it should not**. Second
reproducer written for exactly that: `tools/nullderef/`, which crashes with a **healthy** stack.

| reproducer | stack at `sp` | EL1 aborts | `stack:` section |
|---|---|---|---|
| `stackbomb` (overflow) | unmapped | **2 → 0** | `not mapped at 0000007fffeff000 (sp=0000007fffefff90) -- stack overflow?` |
| `nullderef` (control) | healthy | 0 | **full dump preserved** — `sp=…7fffffff40 from=…7ffffffec0 to=…8000000000` + words |

Both processes died, `ALIVE-1`/`ALIVE-2`/`ALIVE-AFTER-NULLDEREF` all ran, 1 EL0 abort on the control
(the null write, as intended). Without the control, "0 EL1 aborts" would equally have meant the dump
was suppressed for *everything* — a far worse regression than the bug.

Pushed; manifest `2026-09-23-stackdump-guard.md`.

## 1t. ↩↩ 2026-09-23 — C2 IS STALE: the stack-exhaustion DoS does not reproduce

The row said *"a userspace process that exhausts its 1 MiB stack takes down the kernel"* — a
userspace-triggerable availability bug, and the highest-severity thing on the list. **It is no
longer true.** Measured before designing anything, which is what the plan asked for and what turned
out to matter.

Reproducer built at **`-O0`** deliberately (`-O2` can flatten the recursion into a loop and never
overflow — a false "fixed"), and `objdump` confirms a real `bl <recurse>` survived. Assert the work
happened: the banner *did* print — it is interleaved into the dump, and the registers carry it as
ASCII (`x9` = `"sing 4Ki"`, `x13` = `"er stack"`).

| trial | bombs | EL1 aborts | system afterwards |
|---|---|---|---|
| `stackbomb1` | 1 | 2 | **`POST-BOMB-ALIVE` + a second command both ran** |
| `stackbomb2` | 1 | 2 | **`POST-BOMB-ALIVE`** |
| `stackbomb3` | **3 in one boot** | 6 | **`ALIVE-1`, `ALIVE-2`, `ALIVE-3` all ran** |

The process dies, psh keeps working, and repeated abuse in one boot changes nothing. In 2026-08-22
the follow-up `echo` never printed at all.

### And the fault is a different one — `hal_cpuPushSignal` is not in it

`addr2line` on the shipped kernel named the site in one command:

```
pc=0xffffffffc000873c -> exceptions_dumpUserStack  hal/aarch64/exceptions.c:302
esr=0x96000007   <- WnR=0, a READ  (the 2026-08-22 evidence was 0x96000047, a WRITE)
far=0x7fffefff10 == sp(0x7fffefff90) - 128
```

`sp - 128` is exactly the first word `exceptions_dumpUserStack()` reads. So the EL1 abort is **the
crash dumper reading the user stack**, not the signal-frame memcpy the work-order describes — and
the kernel recovers from it. `hal_cpuPushSignal` is still unguarded in the source, but the
reproducer never reaches it.

⇒ **C2 rewritten** from "userspace-triggerable kernel DoS" to what is actually there. The severity
drops from *availability* to *diagnostic quality*: a stack-overflow crash prints two confusing
nested dumps instead of one useful one.

**Fixed anyway (kernel `HEAD`), because a kernel dereferencing unmapped memory is wrong regardless.**
The function's own comment already said *"a nested fault taken here at EL1 would turn every
userspace crash into a board reset"* and clipped the range to `sp`'s page so a read "that starts on
a mapped page cannot walk off it" — it just never checked that the starting page **is** mapped, and
in a stack overflow it cannot be. Guard is `at s1e1r` + `PAR_EL1.F`: asks the MMU, takes no lock —
which matters in exception context, where pmap's spinlock could deadlock against whatever was
interrupted.

## 1v. ✅ 2026-09-22 — C3 FIXED on the third attempt: refuse a busy unmount

The first two fixes (§1w) both tried to **drain** requests in flight. That cannot work, and the A/B
said so twice before I understood why: **the writer holds the file open and keeps sending new
requests**, so between two of `dd`'s writes the in-flight count is legitimately zero and any drain
is satisfied. Draining answers "is anything happening right now", when the question is "is anything
still using this".

The open *count* is the stable signal, and libext2 already had it without knowing: `ext2_open()`
takes a reference it never releases and `ext2_close()` nets it back, so `refs` **is** an
open-descriptor count. `ext2_objs_busy()` (filesystems `HEAD`) reports it; umass asks through a new
optional `busy` callback and returns **`-EBUSY`**, which is what Linux does and what callers expect.
Checked first, on the device thread, before anything is torn down — so a refusal leaves the mount
exactly as it was.

### The A/B, all four runs on the same harness

| run | build | `umount` during the write | data |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | master | **rc=0 at t=3** | `Invalid argument`, **5 of 200 MiB** |
| B | + pool-thread drain | rc=0 at t=3 | unchanged |
| C | + wait before `portDestroy` | rc=0 at t=2 | writer **hangs forever** |
| **D** | **+ `-EBUSY` on open files** | **rc=1 at t=3, refused** | **`200+0 records`, all 200 MiB** |

```
umass: /dev/umass0 is busy (1 object(s) still open) -- not unmounting
TAG-umount-during rc=1 t=3
TAG-dd-done rc=0 t=124      200+0 records in / 200+0 records out
TAG-umount-after rc=0 t=125
```

0 faults. And the unmount is **refused, not broken** — the retry once the writer finished returned 0.

**Verified by reading the data back, not by trusting `dd`'s own count** (one instrument in the
script *did* fail — `TAG-size` printed `none` because its `ls -l | awk` did not work, which is
exactly why the read-back matters). Remount: `race.bin` is **209 715 200 bytes**, exactly 200 MiB,
and `dd` reads it back `200+0 records`, 0 faults.

Host suite is **10 harnesses, ALL GREEN**, including the new `busy` predicate tested in both
directions — the check that matters is that a **cached-but-closed** object is *not* busy, because
counting those would have made every unmount fail forever.

## 1u. ✅ 2026-09-22 — C3 GATED FROM A PRISTINE FILESYSTEM, merged and pushed

The first `e2fsck` of the stick came back **dirty** — 7 orphaned inodes with `i_blocks == 0` but
real blocks allocated, and a used-inode count of **4294967198** (a 32-bit underflow). That is not a
regression: it is the **on-disk fingerprint of the broken runs**, A and C, which tore the
filesystem down under live writes. Rather than argue that, I reformatted and re-ran.

`mke2fs` on the host to the stick's exact geometry (2 GiB, 1 KiB blocks, `PHXTEST1`), written by
Phoenix itself (`2048+0 records`, **17.9 MB/s**, 0 faults), then the race again, then imaged back:

| | files | blocks |
|---|---|---|
| pristine reference | 11 | 134 779 |
| after the race | **12** (+`race.bin`) | **340 384** |

**`e2fsck -fn` rc=0, completely clean.** And the arithmetic closes: the delta is **205 605** 1 KiB
blocks, of which 204 800 is the file's data, leaving **805** — exactly the single- and
double-indirect blocks a 200 MiB file needs at this block size. Fully allocated, correctly
accounted, no orphans, no leaks.

Merged to `devices master` (`7315705`) and `filesystems master` (`522ecc8`), `--scope core` built,
blob verified by `strings`, both pushed. Manifest `2026-09-22-umass-busy-umount.md`.
**C3 removed from `docs/KNOWN-ISSUES.md`** (29 open issues remain).

## 1w. ⛔ 2026-09-22 — TWO failed fixes for the umount race. Stopped; branch is WIP.

The A/B works and the defect is real (§1x). **Neither of my fixes closed it**, and the second made
it worse. Recording that rather than leaving a branch that looks finished.

| run | devices | result |
|---|---|---|
| `umountrace-A` | `d593201` (master) | `umount` rc=0 at **t=3**, `dd: Invalid argument`, **5 of 200 MiB** written |
| `umountrace-B` | `4b64bc9` (pool-thread drain) | rc=0 at **t=3** — unchanged |
| `umountrace-C` | `8cdd927` (+ wait before `portDestroy`) | rc=0 at **t=2**, and now the writer **never returns** — `TAG-dd-done` never prints and the capture window expires |

**What I got wrong.** The pool-thread use-after-free is real, but it is not the mechanism this test
exposes. `_umass_partUnmount` runs on the *device* message thread and waits on `fsthrRunning`, which
`umass_fsthr` clears as soon as it has **queued** the mtUmount — so `portDestroy()` runs about a
millisecond later, under live requests. Fixing the free without fixing the port teardown changed
nothing; then making `portDestroy` wait turned "fails fast and loses data" into "the writer hangs",
which is a worse failure.

⚠ **A verification gap found on the way:** `/etc/build-versions` on the NFS root is written by the
*sync* step, which `--scope core` does not run — so it read `d593201` for all three runs while the
TFTP loader was correctly fresh each time. I confirmed the right code was running by `strings` on
`loader.disk`, not by the banner. **Do not grade which build is running by `build-versions` on a
netboot cycle.**

⇒ Branch `agent/umass-umount-drain` pushed as work-in-progress, **not merged**; devices master and
the bench loader are back to `d593201` and verified clean (`strings` finds none of the WIP code).
The defect is now issue **C3** in `docs/KNOWN-ISSUES.md`.

## 1y. ↩ 2026-09-22 — I said "no stick is plugged in". IT WAS. My check was wrong.

I graded "is a USB stick present?" by grepping the boot log for `umass`/`Mass Storage` and finding
one line. There was no stick in that answer because **the string I looked for is not how presence is
reported** — absence of a log line is not absence of a device. Owner corrected it; the stick had
been plugged in the whole time.

The evidence was in the *same logs I grepped*, both lanes, all day:

```
xhci: port 2 PORTSC=0x00281203 ccs=1 ped=1 speed=4 pls=0     <- speed=4 = SuperSpeed, connected+enabled
xhci: bulk-OUT pipe ready slot=2 ep=4 maxpkt=1024 burst=4
xhci: bulk-IN  pipe ready slot=2 ep=3 maxpkt=1024 burst=4
```

`maxpkt=1024 burst=4` is the SuperSpeed mass-storage signature this log already records in §0.

Asking the Pi instead of the log settles it in one command (`usbprobe`, 19:26):

| check | result |
|---|---|
| `/bin/ls /dev` | **`umass0`**, **`umass1`**, plus `usb-0781-5581-if00` (SanDisk), `usb-046d-c31c` (kbd), `usb-093a-2510` (mouse) |
| `/bin/dd if=/dev/umass0 of=/dev/null bs=1M count=8` | **`8+0 records in/out`** |
| faults | **0** |

No regression, no hardware fault. ⛔ **The rule this breaks is already in my own memory** — *"a
content check on a MISSING path greps clean"*. A presence check must ask the system for the thing
(`ls /dev`), never ask a log for a word. And the cost was not just a wrong sentence: I wrote a whole
handoff around "needs the owner to plug a stick in" for work I could have gated immediately.

⇒ So the umass unmount fix **is** gatable now, and §1e-resolved's A/B is being run rather than
deferred.

## 1z. ✅ 2026-09-22 evening — `f_fsid` fixed and gated; the card reflashed

**`ext2_statfs()` returned a live heap pointer as the filesystem id** (`filesystems 47634da`):

```c
st->f_fsid = (unsigned long)fs;   /* TODO: filesystem ID should be generated at mount time */
```

That is the address of libext2's own `ext2_t`. Wrong twice over — it leaks a pointer out of the
filesystem server to every caller of `statvfs()`, and it changes on each mount of the same
filesystem, so it identified the *mount attempt* rather than the thing mounted. Now folded from the
on-disk superblock UUID: stable across mounts, unique per filesystem, and nothing needs generating
at mount time. `f_flag` left at 0 deliberately — the `-r` in the emmc syspage entry is the
*driver's* flag; libext2 holds no mount-options state, so reporting `ST_RDONLY` would be inventing
an answer.

**The regression test is falsifiable, and I checked that rather than assuming it.** With the fix
stashed, `attrtest` fails 2 of its 5 new checks (`f_fsid is not the heap pointer`, `f_fsid survives
a remount`) and passes both with it. Host suite **ALL GREEN**.

### The gate needed an ext2 root, so the card was reflashed

`df` only exercises `ext2_statfs` where ext2 is actually mounted, and on the netboot lane the card
enumerates but nothing mounts it. So: `--variant sd` build → qemu verify (*"image PASSES — safe to
flash"*) → stage on the NFS root → rebuild the netboot loader → flash from Phoenix itself → drop
dnsmasq → SD boot. **`1093+1 records out`, 1 146 849 280 bytes in 91.6 s = 12.5 MB/s**, exactly the
image size. That also puts this morning's driver cleanup on the card, which the last handoff noted
was missing.

| check | result |
|---|---|
| lane | SD (`mmcblk0p2:ext2` present, no `nfs;/`) |
| running build | `filesystems 47634da` + `devices d593201` |
| `df /` — **two tools**, coreutils and busybox | both printed |
| the numbers | `1051314` blocks / `813015` used — **exactly the `e2fsck` reference**, so statfs is right, not merely plausible |
| `2 partition(s)` | yes |
| `DMAWR` / `MBR probe` / `not a usable MBR` | 0 / 0 / 0 |
| `test_sparse /root` | **OK, 0 failures** — 18 checks incl. `trunc-holes`, `trunc-tail` |
| faults | **0** |

Manifest: `2026-09-22-fsid-and-card-reflash.md`. Netboot restored as the default lane.

## 1e-resolved. ★★★ 2026-09-22 — the lock-order question ANSWERED, and it found a real UAF in umass

§1e below left the reachability of the inversion open — *"whether that can happen lives in the
storage framework outside libext2"*. That was answerable by reading, and I should have read it then
rather than filing it. The answer differs between the two consumers of libext2, and **one of them is
broken**.

### ✅ libstorage / `bcm2711-emmc` (our SD lane): unreachable BY DESIGN

`storage_umountfs()` drains before it unmounts (`libstorage/storage.c:489`):

```c
requestctx_stop(&fsctx->reqctx);     /* state = stop; while (ctx->nreqs) condWait(...) */
err = fsctx->handler->umount(strg->fs);
```

and `nreqs` brackets the *handler call itself*, not just the receive (`storage.c:217-233`):

```c
ctx->nreqs++;
mutexUnlock(ctx->lock);
ctx->msgHandler(ctx->data, &req->msg);      /* -> fs->ops->read/write/... */
...
if ((--ctx->nreqs == 0) && (ctx->state == state_stop)) condSignal(ctx->scond);
```

So no request can be inside libext2 when `libext2_storage_umount()` runs. The inversion is
unreachable here, and so is the bigger hazard below. This is the right shape.

### 🐞 umass (the USB stick): NO drain — `free(fs)` can run under a live request

`umass` has **two** pool threads (`UMASS_N_POOL_THREADS = 2`, `umass.c:54`, started at
`umass.c:1487`) popping from one shared `rqueue`, and `umass_poolthr` does
(`umass.c:936-942`):

```c
if (req->msg.type == mtUmount) {
        req->part->fs->unmount(req->part->fdata);   /* -> libext2_unmount -> free(fs) */
        ...
}
else {
        req->part->fs->handler(req->part->fdata, &req->msg);   /* -> libext2_handler */
}
```

Nothing waits for in-flight requests. The FIFO orders **dequeue**, not **completion**: pool thread A
can still be inside `libext2_handler` — holding `fs->lock` — when pool thread B dequeues the
`mtUmount` and calls `libext2_unmount()`, which does `ext2_objs_destroy` → `ext2_gdt_destroy` →
`ext2_sb_destroy` → **`free(fs)`**. Thread A then keeps using freed memory and finally unlocks a
destroyed mutex.

This is worse than the lock-order inversion §1e was about, and it is **not a libext2 bug** — a
filesystem library cannot defend itself against being freed mid-call. The fix belongs at the caller
and libstorage already shows its shape: count active requests and drain before dispatching the
unmount.

⚠ **Not gated, because no stick is plugged in** (`fsyncgate` shows `usb: Initializing driver as
host-side: umass` and no `umass0`). Written and syntax-checked on branch
`agent/umass-umount-drain`, ready for the hot-plug session.

### ↩ And a retraction inside §1e itself

§1e says the `_` convention is violated because `ext2_objs_destroy()` calls `ext2_obj_sync()` (takes
`obj->lock`) in one branch and `_ext2_obj_destroy()` in the other. **That is wrong.**
`_ext2_obj_destroy()` calls `ext2_obj_truncate()` (`obj.c:275`), which takes `obj->lock` itself — so
**both** branches acquire `obj->lock` under `objs->lock`. The nesting is uniform and there is no
asymmetry to fix. I nearly "fixed" a consistency problem that does not exist; the check was reading
the callee rather than trusting the name.

## 1e. ⚖ REVIEW FINDING (not changed): a lock-order inversion in libext2

Concurrency is the one area the host harness **structurally cannot** test — my shim makes
`mutexLock` a no-op — so this came from reading, and I am reporting rather than fixing it.

Two orders exist in the same code:

| path | order |
|---|---|
| `ext2_lookup()`, `ext2_unlink()` | hold **`dir->lock`** → call `ext2_obj_get()`, which takes **`objs->lock`** |
| `ext2_objs_destroy()` | hold **`objs->lock`** → reach **`obj->lock`** (via `ext2_obj_sync()` in one branch, and `ext2_obj_truncate()` inside `_ext2_obj_destroy()` in the other) |

Classic inversion: a thread unmounting holds `objs->lock` and wants some directory's lock, while a
thread in `unlink` holds that same directory's lock and wants `objs->lock`.

⚠ **Reachability is NARROWER than I first wrote.** `libext2_handler()` holds **`fs->lock` across
every operation** — which is why the allocators in `block.c`/`inode.c` do not take it themselves —
so two *requests* can never be inside libext2 at once. The inversion therefore needs
`libext2_storage_umount()` to run concurrently with an in-flight request specifically, and whether
that can happen lives in the storage framework outside libext2. If unmount is serialised against
the message loop, it is theoretical.

Note also the asymmetry that made it visible: `ext2_objs_destroy()` calls `_ext2_obj_destroy()`
(underscore = caller holds `objs->lock`) in one branch but `ext2_obj_sync()` (no underscore, takes
`obj->lock`) in the other. **The `_` convention is doing real work in this file and one call site
does not follow it.**

**Why I have not touched it:** fixing it means changing lock discipline I have no way to exercise,
and I have introduced two defects today by fixing things (the backwards walk, and the dirty flag
that reached a use-after-free). Both were caught by the harness; a lock-ordering mistake would not
be. Options if you want it addressed: hold `objs->lock` per-iteration rather than across the whole
teardown loop, or establish and document a single order.

## 1f. 🧭 WHERE TO PICK THIS UP (written for whoever reads next, including me)

**Gated and done:** ext2 defects 12-21 (four data-destroying), libcache
skip-identical-writeback (12.6x), the `bcm2711-emmc` short read (`dd` to EOF now byte-exact),
the six-app showcase 6/6 on the SD lane **verified frame by frame**, and the host suite
`tools/libext2-hosttest/run-all.sh` **ALL GREEN** — 8 harnesses x 2 block sizes, randomised, and
2/4-thread concurrency with real mutexes.

**In flight:** the durability chain (`libphoenix a1bce81` + `kernel 482b54c2` + `ext2 017eb3a`)
is flashed and booted clean; the residue measurement is the last step.

**Open, each with its reason — none of these is an oversight:**

| item | why it is open |
|---|---|
| 1-inode/1-block SD residue | **characterised, not attributed.** `e2fsck` flags three inodes while the used count moved by one; those do not reconcile and I will not invent a mechanism. Next: from a fresh flash, one boot running *only* `mkdir`/`rmdir`+`sync` and another running *only* `test_sparse`+`sync`, to split it |
| lock-order inversion in libext2 | reachable only if unmount races an in-flight request, which lives in the storage framework. Concurrency is now testable (`-DSHIM_REAL_MUTEX`) and libext2 is **clean** as actually driven |
| `storage_write()` `-EINVAL` | no measured symptom, and a short write on a storage device silently truncates the caller's data — choosing between that and `ENOSPC` is a design call |
| `f_fsid = (unsigned long)fs` | `ext2_statfs()` hands a **heap pointer** to userspace; carries a `TODO` already |
| comment style on the ext2 fixes | 104 of 265 added lines are comments, many narrating the defect. House style on published code is the owner's call |
| `fsync()` on a raw block-device fd | the kernel oid fix is **not yet gated**; SQLite is the natural test since it calls `fsync()` for durability |

**The one thing to carry forward if nothing else:** every defect from #12 on came from asking
*what can the current test not see* — sparse files gave seven, directories one, hard links the
object-lifetime bug, `setattr` the directory-eater. Three areas came back clean and are recorded as
such. The question is the tool.

## 1c. 📋 ext2 COVERAGE as it now stands — what is tested, and what is clean

Every defect from #12 on came from asking *what the current test cannot see*, so here is the
answer to that question as of now. All host-side, ASan+UBSan, `e2fsck` as oracle.

| area | harness | result |
|---|---|---|
| sequential create/write/read/delete | `harness.c` | PASS, 1 KiB + 4 KiB |
| sparse files, holes, truncate | `stress.c` | **7 defects (12-18)** → clean |
| random file ops + **remount** | `stress.c` | 12 seeds x 800 ops, 0 mismatches, 0 `e2fsck` |
| truncate tail leak | `tail.c` | **defect 15** → clean, aligned control |
| directories, nesting, rmdir | `dirstress.c` | **defect 19** → 0/10 seeds |
| hard links + symlinks (short/long) | `linkstress.c` | **defect 20** → clean |
| object teardown, case by case | `uaf.c` | **defect 20** → 5/5 cases clean |
| **directory spanning many blocks** | `bigdir.c` | **no defect** — 800 entries, 23 blocks at 1 KiB |
| **device nodes / FIFO / socket** | `devnode.c` | **no defect** — truncate correctly refused, canary intact |
| **`setattr` paths incl. `atSize`** | `attrtest.c` | **defect 21** → clean |
| **`statfs`** | `attrtest.c` | **no defect** — free count moves by 257 blocks for 256 KiB at 1 KiB (256 data + 1 indirect) |
| **concurrency, 2 and 4 threads** | `concurrent.c` + `-DSHIM_REAL_MUTEX` | **no defect** — consistent + `e2fsck` clean, both block sizes |
| block↔group conversion helpers | `blockmath.c` | exact inverses at `fstBlock` 0 and 1 |
| libcache read shapes | `libcache-hosttest` | clean incl. 16 MiB contiguous |
| libcache ranged write-back | `libcache-hosttest` | 22.2x, matches hardware's 21x |

**One command runs all of it:** `./tools/libext2-hosttest/run-all.sh [seeds]` — eight harnesses,
both block sizes, randomised and concurrent runs, ~2 minutes. Verified ALL GREEN against the
current tree. Run it **before** spending a Pi cycle on any libext2/libcache change.

↩ **"Concurrency needs more than a host harness" was WRONG** — that was only true of my shim, which
made `mutexLock` a no-op. `-DSHIM_REAL_MUTEX` gives real pthread **ERRORCHECK** mutexes (not
recursive: Phoenix's are not, so a recursive acquire should abort rather than be tolerated). It
matters because the umass driver dispatches with **`UMASS_N_MSG_THREADS = 2`**. Result: 2 and 4
threads at both block sizes, all threads consistent, `e2fsck` clean — **libext2 is safe as it is
actually driven**, and it is also clean single-threaded under ERRORCHECK, so no path self-deadlocks.

⚠ **Two test bugs on the way, both of which looked like serious defects:**
1. The first version called the API **without `fs->lock`**, which the real handler always holds. It
   produced content mismatches and `e2fsck` **"Multiply-claimed block(s) in inode 24: 535"** — two
   files sharing a block. A pure artefact of the harness not modelling its caller.
2. Then 93 "content differs" failures — **identical at both block sizes**. A race varies; a
   deterministic count does not. A recreated file reset `lens[i]` but not `model[i]`.

Also a `gcc` failure was masked once by a stale binary that ran anyway and produced "results"; the
build is now guarded on its exit status.

**Still uncovered:** only **power-loss / crash-consistency**, which genuinely needs more than a
host harness.

⚠ **One hygiene item for you, noticed while reading `ext2_statfs()`:** it reports
`st->f_fsid = (unsigned long)fs` — a live **heap pointer handed to userspace**. It already carries
a `TODO` saying the id should be generated at mount time. Harmless functionally, but this code is
going to be published, and leaking an allocator address through a public API is the kind of thing a
reviewer will stop on. I have **not** changed it: nothing depends on the value, the TODO shows it
is known, and picking the replacement (superblock UUID? a mount counter?) is your call.

★ Writing this list down paid for itself within the hour: `setattr` was on it, and reading that
path found **defect 21**, which destroyed a directory and returned success. Device nodes were on it
too and turned out clean. Both outcomes are worth the same — the list is what turns "we should
look at that sometime" into a decision about where to look next.

⚠ `bigdir` is a **negative** result and is recorded deliberately. Directory growth held none of
the defects the equivalent file-side machinery did, and knowing an area is clean is worth as much
as a bug when deciding where to look next.

## 1b. ⚖ ONE THING FOR YOU TO DECIDE — comment style in the published ext2 code

The seven defect fixes add **265 lines, of which 104 (39%) are comments**, and many narrate the
defect rather than the invariant — e.g. "`block + i`, not `i`: ... Without it a run terminated by a
HOLE was flushed to the physical block of file block i, the data was lost ... the other three
flush sites always had it; this one did not."

`docs/knowledge/code-quality-and-upstreaming.md` §3.3 asks for **limited** comments that explain
*why*, and calls "generic prose around simple code" a bad comment. By that reading the first
sentence of each of mine earns its place and the forensic tail belongs in the commit message,
where it already is.

⚖ But the same doc says new code should "look like a natural extension of nearby Phoenix code",
and the **adjacent ext2 code already carries exactly this style** (`ext2_block_init()`'s
hole-reads-the-superblock comment, written earlier this week). Trimming mine would make the file
internally inconsistent.

I have **not** changed them: it is a 104-line no-functional-benefit diff over code that was gated
an hour ago, and the house style is yours to set. Say the word and I will cut each to its first
sentence. Nothing else is blocked on this.

## 2z. 2026-09-22 — cleanup owed from the SD work (plan §2 item 2), and the fsync gate re-scoped

Three items were owed. All three are decided; two changed code.

**1. ⛔ H2 `DMAWR dspin` probe — REMOVED (`devices 97959be`).** It printed up to three
`sdcard: DMAWR blocks=… dspin=… pres=… intr=…` lines per boot to test "the DAT-idle poll is not
really waiting, so the CMD13 after a write reconfigures the controller underneath a live SDMA
transfer". H2 died when the write path was root-caused to the **bus-address** bug (raw CPU-phys
instead of the `emmc2bus` address): ADMA2 now ships both directions at 12.7-12.9 MB/s **with that
same poll in place**, which refutes it directly. The poll and its two DO-NOT comments stay — they
are the completion mechanism, and they record a revert that cost a bisect.

**2. ⚖ MBR probe — KEPT, but converted from a per-boot dump to a failure-path classifier.** The
plan asked whether it stays; the answer is "the information stays, the debris does not". As
written it was a `DIAGNOSTIC 2026-09-19` `printf` on **every** boot — and it was also *weak* in the
form it had, because a healthy card prints `first8=0000000000000000` too (the bootstrap area of a
non-bootable MBR is zeroed), so "all-zero ⇒ the transfer never wrote the buffer" would have
mis-classified a good boot. It now prints only when sector 0 read fine but yielded **no usable
partition table** — the one SD failure that is otherwise completely silent, since the lane just
cannot find p2 and nothing logs an error. A healthy boot says nothing extra.

**3. ✅ `run-showcase-gate.sh` reused-label warning — added**, matching `test-cycle-bench.sh`. The
per-app log pick was already safe (it compares against a `mktemp` marker's mtime); the **HDMI
frames were not**, and the 09:49 gate proved it — `check-torch-rois.py` reported *"114 older
frame(s) under this label belong to earlier runs and were NOT scored"*. The warning now says that
up front instead of leaving it in the scorer's output.

### ↩ And the outstanding `fsync()` gate was scoped wrong in the manifest

The manifest said *"SQLite is the natural test, since it calls `fsync()` for durability"*. **It is
not.** SQLite `fsync()`s **regular files**, and those reach `libext2_handler`, which dispatches
`libext2_sync(fdata)` and **never looks at `msg->oid`** — so the zeroed oid could not bite them.
SQLite was hit by the *other two* levels of that bug (the empty `sync()` stub, and the missing
`mtSync` case), both already fixed and gated.

The kernel fix bites exactly one thing: **`fsync()` on a raw block-device fd**. And there the old
behaviour is not "synced the wrong device" but something sharper, readable straight off the source
with no cycle needed — `storage_sync()` gates the flush on

```c
IS_BLOCK_DEVICE_ID(id)   /* ((id) & DEVTYPE_BLOCK) != 0 */
```

which is **false for id 0**, so control fell through to the `else` and returned **`-EINVAL`
unconditionally**, whatever id 0 happened to resolve to. So `fsync()` on `/dev/mmcblk0*` was a
hard, always-on failure, not an intermittent or subtle one. Manifest row and
`PHOENIX-RTOS-RPI4-CHANGES.md` entry 20 both corrected.

Gate (netboot lane, so the card enumerates but `/` is NFS and `/dev/mmcblk0p2` is an unmounted raw
device): `sync /dev/mmcblk0p2` must be silent, with `sync /nonexistent` as the **positive control**
that proves this build reports errors at all — an absence-of-error test with no control is not a
measurement.

⚠ And `sync` is not a straight line to `fsync()`: coreutils does `fcntl(F_GETFL)` / `fcntl(F_SETFL)`
between the open and the sync and **bails out if either fails**, exiting non-zero without ever
calling `fsync`. So the grade is by *which* tagged line appears — `error opening` / `couldn't reset
non-blocking mode` (**void**, it measured `fcntl`) / `error syncing` / silent — never by rc. Recipe
recorded in the `rpi4-storage-test` skill.

### Build verification (`devices 97959be` + `d593201`, `--scope core`, BUILD_RC=0)

Both changes are core, so the blob was checked by `strings` rather than by the build log, with a
positive control so a stale read cannot pass:

| string | want | got |
|---|---|---|
| `DMAWR` (removed probe) | 0 | **0** |
| `MBR probe` (old per-boot dump) | 0 | **0** |
| `not a usable MBR` (new classifier) | ≥1 | **1** |
| `ADMA2 error` (positive control) | 1 | **1** |
| `check-netboot-blob.sh` | `nfsroot` | **`rootfs: nfsroot`** |

⚠ The first build needed doing twice: the MBR edit landed *after* the core stage had already
compiled `sdstorage_dev.c`, and the `strings` check is what caught it — `not a usable MBR` came back
**0** while `DMAWR` was correctly 0. Exactly the failure mode the skill warns about, caught by the
check rather than by luck.

### ✅ The power-cut model now RUNS, and it comes back identical to pristine

`noumount.c` was committed earlier today with a header and no result. It now runs, and it is in
`run-all.sh` (9 harnesses, still **ALL GREEN**). It matters because every other harness ends with
`ext2_objs_destroy()` — which *is* an unmount — so none of them modelled the SD root, which is `/`
and is never unmounted. This one does the workload and stops, like a power cut.

| mode | 1 KiB | 4 KiB |
|---|---|---|
| 1 — mkdir/rmdir | rc=0, e2fsck=0, **residue none** | same |
| 2 — create/write/unlink | rc=0, e2fsck=0, **residue none** | same |
| 3 — both (what the SD gate ran) | rc=0, e2fsck=0, **residue none** | same |

"residue none" means the image's file and block counts come back **equal to a pristine `mke2fs` of
the same geometry** — 11/4096 files, 140/12288 blocks at 4 KiB. So a create-and-delete workload that
dies without unmounting leaves libext2's bookkeeping exactly as it found it: no orphan-list entries,
no leaked inodes, no leaked blocks. That is independent corroboration of this morning's retraction —
the residue on the card could not have been libext2, and it was `/var/tmp`.

⚠ **The grader is graded.** `e2fsck` clean is not a pass here, because a filesystem that never
allocated anything is also clean; the comparison is against the pristine control, and the tagged
`(ino N)` lines assert the workload ran. Checked by running mode 9, which does nothing: rc=0,
e2fsck=0, residue none — and it **fails**, on `allocations=0`.

⚖ Scope: the host harness `pwrite`s straight through, so this tests **libext2's bookkeeping** under
a power cut, not the device cache. That is the right split — the device half is `LIBCACHE_WRITE_THROUGH`
plus the `fsync` chain below.

### ✅ HARDWARE GATE PASSED — `fsyncgate`, netboot lane, 10:40

One cycle covers both the cleanup and the outstanding `fsync()` question.
Log: `artifacts/rpi4b-uart/rpi4b-uart-20260922-104023-fsyncgate.log`, `CYCLE_RC=0`, **0 faults**.

| check | want | got |
|---|---|---|
| lane | netboot | `nfs;/;10.42.0.1;/;v4;takeover`, 0 hits for `mmcblk0p2:ext2` |
| running build | the one just made | `/etc/build-versions`: `phoenix-rtos-devices d59320126c2` |
| card still enumerates | `2 partition(s)` | **`/dev/mmcblk0 ready: 60905 MiB, 2 partition(s)`** |
| `DMAWR …` probe lines | 0 | **0** |
| old per-boot `MBR probe` dump | 0 | **0** |
| new `not a usable MBR` classifier | 0 (the card is healthy) | **0** |
| DMA read path after the removal | `32+0 records` | **`32+0 records in/out`** |
| **`sync /dev/mmcblk0p2`** (= `fsync` on a raw block fd) | silent | **silent** |
| `sync /dev/mmcblk0` | silent | **silent** |
| `error syncing …` | 0 | **0** |
| `couldn't reset non-blocking …` (the VOID marker) | 0 | **0** |
| **`sync /nonexistent-control-xyz`** (positive control) | 1 | **`sync: error opening '/nonexistent-control-xyz': No such file or directory`** |

So `fsync()` on a raw block-device fd now **succeeds**, and the control proves this build does
report errors, so the silence is a result and not a missing test.

⚖ **Stated precisely: the *new* behaviour is observed, the *old* behaviour is deduced.** I did not
boot the pre-fix kernel to watch it fail — the deduction is from source and is not a judgement call
(`IS_BLOCK_DEVICE_ID(0)` is `(0 & DEVTYPE_BLOCK) != 0`, which is false, so the `else` returns
`-EINVAL` no matter what id 0 resolves to). Re-proving that empirically costs a kernel revert, a
build and a cycle for a claim the compiler already settles; if anyone wants it observed, that is
the recipe.

## 2. PLAN (2026-09-21, owner: "continue on all of these, autonomously")

Ordered by value/risk. One Pi cycle at a time; correctness gates before anything moves.

**A. Metadata write latency — 185 ms/file.** The biggest speed lever left.
   A1 ✅ **DONE — decomposed, and it is NOT the shell and NOT simply `sb_sync`.** Same 200-file loop,
      counts asserted (a loop over a missing dir reports 0 s — the first attempt did exactly that):

      | target | 200 files | per file |
      |---|---|---|
      | RAM (`/tmp`, dummyfs) | 1 s | **5 ms** |
      | umass1 create only (`: > f`) | 8 s | **40 ms** |
      | umass1 create + 11-byte write (`echo >`) | 39 s | **195 ms** |

      Shell/IPC is ~5 ms ⇒ irrelevant. The inode+dirent path is 40 ms. **The 11-byte data write adds
      ~155 ms.** That weakens the `ext2_sb_sync()` theory on its own: `sb_sync` runs in the create
      path too, and create is only 40 ms. The cost is specific to `_ext2_file_write()`.
   A1x (superseded) **Decompose before optimising:** run the identical 200-file loop on `/tmp` (RAM) as well as
      on `umass1`. If RAM is also slow it is shell/IPC, not storage, and the whole `sb_sync` theory
      is wrong. No build needed.
   A2 Then count device operations per file, not bytes.
   A3 Only then change anything. Candidate: `ext2_sb_sync()` runs on **every** `_ext2_file_write()`,
      every inode create and every destroy. Linux keeps the superblock dirty and writes it on
      unmount — the counts in it are advisory and fsck rebuilds them. Correctness-sensitive, so it
      gets its own A/B + `e2fsck` gate.

**B. Finish the busybox-unwinding thread. ✅ DONE 2026-09-22** — registered (`58a9cf5`), built
   (`--with-ports` rc=0), installed to `/usr/bin`, synced to the NFS root, and **verified on
   hardware by round trip**: grep selects, sed substitutes, gzip/bzip2/xz compress→decompress
   `cmp`-identical, tar and tar.gz create→extract identical. `grep (GNU grep) 3.11` and
   `tar (GNU tar) 1.35` printed, so these are the GNU binaries and not the busybox shadow.

**C. Upstream sweep 11 adoption. ✅ DONE 2026-09-22** — merged, `pl011-tty` ported, gated
   (boot + interactive bash + fbcon, 0 faults; showcase 6/6 verified visually), master
   fast-forwarded in all four repos, manifest `2026-09-22-sweep11-adopted.md`, pushed.
   All four siblings are now **behind=0** against upstream.

**D. Hot-plug/unplug.** Software readiness review now; the physical half needs the owner.

**F. ✅ ext2 + storage durability (2026-09-22, not in the original plan).** Ten further ext2
defects (12-21, four data-destroying) found by a new **host harness** and fixed; `libcache` no
longer writes back unchanged lines (**12.6x** on partial-line rewrites); `bcm2711-emmc` returns a
short read instead of `-EINVAL` at the device end; and the **`sync`/`fsync` chain was broken at
three levels** and now works (libphoenix stub → kernel zeroed oid → no filesystem handled
`mtSync`). Host suite `run-all.sh` **ALL GREEN** across 8 harnesses x 2 block sizes, randomised and
2/4-thread concurrent. All non-Pi-specific parts are written up for upstream in
`docs/PHOENIX-RTOS-RPI4-CHANGES.md` (shortlist entries 19-21 plus `setvbuf` and the storage short
read), at the owner's request.

⚠ **And five claims of mine were retracted today**, each by a measurement rather than re-reasoning:
the read-cap fault attribution, "`fsync()` does not exist", "`sync` fixes the leak", "`sync`
reduces the leak", and finally **the leak itself** — it was `/var/tmp`, created at boot. The
corrections are in the log, the skills, memory and the write-up, because a wrong claim in a skill
propagates to every future session.

**A (revisited). ✅ A2 ANSWERED, and a fix landed — but not the fix the plan expected.**
Device ops per `write()` are **5** when it allocates and **3** on overwrite, of which **2 store
bytes already on the device** (§2b, measured not reasoned). So the lever was neither deferring
`ext2_sb_sync()` nor the ranged flush: it was **not writing a line whose contents did not change**
(`a46399c`, §2f) — general to every filesystem, and with no correctness question. Host A/B: 20
identical rewrites 10240 B → **0 B**. ✅ **Hardware-gated** (§2f): after removing the warm-cache
confound, **12.6x** on partial-line rewrites — 6.811 s vs 0.542 s of device work — against a
predicted `memcmp` cost of ~1.3% of a device write.

**Defence in depth. ✅ `fcfb121`** — nothing in `block.c` validated a block number against
`fs->sb->blocks` (**zero** occurrences of the field). That is why defect 14's underflow became a
*Data Abort in the driver* rather than an error return. `ext2_blockValid()` now gates
`ext2_block_destroy()` and `ext2_block_destroyone()` before the group is derived.

Verified rather than assumed — applying **only** this guard to the pre-fix tree (`38ddfb7`):

| seed | SEGV | block numbers rejected |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1 → **0** | 9 |
| 4 | 1 → **0** | 48 |

and the rejected values are exactly the underflows — 4294967284 (2³²−12), 4294967295, 4294967291 —
so it would have turned that whole class of crash into an error, and independently re-confirms the
mechanism. With the real fixes in place it rejects nothing (10 seeds x 800 ops clean).

**Target regression tests. ✅** `phoenix-rtos-tests` `5581259` adds `trunc-holes` and `trunc-tail`
to `test_sparse`, covering the two data-destroying truncate defects on hardware — the whole stack
including driver, libcache and DMA, which the host harness cannot reach. `syntax-check.sh` CLEAN
under the real target flags.

**E. ext2 read — ✅ DONE 2026-09-22: +13.5% by coalescing, half the gap closed, now CLOSED.**
   Read path coalesces contiguous runs (`ea33d31`), correctness-gated then A/B'd in one session:
   41.5 → 47.1 MB/s, 81% → 90% of the device. The residual ~10% is memcpy, not call overhead, so
   further effort is not warranted. Original analysis below.

   **(original) ext2 read 42.6 vs raw 59.1 MB/s — quantified 2026-09-22, no Pi cycle needed.** The gap is
   **serialisation, not slowness**. Arithmetic: a 64 KiB cache line fetched at the raw 59.1 MB/s
   takes **1.109 ms** and then serves **16** 4 KiB filesystem blocks, i.e. **69.3 µs of device time
   per block**. Observed throughput is 96.2 µs per block. The difference — **26.8 µs per block,
   28% of the total, exactly the 42.6-vs-59.1 gap** — is libext2/libcache CPU (`ext2_block_get`'s
   offset walk, the cache set lookup, the 4 KiB memcpy out of the line) that runs **between**
   transfers instead of during them.

   ⇒ The lever is **overlap, not efficiency**: prefetch the next line while the current one is being
   copied out. Shaving the per-block CPU helps only in proportion; hiding it behind the transfer
   recovers the whole 28%. Note this also means micro-optimising `ext2_block_get` is close to
   worthless on its own. Still the lowest-value of the five, but it is now a specific piece of work
   rather than "needs profiling".

⚠ **Process fix (mine):** an `until` waiter keyed on a marker that only a task I later `TaskStop`'d
would have written spun `sleep 10` for ~4 h. A waiter must key on something that terminates
regardless — process existence — never on an artifact the killed task would have produced.

## 2c. 2026-09-21 — 🐞➡✅ ELEVENTH ext2 defect: a hole read back the SUPERBLOCK

Found by reading the write path while decomposing topic A; unrelated to that, fixed on its own
merits (`phoenix-rtos-filesystems` `62d513a`).

`ext2_block_init()` passed `*bno` straight to `ext2_block_read()` without checking it, so an
**unallocated** block — `*bno == 0` — read **block 0 of the device**: the boot sector and superblock.

* Reading a **hole** in a sparse file returned superblock bytes where POSIX requires zeros.
* Every freshly created file's first block began as a **copy of device block 0** with only the
  written bytes laid over it (a sub-block write reads the block, memcpy's the payload in, writes all
  4 KiB back). Those stale bytes become readable the moment the file is extended — superblock
  content leaking into user data.

✅ Test added (`phoenix-rtos-tests` `330259c`, `fs/test_sparse.c`): a genuine hole, and a short write
followed by `ftruncate` so the untouched block tail becomes readable. Reports how many bytes were
non-zero and the first offending value, so a regression says how bad it is. **Validated on Linux
ext4** — compiles under `-Wall -Wextra`, 11/11 checks pass — so it is not vacuously green.
✅ **HW-VERIFIED 2026-09-22** (`sparserun`): **11/11 PASS on the 4 KiB ext2 stick** — the filesystem
that had the bug — and 11/11 on the RAM control, amplification still 1.00x. Both the genuine-hole
case and the short-write-then-extend case (the one that leaked superblock content into user data)
now read zeros.

## 2d. 2026-09-21 — libphoenix line-buffering: a candidate bug, HELD until verified

Found while chasing why bash's `echo` is slow. bash's echo writes **`putchar()` per character**
(`builtins/echo.def:176`) and bash sets stdout line-buffered, so it is the exact shape that exercises
this path.

In `fwrite_unlocked()` the line-buffer flush — `memrchr(ptr, '\n', writesz)` then `write_data` — sits
**only in the `bufpos == 0` branch**. The "fill the incomplete buffer first" branch calls
`buffer_data()`, which is a bare `memcpy` with no newline check. So once the buffer is non-empty, a
`'\n'` is appended and **the line is not flushed**; it waits for the buffer to fill or an explicit
`fflush()`. C17 7.21.3p3 requires the flush.

⚠ **NOT COMMITTED, and here is why.** `phoenix-rtos-tests` already has
`TEST(stdio_bufs, setvbuf_linebuffer)` doing precisely `fputs("0123")` then `fputc('\n')` then
reading through a second descriptor — which is the pattern my reading says is broken. So either that
test is **currently failing** (and the fix makes it pass), or my reading is wrong and the change
should be dropped. Asserting a bug when an existing test contradicts it is how a plausible theory
gets committed as fact, which has already happened twice today.

⏭ Resolve by running the libc stdio suite on hardware and reading `setvbuf_linebuffer`'s CURRENT
result first. The fix is written and syntax-clean, sitting uncommitted in the working tree.

ⓘ It would not explain the `echo` slowness either way — it causes *fewer* writes, not more.

## 2a. ↩↩ 2026-09-21 — TOPIC A RETRACTED: the instrument was bash, not the filesystem

Same 512 bytes, same filesystem, four writers:

| writer (10 files) | time | ms/file |
|---|---|---|
| bash `echo` builtin | 50 s | **5000** |
| `dd bs=512 count=1` (ONE `write()`) | 1 s | **100** |
| `cp` (buffered) | 1 s | **100** |
| RAM `echo` | 0 s | ~0 |

`dd` and `cp` are **50x faster than `echo`** for identical bytes to an identical filesystem — and
both of them *spawn a process*, so the write itself is well under 100 ms. **bash's `echo` builtin
issues per-byte writes**; on dummyfs each is ~0.3 ms so it never showed, on ext2/umass each is ~12 ms
and it dominates everything.

↩ **So the whole A line was measuring bash.** A1's "create-only 40 ms vs create+write 195 ms ⇒ the
write path adds 155 ms" used `echo >` throughout: that 155 ms is ~16 one-byte writes, not a
filesystem cost. The 12.3 ms/byte linearity is the per-write cost multiplied by a per-byte writer.
The `ext2_sb_sync()` theory, the `msg_map` theory and the "185 ms per small file" framing all rest on
this and are withdrawn.

**What survives, and is worth fixing:**
1. **The bash port writes unbuffered.** Pathological on real storage — a 512-byte `echo` costs 50x a
   `cp` of the same bytes. A ports-level fix (bash output buffering), not a kernel one.
2. **A single small write to ext2 costs ~12 ms** — the block read-modify-write plus inode sync plus
   `ext2_sb_sync()` chain. Still the genuine filesystem-level lever, but an ordinary buffered writer
   issues few of these, so it is worth far less than it looked.

⚠ **Method note for next time:** every timing in the A line came from one tool, and no control
compared it against another writer. The `dd`/`cp` control took one cycle and would have caught this
at the start.

## 2b. 2026-09-22 — A2 ANSWERED BY ARITHMETIC: 3 device writes per `write()`, and one is a no-op

A2 asked for device-op counts per file rather than bytes. Reading the write path answers it without
a Pi cycle. Every `_ext2_file_write()`, **whatever its size**, ends with a fixed tail:

1. the data block read-modify-write — `ext2_block_init()` (read) + `ext2_block_syncone()` (write)
2. `_ext2_obj_sync()` — the inode
3. `ext2_sb_sync()` — `sizeof(ext2_sb_t)` = 1 KiB at `SB_OFFSET`

↩ **Corrected by measurement 2026-09-22.** I wrote "3 device writes per `write()`" from reading the
code. The host harness counts them, and the answer depends on whether the write allocates:

| one 11-byte write | device write commands | of those, byte-identical to what was there |
|---|---|---|
| **allocates** a block | **5** (block bitmap, gdt, data, inode, sb) | 1 |
| **overwrite** in place | **3** (data, inode, sb) | **2** |

Identical counts at 1 KiB and 4 KiB. So my "3" was right for an overwrite and wrong for the
