create-heavy 200-file case, which pays 5. At ~4 ms/command that is ~20 ms allocating, ~12 ms
overwriting — and ~12 ms is exactly the per-byte figure §2a measured from `echo`, whose writes
after the first *are* overwrites. The arithmetic and the measurement now agree on both shapes.

★ **Two of the three commands in an overwrite change nothing at all.** The superblock (no
allocation ⇒ no count moved) and the inode block (`time(NULL)` is 1-second resolution, so a
same-second rewrite of equal length leaves it byte-identical). That is measured by comparing each
write against the device contents, not inferred. **67% of the device traffic for an in-place
overwrite is pure wear** — and the pathological `echo` workload is precisely in-place overwrites.

**Two levers, with predicted magnitudes stated before any code changes:**

* **Skip `ext2_sb_sync()` when the superblock did not change.** The superblock holds free-block and
  free-inode counts; those move only on allocate/free. An **overwrite in place allocates nothing, so
  today we write back a byte-identical superblock** — a pure no-op device command. Gating it on a
  dirty flag is semantically identical and therefore carries **no correctness risk**.
  ⚠ Predicted effect on the 200-file benchmark: **none** — every file there allocates, so the sb
  genuinely changes. It pays off for rewrite/append-in-block workloads (logs, databases), not for
  the create-heavy case that started this thread. Saying so in advance so the A/B cannot be
  read as a disappointment.
* ★ **Better than either: have libcache not write a line whose content did not change.** The
  measurement above says 2 of 3 commands are byte-identical write-backs. A content check when
  marking a line dirty would drop them, at the libcache level, for **every** filesystem rather
  than just ext2 — and with no correctness question at all, since a write that stores identical
  bytes is unobservable. Predicted: in-place overwrite 3 commands → 1, so the ~12 ms/byte `echo`
  path → **~4 ms**. Testable entirely on the host with `tools/libcache-hosttest/`.
* **Defer `ext2_sb_sync()` to unmount/sync, as Linux does.** This is the one that would help the
  create case: 3 commands → 2, so ~12 ms → **~8 ms, ~1.5x**, not the 3x a "remove a third of the
  work" framing suggests. Correctness-sensitive (stale counts after power loss; fsck rebuilds them,
  which is exactly Linux's bargain) ⇒ own A/B + `e2fsck` gate.

**Neither is started.** Recording the analysis now because it is the part that needed no hardware;
the SD read-cap re-verify owns the bench first.

## 2c. 2026-09-22 — the ranged write-back now has HOST validation, agreeing with hardware to 6%

`tools/libcache-hosttest/run-write.sh` A/Bs `cache_write()` on the host under ASan+UBSan against a
shadow buffer, replaying the ext2 metadata shape (scattered 128 B write-back).

| flush granularity | device bytes / user bytes | device content |
|---|---|---|
| default (whole 64 KiB line) | 460.80x | byte-exact |
| 512 B (ranged) | **20.75x** | byte-exact |

**22.2x reduction**, against the **21x** the hardware A/B measured (21.02x → 1.00x). Two
independent measurement paths within 6%. The absolute ratios differ because the synthetic load is
deliberately scattered; the *ratio between the two* is the quantity the hardware result claimed.

Worth having because the narrowed flush range is exactly the kind of change that silently drops
part of a write, and until now its only check was an end-to-end `e2fsck` on the Pi — which would
catch a corrupted filesystem but not tell you the flush range was the cause. Both granularities
reproduce the device byte for byte.

## 2d. ★★★ 2026-09-22 — libext2 NOW RUNS ON THE HOST, with the e2fsck oracle, in seconds

`tools/libext2-hosttest/` runs **the real libext2** — the same eight `.c` files that ship on the
Pi — against a file-backed device, then hands the image to `e2fsck -fn`. libext2 reaches storage
through two plain callbacks (`fs->legacy.read/write`), so nothing had to be faked but the Phoenix
headers; the harness replicates `libext2_mount()` minus its `sys/msg.h` dependency and drives the
public API. Built with ASan+UBSan.

**This changes the cost of ext2 work.** Eleven defects were found this week at 5-10 minutes per
attempt (build, boot, `dd` the device back, `e2fsck` host-side). The same oracle now runs in under
a second, and the read/write callbacks **count device operations**, which is exactly what A2 asked
for and what A3's gate will need.

It found two defects on its first two runs.

### Defect 12 — `ext2_truncate()` frees indirect blocks without decrementing `i_blocks`

`e2fsck` on the very first run: `Inode 14, i_blocks is 18, should be 0`. Bisected over file shapes
(`isolate.c`), all truncated to 0:

| file | `i_blocks` after | that residual is |
|---|---|---|
| 12 blocks (direct only) | **0** ✔ | nothing to free |
| 13 blocks | 2 | 1 single indirect |
| 300 blocks | 6 | 1 single + 1 double + 1 single |
| 2 MiB | 18 | 1 + 1 + 7 singles = 9 blocks |
| 2 MiB → 4096 | 26 | 8 legitimate + the same 18 |

Every residual equals the file's indirect-block count exactly, and allocation *does* count them
(`after_write = 4114 = (2048 data + 9 indirect) x 2`). So the free path is missing the decrement
the allocate path performs. Not yet fixed — a build is holding the sibling trees.

### Defect 13 — `ext2_inode_init()` validates `ino` AFTER using it to index `fs->gdt[]`

```c
uint32_t group = (ino - 1) / fs->sb->groupInodes;          /* 62 */
uint32_t bno = fs->gdt[group].inodeTbl + ...;              /* 64  <-- indexes gdt */
...
if (((fs->root != NULL) && (ino < fs->root->id)) || (ino > fs->sb->inodes))
        return NULL;                                        /* 68  <-- the check */
```

Same shape as the `strncmp` over-read: the bound is tested after the access it guards. On the Pi
this reads heap garbage and produces a **wrong `inodeTbl`, silently**; ASan faults on it at once.
Reachable from any bad inode number — my harness hit it by not checking `ext2_create()`'s return.

⚠ And the same defect is in **`ext2_inode_sync()`**, which is the **write** path — a block number
derived from an over-read of `fs->gdt[]` would write an inode table entry over an arbitrary block.

### ✅ Both FIXED and host-validated — `phoenix-rtos-filesystems` `1ef6f56`

All six indirect-block release sites now go through one helper that frees and decrements together,
so the two cannot drift apart again; both inode functions validate before deriving the group.

| check | 1 KiB | 4 KiB |
|---|---|---|
| `e2fsck -fn` | **clean** | **clean** |
| harness checks | all pass | all pass |
| all 7 truncate shapes account exactly | ✔ (incl. partial trunc, `i_blocks=8`) | ✔ |

⚠ **Still needs a `--scope core` build and a Pi gate before it ships** — host validation is strong
evidence, not a substitute for the gate.

⚠ **The SD image now staged predates `1ef6f56`.** It carries the read-run cap (`38ddfb7`) but NOT
the `i_blocks` fix, because it was built before that commit. That is fine for the read-cap
re-verify, which is what the flash is for — but the card must not be described afterwards as
carrying the `i_blocks` fix. A later build ships that.

## 2e. ⚠ 2026-09-22 — I ran TWO concurrent builds, and it broke the netboot lane

`ps -p <pid>` returned nothing for the build's step-1 process, and I read that as "the task was
killed by the harness timeout" and launched a second, detached build. It meant only that **step 1
had finished**; the task was still running its later steps. The two builds then fought over the
same `.buildroot`, and the first one's netboot rebuild failed:

```
netboot build rc=1
rootfs:    sd
REFUSING: this blob boots a sd rootfs, but a nfsroot one is required.
```

So the TFTP loader was left an SD blob and netboot was down — the one thing the standing rules
call priority #1. The detached build's own step 4 is the fix and is running; nothing else may
start until it reports.

★ **A gone PID means that STEP finished, not that the task died.** A multi-step background task
has a different child pid per step. The only reliable liveness signal is the task notification, or
the wrapper pid — never a pid captured from one stage.

⚠ And `pgrep -f sdbuild.sh` then reported the finished build as still alive, because **the pattern
matched the argv of the shell running the `pgrep`** — the same self-match that has produced orphaned
waiters here twice. Netboot is restored (`rootfs: nfsroot`, built 03:31:15). The SD image from that
tangle was discarded rather than flashed: it was produced while two builds were mutating one
`.buildroot`, and `qemu-boot-sdimage.sh` only proves plo parses the loader, not that the rootfs is
coherent. A clean single build is running, and because `1ef6f56` landed at 03:35:59 before it
started, that image carries the ext2 fixes as well as the cap.

## 2f. ★★ 2026-09-22 — libcache no longer writes back a line that did not change (`a46399c`)

This is the lever the A2 measurement pointed at, and it is better than either candidate in §2b:
**a write that stores identical bytes is unobservable**, so there is no correctness question at all,
and it applies to every filesystem rather than just ext2.

`cache_write()` compares the incoming bytes against the cached line first; on a match it neither
dirties the line nor runs the write policy. Guarded by `contentsKnown`, because a full-line miss
deliberately skips the fetch and `linePtr->data` is then uninitialised.

| host A/B — 20 identical 128-byte rewrites | device traffic |
|---|---|
| baseline | 10240 bytes |
| `a46399c` | **0 bytes** |

Device byte-correct in both, at whole-line and 512 B granularity; scattered-write amplification
unchanged (20.96 → 20.76); read path unaffected. The harness prints which behaviour it saw, so it
cannot pass silently — the baseline run says "re-written", which is what makes it a real gate.

**Predicted on hardware, to be checked:** the `memcmp` is ~1.3% of a device write (64 KiB compare
~65 µs at ~1 GB/s vs ~5 ms for a 64 KiB SD write), so bulk throughput should be unchanged within
noise while metadata-heavy work drops ~two thirds of its device commands. If bulk regresses, gate
the compare on a size threshold.

### Measuring it: two obvious designs measure NOTHING

Predicting the magnitude first killed two designs before they burned a cycle:

* **A bulk rewrite shows nothing, correctly.** A 64 MiB rewrite writes *full* 64 KiB cache lines,
  and `cache_write()` deliberately skips the fetch for a full-line write — so `contentsKnown` is 0
  and the comparison cannot apply. That is right (fetching purely to compare would cost a read),
  but it means the win lives **only in partial-line writes**: metadata and small writes.
* **A shell loop of 200 `dd`s is swamped by process spawn** — ~100 ms each (§2a). A ~1 s effect
  inside a ~20 s number is not a measurement.

So `lcmeas.sh` writes 8 MiB in **4 KiB chunks** (partial against a 64 KiB line) with **one `dd`**
reporting its own rate, the A/B being *the same command twice*: first over differing bytes, then
over bytes already on disk.

### First result (`lcmeas`, 04:39) — big effect, but my A/B had a CONFOUND

| pass | time | rate |
|---|---|---|
| `changing` — zeros over random | 9.278 s | 904 kB/s |
| **`identical`** — the same command again | **0.640 s** | **13.1 MB/s** |
| `identical-again` | 0.641 s | 13.1 MB/s |

0 faults; `TAG-size` 8388608 as required, so the work happened. **14.5x**, and reproducible — but
↩ **the changing pass ran FIRST and warmed the cache**, so the identical pass avoided the line
*fetches* as well, skip or no skip. Part of that 14.5x is a warm cache, not the change. The pair
does not isolate what I wanted, so `lcmeas2` adds the missing control: a **changing** pass with the
cache already warm (p3 identical-warm vs p4 changing-warm, differing only in whether the bytes are
already there).

### ↩ The bulk prediction was wrong — in the safe direction

I predicted `TAG-bulk-write-64M` within ~5% of 18.2 MB/s. Measured **14.3 MB/s**, which looked like
a 21% regression until I found the comparable baseline: 18.2 is a **1 GiB** write, whereas the
pre-change **64 MiB** ext2 writes on umass1, same command shape, sit in this week's logs at **9.4
and 9.6 MB/s**. So bulk write went **9.5 → 14.3 MB/s, about +50%** — it did not regress.

⚠ I am **not** claiming that +50%: seven ext2 fixes and the libcache change shipped in one image,
and defect 18's fix in particular changes how write runs coalesce. Attributing it needs its own
A/B. What the number does settle is what the prediction was for — **the `memcmp` costs no
measurable bulk throughput**. Reads nudged 45.9-47.1 → **49.1 MB/s**, within the run-to-run spread.

★ **Incidental, worth keeping: `/dev/urandom` on this Pi runs at 64.9 kB/s.** The 8 MiB seed pass
took **129 s**, longer than every other pass combined. Never generate bulk test data with it here;
stage a deterministic file host-side (`lcmeas2` does).

### ✅ CONTROLLED result (`lcmeas2`, 04:45) — **12.6x**, confound removed

p4 and p5 read the **same** NFS source and both run warm, so they differ in exactly one respect:
whether the bytes are already on disk. Subtracting the separately measured NFS read cost (3.124 s
for the same 2048 x 4 KiB reads) isolates device work:

| pass | wall | − NFS read | device work |
|---|---|---|---|
| p4 **CHANGING**-warm (nonzero over zeros) | 9.935 s | −3.124 | **6.811 s** |
| p5 **IDENTICAL**-warm (nonzero over nonzero) | 3.666 s | −3.124 | **0.542 s** |
| p3 identical-warm, `/dev/zero` source (free) | 0.628 s | — | 0.628 s |

p3 is an independent corroboration of p5: a different source, no NFS cost, same ~0.6 s. 0 faults;
`TAG-content-ok rc=0` and `TAG-size 8388608`, so the work happened.

**12.6x on partial-line rewrites of unchanged content**, and the arithmetic closes: 6.811 s over
2048 writes is **3.33 ms per avoided device write**, matching the per-command cost measured
independently this week (3.1 ms at 4 KiB). So the saving is exactly "2048 device writes that no
longer happen", not a cache artefact.

↩ **My prediction of "at least ~1.5x" was far too conservative, and the reason is instructive:** I
reasoned in whole cache lines — "16 x 4 KiB flushes per 64 KiB line become none" — forgetting that
the **ranged** write-back added earlier this week flushes each dirty range *separately*. So it is
2048 flushes avoided, not 128. The two changes compound: ranged write-back made each flush small,
and this one removes it entirely.

## 2g. ★★★★ 2026-09-22 — RANDOMISED stress found a DATA-DESTROYING truncate defect (`680c9b2`)

`tools/libext2-hosttest/run-stress.sh` does seeded random ops (create / write-at-offset / append /
truncate / unlink) graded by a **shadow content model** and `e2fsck`. It failed **5 of 5 seeds**
immediately, with three symptoms that turned out to be **one defect**.

⚠ **Regression-checked first**, per the standing rule: the tree at `38ddfb7`, *before* my `i_blocks`
fix, fails all five seeds with the same SEGV line, the same seeds and a byte-identical content
mismatch — `i_blocks` reads −14 there against −16 after, i.e. my fix correctly decrements one more
indirect block. None of it was mine.

**The defect.** `_ext2_file_truncate()` coalesced runs by tracking the *last* block number:

```c
if (!lbno || (*bno == lbno + 1)) { n++; }      /* a hole sets lbno = 0 ... */
...
ext2_block_destroy(fs, lbno + 1 - n, n);       /* ... so this is `1 - n` */
```

A hole (`*bno == 0`) set `lbno = 0`, so the next iteration's `!lbno` test **continued the run across
it**, and the release computed `1 - n` — which **underflows uint32_t**. Hence:

* `ext2_blockToGroup()` of that huge number gave a huge group and `ext2_block_destroy()` read past
  the end of `fs->gdt[]` — the SEGV at `block.c:125`;
* where it did not fault it **freed blocks that were never allocated, destroying whatever file
  owned them** — which is why the model saw an unrelated file's content change;
* `i_blocks` was decremented by the whole logical range, counting holes — the −14.

Both `FIXME`s this function carried (*"truncation for files with unallocated blocks might fail"*,
*"blocks counting is broken"*) described this one bug. Both are now gone.

| stress, 5 seeds x 400 random ops, 1 KiB | `e2fsck` | SEGV |
|---|---|---|
| before | **rc=12 on 5/5** | **5/5** |
| `680c9b2` | **rc=0 on 5/5** | **none** |

Sequential harness still PASSES at 1 KiB and 4 KiB.

★ The lesson is about coverage, not this bug: **"eleven defects fixed, `e2fsck` completely clean"
was true for the sequential workload it was measured on.** Random ops broke it in seconds. A
create-write-delete script does not explore holes, and holes were where the data loss lived.

### Defect 15 — truncate left stale bytes in the last partial block: a data LEAK (`9a34339`)

Truncation releases whole blocks from `start` on, so when the new size falls **inside** a block the
bytes between it and the end of that block stayed on disk. Nothing reads them while the file is
short (`_ext2_file_read()` clamps to `inode->size`) — but **extending the file again made them
readable**, so previously-deleted content came back. POSIX requires that gap to read as zeros.

Isolated by `tools/libext2-hosttest/tail.c` — write 8 KiB of `0xAA`, truncate, re-extend past the
truncation point, read the gap:

| truncate to | block offset | before | after |
|---|---|---|---|
| 4987 | 891 | stale `0xAA` | zeros |
| 5000 | 904 | stale `0xAA` | zeros |
| **4096** | **0** | zeros | zeros |
| 1 | 1 | stale `0xAA` | zeros |

The **aligned control** is what pins the mechanism to the partial tail block rather than truncation
in general. Both block sizes.

### Defect 16 — `ext2_block_sync()` wrote a hole-terminated run to the WRONG block (`fd5b7d4`)

One of the four sites resolving a pending run's first block omitted the base:

```c
ext2_block_get(fs, obj, i, &bno)          /* the hole branch */
ext2_block_get(fs, obj, block + i, &bno)  /* the other three */
```

`i` indexes the call's buffer, so the base must be added to reach the file block. Without it a run
of allocated blocks **terminated by a hole** was flushed to the physical block owned by file block
`i` — the data never reached the offset written, and it **overwrote whichever file owned that
block**. Needs allocated-then-hole inside one call, i.e. a sparse file.

| randomised stress, 5 seeds x 400 ops, 1 KiB | content mismatches |
|---|---|
| before the truncate work | 19 |
| + tail zeroing (`9a34339`) | 13 |
| + this (`fd5b7d4`) | **10** (seeds 2 and 5 clean, seed 3 down to 1) |

`e2fsck` rc=0 and no SEGV throughout.

### Defect 17 — freeing an indirect block left it in the `ind[]` cache (`c49d0e2`)

`ext2_block_readind()` writes a cached indirect block back when it evicts the slot, and
`_ext2_obj_sync()` writes back whatever is still cached. `ext2_iblock_destroy()` never touched
`obj->ind[].bno`, so freeing a block a slot still named let a **stale copy be written over a block
that had since been reallocated to another file**.

Found by noticing that *every* remaining failure sat in the single-indirect range (file blocks
13-267 at 1 KiB) and none in the 12 direct blocks. The invalidation goes in `ext2_iblock_free()`,
which already centralises every indirect release — the payoff from routing all six sites through
one helper.

⚠ It releases the buffer rather than just clearing `.bno`: a slot with `data != NULL` and
`bno == 0` would make the eviction path call `ext2_block_write(fs, 0, ...)`, which has **no guard
for block 0** and would land on the superblock.

| randomised stress, content mismatches | |
|---|---|
| before the truncate work | 19 |
| + tail zeroing `9a34339` | 13 |
| + hole-terminated run base `fd5b7d4` | 10 |
| + `ind[]` invalidation `c49d0e2` | **2** — 4 of 5 seeds completely clean |

`e2fsck` rc=0 and no SEGV throughout; sequential harness PASSES at both block sizes; the
truncate-tail leak test still reports no stale bytes.

### Defect 18 — `ext2_block_sync()` lost the first block of a run after a discontiguity (`74823c9`)

**This was the last one. The randomised stress is now completely clean.**

```c
lbno = 0;
i = j++;      /* new run starts at old j -- but nothing records ITS block */
```

After flushing the run in progress, the discontiguity branch left the **new** run's first block
unrecorded. The next iteration saw `!lbno`, accepted whatever followed and set `lbno` from *that*,
so block `i` and block `i+1` were never compared with each other. A run whose first two blocks were
discontiguous was written **as if contiguous**, laying the second block's data over whatever
followed the first. The fix captures the current physical block number before the flush (which
re-fetches through `bno`, a pointer into the indirect-block cache) and seeds the new run with it.

| randomised stress, content mismatches | |
|---|---|
| before the truncate work | 19 |
| + tail zeroing `9a34339` | 13 |
| + hole-terminated run base `fd5b7d4` | 10 |
| + `ind[]` invalidation `c49d0e2` | 2 |
| + this `74823c9` | **0** |

**Final state — ~182 000 random operations across 115 runs, everything clean:**

| configuration | ops | mismatches | `e2fsck` dirty | SEGV |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 KiB, writes ≤4 KiB | 20 x 800 | **0** | 0/20 | 0 |
| 4 KiB, writes ≤16 KiB | 20 x 800 | **0** | 0/20 | 0 |
| 1 KiB, writes ≤4 KiB | 30 x 2000 | **0** | 0/30 | 0 |
| 4 KiB, writes ≤16 KiB | 30 x 2000 | **0** | 0/30 | 0 |
| 1 KiB, writes **≤64 KiB** | 15 x 2000 | **0** | 0/15 | 0 |

The last row matters: 64 KiB writes at 1 KiB blocks produce runs of up to 64 blocks — the shape
the read-cap investigation originally (wrongly) suspected — and it is clean.

Sequential harness PASSES at both block sizes; truncate-tail leak test clean.

**Cost of the seven fixes, measured before they reach hardware** (same harness scenario, device-op
counters, ext2 at `38ddfb7` vs now):

| | device writes | device reads |
|---|---|---|
| before | 1147 (3 270 656 B) | 941 |
| after | **1134** (3 257 344 B) | 941 (identical) |

So they are **free — slightly better**, because defect 14 no longer issues bogus destroys and the
runs now coalesce correctly. ⚠ This scenario is not truncate-heavy, so it does not show the one
place the fixes *add* work: defect 15's tail zeroing costs one extra read+write per **non-aligned**
truncate. That is bounded and per-truncate, not per-block, but it is the thing to watch if a
truncate-heavy workload ever looks slow.

### ↩ Two hypotheses I tested and DISCARDED — both looked convincing

Recording these because the wrong leads cost as much as the right one, and each was killed by a
cheap measurement rather than by argument:

1. **"`fstBlock` arithmetic"** — the residue was 1 KiB-only, and 1 KiB is the only block size where
   `s_first_data_block == 1`. A round-trip test of `ext2_blockToGroup/ToOffs/FromOffs` over *every*
   block showed them **exact inverses** at `fstBlock` 0 and 1. Wrong.
2. **"group crossing"** — the 1 KiB image has 8 block groups and the 4 KiB one has **1**, so 4 KiB
   could not exercise it. Forcing 8 groups at 4 KiB (`mke2fs -g 2048`) still gave **0**, and 1 KiB
   with a *single* group still gave **2**. Group count is irrelevant. Wrong.

★ **What it actually was:** the stress wrote ≤4096 bytes, which fits in one 4 KiB block, so at
4 KiB it barely entered the multi-block write path at all. Raising the max write length reproduced
the same 2 failures at 4 KiB, and "1 KiB-only" evaporated. **A workload asymmetry masqueraded as a
block-size dependency for three hypotheses running.** When a bug looks specific to a
configuration, check whether the *test* differs there before concluding the *code* does.

## 2b. ⚠ 2026-09-21 — a 24x CLIFF between a 17-byte and a 513-byte file write

Measured while decomposing topic A (`A2v3`, counts asserted, `echo` builtin so no process spawn
per file):

| payload | 50 files | per file |
|---|---|---|
| 16 B (+newline = 17) | **13 s** | 260 ms |
| 512 B (+newline = 513) | **309 s** | **6180 ms** |

Both are **sub-block** writes taking the identical path in `_ext2_file_write()` — malloc 4 KiB,
`ext2_block_init`, memcpy, `ext2_block_syncone` — with **identical device traffic**. libext2's block
path therefore cannot explain it.

What does change at that boundary is the IPC message: `msg_t.i.raw` is **64 bytes**
(`phoenix-rtos-kernel/include/msg.h:115`), and `proc/msg.c:289` copies the payload inline when it
fits, else calls `msg_map()` (line 564) to map the sender's pages. 17 B fits; 513 B does not.

⚠ **Held as PROVISIONAL, and one observation already argues against the simple version:** 1 MiB `dd`
writes sustain **9.6 MB/s** through that same `msg_map` path, so "`msg_map` is slow" cannot be the
whole story — a 513 B write costing 6.2 s while a 1 MiB write costs ~0.1 s is not a per-message
mapping cost. Single sample, too.

### Mechanism read out of `msg_map()`, and a PREDICTION recorded before the measurement

`proc/msg.c:35` — for a payload that fits inside ONE page (`boffs != 0` and
`FLOOR(data) == FLOOR(data+size)`) the out-of-line path does, per message: reserve a page in the
destination map, **`vm_pageAlloc` a fresh physical page**, `vm_mmap` the source page into the kernel
map, two `page_map` calls, a `hal_memcpy`, then tear all of it down again. For a 1 MiB payload the
256 whole pages are mapped directly and only the two partial EDGE pages pay that cost — 2/258 of the
work instead of all of it.

**Prediction:** fast below the 64 B `raw` boundary, a step at it, then roughly **FLAT from 96 B to
512 B**, since every one of those is a single sub-page payload paying the same fixed setup. If
instead the cost rises smoothly across 64→512, this mechanism is wrong and the IPC story goes.

### ❌ PREDICTION FALSIFIED — it is LINEAR IN BYTES, ~12.3 ms each

| bytes | 10 files | ms/file | ms/byte |
|---|---|---|---|
| 16 | 3 s | 300 | 18.8 |
| 48 | 7 s | 700 | 14.6 |
| 64 | 8 s | 800 | 12.5 |
| 96 | 12 s | 1200 | 12.5 |
| 128 | 16 s | 1600 | 12.5 |
| 256 | 31 s | 3100 | 12.1 |
| 512 | 63 s | 6300 | 12.3 |

No step at 64 B; a straight line through the origin at **~12.3 ms/byte**. So the `msg_map` story is
**dead** — and so is ext2's block path, which is O(1) in payload size, and the console (the payload
never appears in the UART log: 0 occurrences).

**The only shape that fits a per-BYTE cost is one message per byte.** Cross-check from A1: RAM was
5 ms for a 16 B file = **0.31 ms/byte** — also linear, just 40x cheaper. So the write path appears to
issue a message per byte, and each costs ~0.3 ms on dummyfs and ~12 ms on ext2/umass (where each one
is a read-modify-write of the block plus an inode sync plus a superblock sync).

⏳ `A2wr` in flight to confirm: the same 512 bytes written by `echo` (suspected per-byte), by
`dd bs=512 count=1` (a **single** `write()`), and by `cp` — plus a RAM control. If dd is ~12 ms where
echo is 6300 ms, the cost is the NUMBER OF WRITE CALLS, not the bytes, and the fix is buffering.

⏳ `A2bis2` in flight (16/48/64/96/128/256/512 B, 10 files each, from a clean restore) to test that.
⚠ Take 1 was void: `mkdir` here treats `-p` as an OPERAND, so `mkdir -p` errored on a literal `-p`
directory left by my earlier scripts. A2v3's numbers are unaffected — its counts were asserted at 50.

ⓘ If this holds it is far more significant than ext2 metadata latency — it would affect every write
above ~48 usable bytes, system-wide, not just this filesystem.

## 2j. ✅ 2026-09-22 — `test_sparse` on the 1 KiB SD root: CRASH RESOLVED, gate PASSES

**Closed 04:33.** Card reflashed with `filesystems 74823c9` + `corelibs a46399c` (all seven ext2
fixes, the block-number guard's predecessors, and the libcache skip), then SD-booted and
`test_sparse /root` run **twice** — the second run being the one that matters, since `O_TRUNC` on
the now-persistent sparse files is the defect-14 trigger.

| | before | after |
|---|---|---|
| lane | `bcm2711-emmc;-r;/dev/mmcblk0p2:ext2`, no `nfs;/` | same (genuine SD boot) |
| `test_sparse /root` | crashed | **OK (0 failures), twice** |
| faults | **2**, in `process "bcm2711-emmc"` | **0** |
| flash | — | 1093+1 records, 1146849280 B exact, 12.4 MB/s |

The cause was **defect 14**, not the read-run cap I first blamed — see the retraction under §2j's
predecessor. Fixed for the right reason, confirmed on the lane that showed it.

(Original investigation notes follow.)

## 2j-orig. the investigation as it ran

SD boot with `ea33d317752b` (the read-coalescing commit) is otherwise fine: right lane
(`bcm2711-emmc;-r;/dev/mmcblk0p2:ext2`), right version, boot to psh, `uname` fine, psh survived.
But `/bin/test_sparse /root` **passed 10 of 11 checks and then took a `Data Abort (EL0)`** at the
last one. Same binary, same build, passes **11/11 on the 4 KiB stick** — so it is specific to the
1 KiB root.

Register detail: `far = 0x104c010`, `x0 = 0x4c030`, **`x19 = 0xffffe0`** — and
`far − x0 = 0xffffe0` exactly. So it faulted reading ~16 MB past a buffer with a length that should
have been 4079. That smells like a bad length, not like wrong file data.

⚠ **Not yet attributable.** The check immediately before (`read returns the whole tail`) PASSED, so
`read()` returned the right count — if my coalescing returned wrong DATA the last check would have
FAILED, not crashed. But that is reasoning, and reasoning has lost repeatedly this session.

⚠ Also: the `cp`/`cmp` of a 1.5 MB file in that cycle produced **no output**, which is ambiguous —
`cmp` is silent when files match and equally silent if it never ran. Not counted.

### ↩↩ 2026-09-22, DEFINITIVE: this fault was NEVER the read run. It was defect 14.

The capped image was flashed to the card and SD-booted (lane graded by the root arg:
`bcm2711-emmc;-r;/dev/mmcblk0p2:ext2`, no `nfs;/`). **`test_sparse /root` faults exactly as
before**, in `process "bcm2711-emmc"`, with `x19 = 0x00ffffe0` — **byte-identical to the
pre-cap run**. A fix that changes nothing about the register state did not address the cause.

Resolving the PC against the very binary in that image
(`aarch64-phoenix-addr2line -e .buildroot/.../prog/bcm2711-emmc 0x41d084`) names
`ext2_blockToGroup`, `block.c:51` — pure arithmetic, so the access is the caller's `fs->gdt[group]`
attributed to the inline. The numbers then close completely:

| evidence | value |
|---|---|
| `x19` | `0xFFFFE0` = 524287 x 32, and `sizeof(ext2_gd_t)` is 32 (packed) |
| `x25` | **524287** — the group index, matching independently |
| implied `bno` = `fstBlock + group*groupBlocks` | `1 + 524287*8192` = `0xFFFFE001` |
| that value as `1 - n` mod 2^32 | **n = 8192** |

`lbno + 1 - n` with `lbno == 0` is exactly **defect 14** — the hole-induced uint32 underflow in
`_ext2_file_truncate()`, fixed in `680c9b2` **after this image was built**.

⚠ And the path in is subtler than "the test truncates": `test_sparse` never calls `truncate()`. It
opens with **`O_TRUNC`**, and on the SD root `/root` **persists across boots**, so the *second* and
later runs truncate an existing **sparse** file to zero — straight into the underflow. That is why
the fault needs the SD lane and a repeat run: on `/tmp` (dummyfs) no libext2 is involved, and on a
fresh filesystem there is nothing to truncate. It also means the currently shipped `test_sparse` is
a valid reproducer for the re-gate without any change.

★ **Two lessons, both about my own reasoning:**
1. I attributed the fault to the read-coalescing run length because the fault appeared in the same
   session as that change, at a 1 KiB block size where runs are longest, and `x19` happened to look
   like a plausible byte count (~16 MiB). Every one of those was circumstantial. **`addr2line` on
   the shipped binary was available the whole time and would have named the function in one
   command.** Resolve the PC before theorising about the registers.
2. The cap (`38ddfb7`) is still defensible on its own terms — an unbounded run is worth bounding —
   but it fixed nothing, and I reported a green netboot re-gate as though it had. The netboot lane
   roots on NFS and never exercises libext2 truncate, so it could not have failed either way.

⚠ **And the version stamp OVERSTATED what shipped, in the same run.** That boot's
`/etc/build-versions` printed `phoenix-rtos-corelibs a46399c` — the libcache skip-identical-write
change — but the image does **not** contain it. `a46399c` was committed at 03:41, the SD build's
source prepare was ~03:38, and the stamp is taken at **image** time (03:47). Grepping the buildroot
source copy is what settles it, and did: 0 occurrences at image time. This is the documented
stamp-is-not-the-binary trap firing in the direction that *claims more* than is there — the more
dangerous direction, because it reads as reassurance.

### ➡ the ORIGINAL (wrong) attribution, kept for the reasoning trail

The discriminator misled me at first: `test_sparse /tmp` **also** faulted, and dummyfs does not use
libext2 — which looked like an exoneration. The register dump settled it:

```
Exception #36: Data Abort (EL0)   x19=0000000000ffffe0
in thread 47, process "bcm2711-emmc" (PID: 8)
```

**The faulting process is the SD storage driver**, which hosts libext2 — not the program doing the
I/O. The test crashed because the driver serving its ROOT filesystem crashed. ⚠ **Grade a fault by
the faulting PROCESS, not by what you happened to be running.** The test itself passes 11/11.

Cause: `ea33d31` coalesced contiguous blocks into one `ext2_block_read()` and put **no bound on the
run**. Before it this path never asked the storage layer for more than ONE block; at 1 KiB a
contiguous 16 MB file is 16384 blocks, and `x19 ≈ 16 MiB` is exactly that. It did not reproduce on
the 4 KiB stick, where the same file spans a quarter as many blocks — which is precisely why the
1 KiB lane had to be tested rather than argued.

Fix: cap the run at the 64 KiB block-cache line. A longer run cannot save another fetch, so the
+13.5% should be unaffected — re-gated on netboot, **and it was** (46.6 / 46.4 MB/s, 0 faults).

### ↩ "ROOT-CAUSED" IS TOO STRONG — downgrading it to "symptom removed, mechanism open"

Reading the code around the fix afterwards turned up two facts that my explanation does not
survive. Recording them because the heading above would otherwise tell a future reader this is
closed:

1. **The device never saw a 16 MB request, even before the cap.** `cache_read()` walks the request
   one cache line at a time and `cache_fetchLine()` fetches exactly `lineSize` (64 KiB) per miss.
   So the storage layer was already chunked at 64 KiB; my cap changes the number of `cache_read`
   *calls*, not the size of any device transfer. "A 16 MB transfer overwhelmed the driver" is
   therefore not what happened.
2. **The write side has coalesced unbounded runs since forever.** `ext2_block_sync()` merges
   contiguous runs with no cap and hands `j - i` blocks to `ext2_block_write()`. Long runs have
   been reaching the storage layer on every write for as long as this code has existed, without
   faulting. So run length *alone* cannot be the trigger.

What I did verify by hand is that the new loop is **bounds-safe**: with `offs = a·B + r`, the
unaligned head writes `B − r`, the body writes `(blkEnd − a − 1)·B`, and the sum is
`⌊(r+len)/B⌋·B − r ≤ len`; the tail then copies exactly `len − l`. So it does not overflow the
caller's buffer, and `TAG-ref-still` blank confirms the data is byte-identical.

### libcache EXONERATED by a host test — and the hypothesis that replaces it

`tools/libcache-hosttest/` builds `libcache/cache.c` on the host under ASan+UBSan against a
pattern-filled fake device and replays the faulting shape. Result: **clean on every shape** —
16 MiB contiguous (the exact case), 16 MiB unaligned, +1000, −1, and small reads — all correct
byte-for-byte, nothing written past the buffer. It also prints `largest single device read =
65536 bytes` for a 16 MiB request, which *demonstrates* fact 1 above rather than asserting it.

So the cap is not covering a large-count assembly bug. What is left is **how long the driver lock
is held**: `sdstorage_cachedRead()` wraps the whole `cache_read()` in `mutexLock(ctx->lock)`, so
one call now holds the SD driver's lock for the entire transfer.

| run | bytes under one lock hold | at SD's 12.9 MB/s |
|---|---|---|
| uncapped, 16384 blocks x 1 KiB | 16 MiB | **~1.24 s** |
| capped at `EXT2_READ_RUN_MAX` | 64 KiB | ~5 ms |

A ~250x increase in worst-case lock hold, on the lane whose device is 4.6x slower than the stick —
which also fits why the 4 KiB stick never showed it. ⚠ This is a **hypothesis with arithmetic
behind it, not a proven mechanism**; I have not caught the faulting thread in the act. It is
recorded so the next person starts from the right suspect, not from libcache.

⚠ **So the cap is justified on its own terms** — it matches the cache line, and a run longer than
one line cannot save a fetch, which is why it costs nothing — **but it may be masking a defect in
`cache_read()` on large counts rather than fixing one.** The difference matters: a masked defect
is still reachable by any other caller that passes a big count. The SD re-verify tests whether the
symptom is gone, which is NOT the same as knowing why. Leaving this open rather than closing it.

## 2i. 2026-09-22 — re-verifying the SD lane AFTER the read-coalescing change

The 1 KiB SD verification (§2g) was done **before** `ea33d31` landed, and that commit changes the
read path for every ext2 — including the SD root, where every file read at boot goes through it.

⚠ It is not the same code path at the two block sizes: a 64 KiB cache line spans **16** blocks at
4 KiB but **64** at 1 KiB, so run-coalescing produces much longer runs on the SD root than anything
tested on the stick. And no netboot gate can cover it, because netboot roots on **NFS**.

So the card is being reflashed with the current build and SD-booted again. Same grading as before:
root arg (`bcm2711-emmc;-r;/dev/mmcblk0p2:ext2`, no `nfs;/`) plus `/etc/build-versions` against SHAs
recorded beforehand.

⚠ **Target SHA updated:** the first reflash carried `ea33d31` and that is the build that faulted.
The re-verification must show **`38ddfb7`** (the capped run) in `/etc/build-versions` —
`ea33d31` on the card now means the flash did not take, and a clean boot would be meaningless.
The pass condition is the fault GONE on the 1 KiB lane, which is the only lane that ever showed it.

⚠ **Two ways this re-verify could pass while proving nothing**, both worth stating because both
look like success:

1. **The SHA stamp is not the binary.** `/etc/build-versions` is stamped at image time, so a
   `project image`-only rebuild (what `--scope auto` runs when the siblings are CLEAN — and
   `38ddfb7` is committed, so they are) prints the new SHA over **old objects**. Build `--scope
   core` and grade the *artifacts*: `libext2.a`, `prog/bcm2711-emmc` and `prog/usb` must all be
   newer than the commit. For the netboot re-gate this was checked and holds — libext2.a 02:57:03,
   bcm2711-emmc 02:57:03, usb 02:57:04, loader.disk 02:57:06, all after the 02:56:59 commit, and
   `runcap2` booted at 02:58:38.
2. **A clean boot is not the reproducer.** The fault came from `test_sparse` building and reading
   back a 16 MB contiguous file on the 1 KiB root. Booting proves only that the files read at boot
   are short. The SD cycle must run **`test_sparse /root`** (the ext2 root — `/tmp` is dummyfs and
   touches no libext2) and show 0 `Exception` lines with no `process "bcm2711-emmc"`.

✔ The netboot half is **done**: with the capped binary verified above, ext2 read 46.6 / 46.4 MB/s
against 41.5 before coalescing (raw control 51.1), `TAG-ref-still` blank = byte-identical, 0
exceptions. The cap cost nothing, as predicted — a run longer than the cache line cannot save a
fetch.

## 2h. 2026-09-22 — topic E: a LOW-RISK lever, found by looking at the write path

I had shelved E because the obvious fix (prefetch to overlap CPU with the transfer) needs
concurrency in a driver where I found real bugs tonight. Re-reading the code turned up a simpler
one: **`ext2_block_read()` already takes a block COUNT, and `ext2_block_sync()` has always
coalesced contiguous runs on the WRITE side — the read side never did.**

`_ext2_file_read()` looped `ext2_block_init()` once per block, so a sequential read paid an
`ext2_block_get()` plus a separate single-block `ext2_block_read()` for **every 4 KiB**. The read
path now detects contiguous physical runs and issues one call per run, mirroring the write path.

That attacks exactly the measured cost: 69.3 µs of device time per block against 96.2 µs observed,
the 26.8 µs difference being the whole 42.6-vs-59.1 MB/s gap. No concurrency, no new API, and it
preserves the hole-reads-as-zeros fix (a hole has no run to coalesce and is zero-filled explicitly).

### Prediction, recorded before the measurement

The change removes 15 of every 16 `ext2_block_get()` + single-block `ext2_block_read()` pairs on a
sequential read (16 × 4 KiB blocks per 64 KiB line), leaving the memcpy volume unchanged.

* If the 26.8 µs/block is mostly **per-call overhead** — lookup, offset walk, call frames — read
  throughput should move substantially from **42.6** toward the raw **59.1 MB/s**.
* If it is mostly the **memcpy out of the cache line**, which coalescing does not reduce, the number
  barely moves and the conclusion is that the remaining gap is memory bandwidth, not call overhead.

Either outcome is informative; the second would close E as "not worth more effort".

✅ **Correctness gated first** (`readcoal`, committed `ea33d31`): reference file identical, 8 MiB
round trip identical, sparse test 11/11 on the 4 KiB stick. Correctness before speed, because this
touches the read path for every ext2 including the SD root.

📊 **Measured (`readmeas`), and the baseline moved too:**

| | this run | earlier session |
|---|---|---|
| ext2 read 64 MiB | **45.9 then 47.1 MB/s** | 42.6 |
| raw read 64 MiB | **52.4 MB/s** | 59.1 |

⚠ The **raw** number changed as well (52.4 vs 59.1), so comparing 42.6 → 47.1 across sessions mixes
two variables. Same-boot ratio: ext2 now reaches **90% of raw** (47.1/52.4) where the old figure was
72% (42.6/59.1) — but that 72% used a raw number from a different boot, so it is not a clean
comparison either.

✅ **SAME-SESSION A/B — single variable, only `ext2/file.c` differs:**

| arm | ext2 read 64 MiB | raw read (control) | ext2 as % of device |
|---|---|---|---|
| OLD — one call per block | 41.8, **41.5** MB/s | 51.4 | **81%** |
| NEW — coalesced runs | 45.9, **47.1** MB/s | 52.4 | **90%** |

**ext2 read +13.5%**, with the raw control differing by only 1.9% between the two runs — which is
what makes the comparison trustworthy, and why the cross-session version (42.6 vs 59.1) was not.
The gap to the device ceiling goes **19% → 10%: about half of it closed.**

↩ **And the prediction was half right, which is the useful part.** I predicted a substantial move if
the 26.8 µs/block was mostly per-call overhead, and almost none if it was the memcpy out of the cache
line. The answer is roughly half each: coalescing removed the per-call half, and the remaining ~10%
is memory-bandwidth work that coalescing cannot touch. **E is now closed as "not worth more
effort"** — the remaining lever would be prefetch/overlap for at most ~10%, needing concurrency in a
driver where real bugs surfaced tonight.

ⓘ Note the device itself read 51-52 MB/s in these runs against 59.1 earlier, so the absolute raw
figure varies by session; only same-session comparisons mean anything here.

## 2g. ⚠ 2026-09-22 — the ext2 work has NEVER been exercised on a 1 KiB filesystem

Noticed while reviewing my own cumulative ext2 diff (11 commits, +200/−44) for publication quality.

| lane | root filesystem | exercised my ext2 changes? |
|---|---|---|
| netboot | **NFS** (`nfs-fs`) | **no** — so the six-app showcase gate did not touch ext2 at all |
| USB stick | ext2, **4 KiB** blocks | yes — all of tonight's testing |
| **SD** | ext2, **1 KiB** blocks (`mke2fs -b 1024`) | **NO** |

That matters specifically because `fstBlock` is **1** for 1 KiB blocks and **0** above, and the
block↔bitmap off-by-one fix (`505c4a3`) keys on exactly that. I showed algebraically that the
helpers reduce to the original expressions when `fstBlock == 1`, so the SD lane should be
bit-for-bit unchanged — but that is *reasoning*, and reasoning has lost to measurement repeatedly
in this session (the `msg_map` theory, the `sb_sync` theory, the line-buffering theory).

✅ **Card flashed** (`sdflash`): `1093+1 records in / 1093+1 records out`,
`1146849280 bytes copied, 91.8435 s, 12.5 MB/s` — the record count was computed from the image size
**before** the run, so a short write could not pass as success. Image verified in QEMU first
(`nfs;/ refs=0, bcm2711-emmc refs=4 → SD variant`, `PASS`). ✅ **SD BOOT VERIFIED — the 1 KiB gap is CLOSED.** Graded both ways the skill insists on, against
expectations written down *before* the run:

| check | result |
|---|---|
| lane | `bcm2711-emmc;-r;/dev/mmcblk0p2:ext2`, **no `nfs;/`** — genuinely the SD lane |
| `libphoenix` | `fa8521d3e979` = expected ✓ |
| `phoenix-rtos-corelibs` | `a9fdd5999471` = expected ✓ |
| `phoenix-rtos-devices` | `ad21abf6f356` = expected ✓ |
| `phoenix-rtos-filesystems` | `786aacd4bcff` = expected ✓ |
| root mount | `sdstorage_srv: mounted /dev/mmcblk0p2 (ext2, id=2) as / after 0 tries` |
| faults / psh | **0** faults, prompt reached |

So the eleven ext2 fixes run on **1 KiB** blocks as well as 4 KiB, and the algebraic argument that
`fstBlock == 1` reduces to the original arithmetic is now backed by a boot. Bench restored to the
netboot lane.

Procedure, following the `rpi4-flash-sd` skill: build the SD image, stage it on the export,
**rebuild the netboot loader** (⚠ `--variant sd` overwrites the TFTP `loader.disk` with a blob that
has no `nfs;/`, and the flash itself is done *over netboot* — so skipping this strands the bench),
flash from the Pi, then SD-boot and grade by the **root arg** (`bcm2711-emmc;-r;/dev/mmcblk0p2:ext2`
with no `nfs;/`) plus `/etc/build-versions`, since a card booting the OLD image looks exactly like
success otherwise. The card currently holds a 2026-09-20 image, so booting it as-is would prove
nothing.

✅ Also green this turn — full libc regression after the sweep, **694 tests, 0 failures**:
`stdio` 91, `string` 208, `libcache` 40, `misc` 212, `pthread` 30, `signal` 32, `time` 43,
`dirent` 38.

## 2f. 2026-09-22 — 🐞➡✅ TWO `setvbuf` bugs, caught by running the libc suite after the sweep

Sweep 11 changed **libphoenix**, and the gates I had run (console, six-app showcase) do not exercise
stdio buffering. So I ran the libc suite to cover it — and it **failed**:
`stdio_ext/bufsize_and_linebuffering`, `Expected 0 Was -1`.

1. **`setvbuf(fp, NULL, _IOLBF, 0)` returned −1.** With a null `buf` the implementation provides the
   buffer and `size` is only a hint (C17 7.21.5.6), so a request of 0 must not fail — but it asked
   `buffAlloc()` for 0 bytes, got NULL, and reported failure. **glibc defines `setlinebuf(s)` as
   exactly this call**, so portable code asking for line buffering was told no.
2. **`stream->flags &= ~(F_USRBUF & F_LINE);` — `&` where `|` belongs.** `F_USRBUF` is `1<<4`,
   `F_LINE` is `1<<2`, so their AND is 0, the mask was `~0`, and the statement **cleared nothing**.
   A stream could never be switched back *out* of line buffering, and a buffer libphoenix had
   allocated itself could stay marked `F_USRBUF` — the caller's — so the free path skipped it.

✅ Fixed in `fa8521d` and **verified by the same suite**:

| | stdio suite |
|---|---|
| before | `91 Tests **1 Failures** 1 Ignored` → **FAIL** |
| after | `91 Tests **0 Failures** 1 Ignored` → **OK** |

(the 1 Ignored is a pre-existing deliberate `#1403` skip).

✅ **Full libc regression after the sweep — 551 tests, 0 failures:**

| suite | result |
|---|---|
| `test-libc-stdio` | 91 / **0** (was 1 failure) |
| `test-libc-string` | 208 / 0 |
| `test-libcache` | 40 / 0 |
| `test-libc-misc` | 212 / 0 (11 ignored) |

⏳ Second batch running (`pthread`, `signal`, `time`, `dirent`) to widen the coverage, since the
sweep changed libphoenix and those are the areas the console/showcase gates cannot reach.

ⓘ Worth noting how this was found: not by suspicion but by **running a gate that covered the thing
the sweep actually changed**. The console and showcase gates were both green and neither touches
stdio buffering.

## 2e. 2026-09-22 — topic B in flight, and a trap headed off before it produced a false pass

`--with-ports` build running; all six appear in the log (`grep-3.11`, `sed-4.10`, `tar-1.35`,
`gzip-1.15`, `bzip2-1.0.8`, `xz-5.4.7`). ⓘ The bash `pathnames.h` `Error 1` in that log is marked
`(ignored)` by make and is a pre-existing quirk of that port, not a new breakage.

⚠ **The obvious test would have passed on the wrong binaries.** `/bin` precedes `/usr/bin` and
busybox supplies `/bin/{grep,sed,tar,gzip}`, so `command -v grep` resolves to **busybox** — a
presence check by bare name proves nothing about the GNU port. The test now checks `/usr/bin/<tool>`
explicitly, reports whether a busybox shadow exists, and prints `--version` to prove which binary it
actually got. It also grades every tool by a real round trip compared with `cmp` rather than by
`--version` alone, since a `--version` check passes on a binary that can do nothing else.

✅ **DONE — build rc=0, all six installed and HW-verified by round trip**

| check | result |
|---|---|
| present at `/usr/bin` | all six ✅ (busybox shadows `/bin/{grep,sed,tar,gzip}` confirmed present, `bzip2`/`xz` none) |
| identity | `grep (GNU grep) 3.11`, `tar (GNU tar) 1.35` — the GNU binaries, not the shadow |
| grep / sed | selected `bravo`, substituted `CHARLIE` |
| gzip / bzip2 / xz | compress → decompress → `cmp` **identical** |
| tar / tar.gz | create → extract → 4 lines, `cmp` **identical** |

✅ Build rc=0, all six installed to `/usr/bin/` in the buildroot rootfs; `sync-netboot-tree.sh`
then put them in the NFS export (the root is a hand-maintained superset and does not follow a build
on its own). ⓘ The `1257` hits for `error|failed` in that log are configure noise
("checking for error_at_line"); `rc=0` is the authority. ⏳ Boot test running.

📊 **Size cost, for the busybox decision:**

| | size |
|---|---|
| grep 232K · sed 200K · tar **1.5M** · gzip 136K · bzip2 436K · xz 312K | **2.8 MB total** |
| busybox (≈37 applets) | **436 KB** |

So the six GNU tools cost **~6.4x all of busybox** for a subset of its coverage, `tar` being more
than half of it. That is the real trade being made: full GNU semantics and maintainability against
2.4 MB of image. Worth the owner's eye before busybox is actually dropped rather than merely
shadowed.

## 3. 2026-09-21 evening — skills authored; sweep 11 merged on BRANCHES

**Skills (`.claude/skills/`, force-added past the `.claude/` ignore like the existing two).** Written
so the night's lessons stop being rediscovered:
- **`rpi4-storage-test`** — the `e2fsck`-on-a-read-back oracle (Phoenix writes, Linux judges), what
  each message means and which direction is data loss vs leaked space, `/clean4k.img` + `/ref-data.bin`,
  the two-boot split for cumulative amplification counters, "measure metadata not bulk", and that
  `sha256sum` crashes here in a way that mimics corruption.
- **`rpi4-core-change`** — edit → syntax-check → build → prove the blob with `strings` → A/B → commit
  → push → manifest, each step annotated with how it has silently passed on the wrong thing.
- **`rpi4-run`** (extended) — **the Pi lock**, stated as *do not write a waiter; let the task
  notification serialize you*, with the four variants tried and why each self-matches or orphans.

**Sweep 11 merged on branches, master untouched in every repo:**
| repo | branch | state |
|---|---|---|
| `libphoenix` | `agent/sweep11-libphoenix` | merged, 2 conflicts resolved (`32dcee7`) |
| `phoenix-rtos-devices` | `agent/sweep11-devices` | merged + **pl011-tty ported** (`ad21abf`) |
| `phoenix-rtos-posixsrv` | `agent/sweep11-posixsrv` | merged **clean**, as the census predicted (`df2f604`) |
| `phoenix-rtos-project` | `agent/sweep11-project` | merged (`2f31e8e`); submodule pointer → our merged posixsrv head, verified to contain both sides |

ⓘ `syntax-check` earned its keep on the port: it caught a **real** scope bug (`batch`/`n` were
declared inside the TX block the fbcon call moved out of). The eight errors that remain there all
name an old symbol or the old arity, i.e. the documented stale-sysroot-header false mode.

Resolutions: `sys/wait.h` takes upstream (its `__INLINE` is our `__inline__` hack done properly —
verified `__INLINE` is defined in `sys/cdefs.h` post-merge); `complex.h` stays deleted (we moved it);
**our `FIONREAD` kept but rewritten** — upstream deleted `tty->temp` and made `out_arg` a caller
buffer, so it `memcpy`s out like `TIOCGHALFD`. Dropping it was not an option: `-EINVAL` for FIONREAD
makes interactive bash mis-detect EOF and exit at its first prompt.

Two places a mechanical port would have been wrong: **`pl011_fbcon_write()` now runs AFTER releasing
`tty->lock`** (that lock covers every console reader/writer; holding it across a glyph blit would
stall the console — upstream's template can't show this, none of its drivers render), and the idle
gate's unlocked `libtty_txready()` is gone, so the value is sampled inside the locked section and
carried out in `txPending`.
✅ **BUILDS** (`--scope core`, rc=0) — first real compile of the merged libtty plus the ported
driver. One failure on the way, and it was caused by my own resolution:

🐞 `libm/phoenix/complex.c` failed with `expected ';' before 'double'` on every declaration. The
upstream commit that introduced `__EXPORT_INLINE` changed **two** things together — it added
`#include <sys/cdefs.h>` to `include/complex.h`, and it retitled the declarations in `complex.c` to
use the macro. Keeping `include/complex.h` deleted was right (we moved it in `d0a2884`), but the `.c`
half merged **cleanly** and then could not compile. Fixed by including `<sys/cdefs.h>` where the
macro is used.

⚠ **Lesson for future sweeps:** a modify/delete conflict can leave a **non-conflicting** hunk in
another file that depended on the deleted side. `git merge-tree` flags the conflict and is silent
about the coupled change; only the build finds it.

✅ **GATE PASSED** (`sweep11gate2`), every line:

| check | result |
|---|---|
| boot to `(psh)%` | ✅ — `libtty_init()` took the new lock arg, the tty came up |
| `uname -a` | ✅ `Phoenix-RTOS 3.3.1 aarch64a72` — console RX **and** TX through the ported thread |
| `bash` → `bash-5.2#` → `echo GATE-bash-alive` → **`GATE-bash-alive`** | ✅ — the **FIONREAD** rewrite works. Its failure mode is exact: `-EINVAL` makes interactive bash mis-detect EOF and quit at its first prompt. It didn't. |
| `exit` → `(psh)%` → `ls /dev` | ✅ full device list; the tty survived session teardown |
| **HDMI framebuffer** | ✅ rendered the whole session — boot, USB SuperSpeed enumeration, the bash session, `ls /dev`. **`pl011_fbcon_write()` works outside `tty->lock`**, which was the one deliberate deviation from upstream's template |
| faults | **0** |

✅ **SHOWCASE GATE 6/6, 0 faults each** (`sweep11show`), torches present 19/19 at the reference
viewpoint:

| app | rc | prompt | faults | frames |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| x | 0 | yes | **0** | 23 snaps |
| qspasm | 0 | yes | **0** | 9446 |
| q3 | 0 | yes | **0** | 9573 |
| q2 | 0 | yes | **0** | 10317 |

⚠ Graded by LOOKING, not just by the table — the gate script says so itself and this project has a
run where a "clean" log drew nothing. Quake II's frame shows the full 3D scene, weapon model and HUD
at 38.24 fps; STK's shows in-game racing with kart, track, minimap and lap counter at 26 KTris.

✅ **master fast-forwarded in all four repos**, all clean, all now **behind=0** — level with upstream
for the first time since the sweep opened.

ⓘ Before the final manifest, rebuild once: the gate blob reports `libphoenix 32dcee70+dirty`,
because the `complex.c` fix was built before it was committed. Content is identical to `73e26dc`;
only the recorded version string differs.

**D (hot-plug) review:** the unplug path is sound — `umass_handleDeletion` unlinks each matching
partition under the lock then tears it down **outside** it, which is the fix for the self-deadlock
found earlier tonight (flush → `umass_write` → same lock). Failure is bounded:
`UMASS_TRANSMIT_RETRIES` is 3, so an unplug-while-mounted flush fails rather than spins. Physical
insert/remove still needs the owner.

## 1l. 2026-09-21 — overnight USB cron RETIRED (its own conditions met)

The hourly job `dd3f3298` said: *delete when the corruption is root-caused and fixed, the write

read-back now **completely clean** across create / write / delete / umount / remount; metadata write

**Left open, in priority order** — re-arm a cron if you want these driven autonomously:
1. **Metadata write latency: 185 ms per small file**, and the bytes only account for ~4-8 ms.
   `_ext2_file_write()` syncs the superblock on **every** write. Correctness-sensitive (power-cut
   semantics), so it wants design, not a quick edit. This is the real speed lever, not amplification.
2. **Upstream sweep 11** — census done, adoption deferred by you;
   [`docs/misc/2026-09-21-upstream-sweep-11-census.md`](../misc/2026-09-21-upstream-sweep-11-census.md).

   `pl011-tty`, which is the Pi 4 console.
3. **Hot-plug / unplug** — still physically untestable while you are away.
4. ext2 read 42.6 vs raw 59.1 MB/s: per-block CPU in libext2/libcache, unchanged.

## 1k. 2026-09-21 — ✅ Ranged write-through flush: 21x less device traffic, NO speedup

**Clean single-variable A/B** (same blob, only `cache_setFlushGranularity` changed; restore in a
separate boot so the counters see ext2 traffic only; 200 small files):

| arm | asked | device | amplification | wall |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| OFF — whole line | 28272640 B | 594411520 B | **21.02x** | 35 s |
| ON — ranged 512 B | 28272640 B | 28272640 B | **1.00x** | 37 s |

✅ **The 20.4x metadata amplification is CONFIRMED (21.02x measured) and ELIMINATED (1.00x).**
566 MB of device writes removed from a workload that asked for 28 MB.

⚠ **But wall time did NOT improve — 35 s vs 37 s, i.e. nothing.** So metadata throughput is **not
device-bound**, and the cron's framing of this as "the 2.6x speed lever" is wrong. What the change
actually buys is **21x less NAND wear** — which matters a great deal on flash, and helps
`bcm2711-emmc`/SD equally — but it is an endurance win, not a speed one.

⏭ **Where the metadata time actually goes.** 200 files in 35 s = **185 ms per file**, against ~4-8 ms
of device time for the bytes involved. It is per-operation latency, not bytes:
`_ext2_file_write()` ends with `_ext2_obj_sync()` **and `ext2_sb_sync()` on EVERY write**, so each
small file write drags a superblock write behind it. That is the next lever — and it is
correctness-sensitive (sync semantics vs power-cut safety), so it wants deliberate design, not a
quick edit.

ⓘ Bulk writes barely moved (9.0 → 9.6 MB/s) for a good reason: a 1 MiB sequential write fills every
64 KiB line completely, so the old whole-line flush was already writing only dirty bytes. Bulk was
never the amplified case.

## 1e. Unwinding busybox — four GNU ports landed

⚠ **Corrected census.** The first count only looked at `/bin`. Busybox also hardlinks **19 names
into `/usr/bin`** and 4 into `/sbin`, 60 in total — so "awk/find/xargs/diff/cmp are already GNU
here" was **wrong**, they are busybox too.

- **Duplicated** (busybox shadows a real implementation, because `/bin` precedes `/usr/bin`):
  `base64 cat chmod cp date dd echo false kill link ln ls mkdir mv pwd rm rmdir sleep stat stty
  touch true`
- **Only busybox**: `awk cmp diff find xargs hexdump xxd which logger sha3sum ed vi sh ash tar sed
  bzip2 bunzip2 bzcat unxz xzcat usleep login init getty syslogd hwclock runsv runsvdir sv`

✅ **Ported (build clean, static, `/usr/bin`)**: **grep 3.11**, **sed 4.10**, **tar 1.35**,
**gzip 1.15**, and the **xz CLI** (the `xz` port existed but built liblzma only, so `unxz`/`xzcat`
on the image were busybox — same for `bzip2`). ⏸ Not yet registered in `ports.yaml`, not yet run on
HW.

ⓘ **Owner decision on the init plumbing (2026-09-20): port none of it.** `init`, `getty`,
`syslogd`, `hwclock`, `runsv`/`runsvdir`/`sv`, `login` are already dead code here — plo starts
every driver from the syspage and `/etc/rc.psh` runs `bind`/`dummyfs`/`posixsrv`/`psh`; the busybox
`/etc/inittab` and `/etc/rc` never execute. Logging is `rpi4-klogd`. Remaining real gaps:
**gawk**, **findutils** (find/xargs), **diffutils** (diff/cmp). No `ash` — bash is the shell.

⚠ **`/bin/sh` is a shared dependency**: grep's `egrep`/`fgrep` are shell wrappers since grep 3.8,
and `tar -czf` execs `/bin/sh -c` hardcoded (extract is fine, it uses `execvp` off PATH). Install
bash as `/bin/sh` when busybox goes.

✅ **libc gaps the ports exposed, fixed rather than worked around** — `<stdio_ext.h>` implemented in
libphoenix (`f831e24`) with 9 tests. gnulib needs these accessors; without them it compiles its own
per-libc `#if` ladders over private FILE internals, which is why **four** ports each carried a patch
teaching it our `FILE`. ★ `__fpurge` already existed as an **empty stub** — a caller asking for
buffered data to be discarded got silent success and kept the data.
⏸ Still open, both needing a **new kernel syscall** (the kernel tracks `thread->sigpend` but exposes
no way to read it), so separately gated: **`sigwait`** and **`sigpending`** are *declared* in
`signal.h` and never defined — a fifth silent-probe class, where a compile-time probe answers yes
and only the link says otherwise. tar wants `sigwait`.
⏸ **libphoenix exports a bare global `debug`** (a real syscall in `<sys/debug.h>`), so any static
binary with its own global `debug` fails to link — sed had to rename its own variable. Renaming the
syscall is an OS-wide API break; the contained fix is a `__debug` strong symbol with a weak `debug`
alias, with libphoenix's internal callers using `__debug`.

## 3. In progress

Nothing mid-flight. Both lanes are gated on the current tree, all 17 repos are clean on `master` and
pushed, and the bench is on netboot with the card carrying the same build.

**Overnight cron `6f104037`** (every 20 min) is still armed. Delete it when you are back —
`CronDelete 6f104037` — or leave it; it has no work queued that needs doing before you look at §0.

Two things are **waiting on an event rather than on effort**, so no amount of bench time moves them:

- **C1 — heap corruption.** Sharpened overnight from "heap corruption" to a **four-byte write at
  `heap+4`**: the low 32 bits of `heap->size` survive intact and only the high half is wrong, and the
  two observed corrupting words decode as `-0.0f` and the smallest negative denormal — float bit
  patterns, in four 3D apps. Instrumented (`hlo32`/`hhi32`/`hfixed`) **and the instrument proven**
  against an injected reproduction, so the next fire is decisive rather than the first run of untested
  code. Hunt: **17 trials plus a clean six-app gate, 0 fires** — which at the historical ~1-in-42 rate has
  probability ≈0.68 of happening anyway, so it bounds nothing, and is recorded that way.
- **C2 residual.** `hal_cpuPushSignal` still writes the signal frame unguarded, but nothing reaches
  it; deciding it needs a reproducer that lands there, and `stack-bomb` does not.

## 4. Open bugs

Single source of truth: [`docs/KNOWN-ISSUES.md`](../KNOWN-ISSUES.md), **rewritten 2026-09-22** at
the owner's request into a list of open issues only — 24 of them, grouped as correctness (C1-C7),
graphics (G1-G3), platform limitations (P1-P6), incomplete features (F1-F4) and debt (D1-D10).
Closed rows moved verbatim to [`docs/done/closed-issues-archive.md`](../done/closed-issues-archive.md);
open `TD-NN` items are folded in as issues; previous decisions were stripped so the owner can
re-decide from the list.

Two claims in the old file were **stale and are corrected there**: the WiFi lwip netif *is* wired up
and registers on every boot (only the join is manual), and USB mass storage *works* (it was listed
as not implemented). One item was **resolved without the doc noticing**: TD-19's missing `isb`
landed in kernel `7348dd99` on 2026-09-19.
